File: `services/hermes_server.py`.
  - **The runtime governance seam failed open on nearly every dispatch.** `runtimes/routing.py` builds the acting identity from `context.get("agent_name") or context.get("agent")`, and `context["agent"]` is sometimes a full agent-profile dict; `resolve_identity`/`slugify_agent` then called `.strip()` on it, raising `'dict' object has no attribute 'strip'`, which the seam caught by *allowing* the dispatch — silently skipping policy enforcement. Identity derivation is documented as total, so it now coerces any non-string (a dict yields its best name field) via `_coerce_text` instead of raising. File: `packages/governance/identity.py`.
  - **The `tasks.source_id` unique index could never build.** It was declared `sparse=True`, but a sparse unique index still indexes documents whose field is present-but-null, so the many tasks written with an explicit `source_id: null` all collided (`E11000 dup key {source_id: null}`) and the build failed on every restart. Switched to a `partialFilterExpression` restricting the index to string source_ids, so nulls are excluded and uniqueness is still enforced on real source ids. File: `backend/server.py`.
- **Provider failures now log their cause, so "which provider keys work?" is answerable from the logs** (2026-08-14). The brain-watchdog path logged only "Brain watchdog: <provider> failure #N", which cannot distinguish a dead/expired key (HTTP 401/403 — a human must rotate it) from a transient rate limit (429 — heals on its own). The HTTP status of the final failing attempt is now threaded from `ProviderRouter._try_one_provider` through `_notify_watchdog` into `WatchdogState.record_failure`, which classifies it into a human-readable cause ("auth failed — check/rotate the API key", "rate limited — transient, backs off", etc.). Purely additive and backward-compatible: the status is optional and the log degrades to the historical line when it is unknown. This is the signal the autonomous Render ops loop (`services/render_ops.py`) and an operator both read to tell a key problem apart from a load problem. Files: `packages/ai/router.py`, `packages/ai/watchdog.py`.
- **The lockfile drift recurred on master while this was open, which is the case for the gate** (2026-08-14). Merging master into the branch brought in a batch of dependency bumps that regenerated `frontend/package-lock.json` and dropped the `tailwindcss/node_modules/yaml` entry again — the same `yaml@2.9.0` omission repaired in #1259, back within days. `npm ci` on that merged tree exits 1; the pipeline was green throughout, because the frontend job still ran `npm install` on master. Repaired again with `npm install --package-lock-only` (18 insertions, no version bumps) and re-verified: `npm ci` completes (1479 packages), 174/174 frontend tests, `npm run build` compiled. This is the recurrence the `npm ci` gate in this PR exists to stop, observed rather than predicted.
- **CI now fails on lockfile drift instead of hiding it** (2026-08-10). The frontend job ran `npm install`, which silently repairs a `package-lock.json` that has drifted out of sync with `package.json`. That is why the missing `yaml@2.9.0` entry (#1259) could sit on master with the pipeline green while every clean clone failed on `npm ci`. The job now runs plain `npm ci`, which refuses a desynchronised lock, so the drift fails on the PR that introduces it. **Without `--legacy-peer-deps`**, and that detail is the whole fix: the flag changes the tree npm validates and takes the sync check with it. Measured on a lock with the `yaml` entry deleted — `npm ci` exits 1 with `Missing: yaml@2.9.0 from lock file`, `npm ci --legacy-peer-deps` exits 0 and installs — so keeping the flag would have left the gate unable to catch the one drift it exists for. Plain `npm ci` resolves nothing itself, it installs the locked tree, so the peer conflicts that made `npm install` need the flag do not arise; verified end to end (1479 packages, 174/174 tests, `npm run build` compiled). Only the PR gate changed; the deploy and security-scan workflows still use `npm install`, deliberately, so a lockfile problem blocks a merge rather than a production deploy. File: `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.

- **`npm ci` works again on a clean checkout** (2026-08-10). `frontend/package-lock.json` was missing `yaml@2.9.0`, Tailwind's optional peer dependency, so `npm ci` refused with `EUSAGE — Missing: yaml@2.9.0 from lock file` on any fresh clone. CI did not catch it because the frontend workflow uses `npm install`, which repairs the lock in place rather than failing on it. Regenerated with `npm install --package-lock-only`, which added exactly one nested entry (`node_modules/tailwindcss/node_modules/yaml`) and bumped no existing version. Every command here runs from `frontend/`: verified by running `npm ci` to completion there, then the full frontend suite (174/174) and `npm run build` against that clean install.

- **The boot bundle no longer ships the dashboard nobody opens** (2026-08-10). `App.js` imported the legacy v4 shell (`DashboardLayout`, ~24 screens) eagerly while `V5App` — the shell every authenticated user is redirected to — was lazy. Every visitor therefore downloaded the entire v4 dashboard *before* the v5 chunk could start, which on a phone is the whole cost of the first paint spent on screens that are unreachable from the UI. Making the legacy shell lazy takes the main bundle from **864.9 KB to 375.0 KB** (measured on `npm run build`, both figures uncompressed), with a Suspense boundary added on the `/legacy/*` route since a lazy component without one throws.
- **Platform Controls' save bar was unreachable on a phone** (2026-08-10). The bar is `position:fixed; bottom:0`, and so is the shell's mobile bottom nav — at `z-index:50` against the bar's `20`, the nav rendered over it and Apply could not be tapped at all below the 1024px breakpoint. The bar now sits clear of the nav on mobile and returns to the bottom edge on desktop.
- **Providers and Controls both own brain settings, and neither said so** (2026-08-10). The "Brain & local-runtime controls" section assigns a concrete provider and model per role and flips the local runtime; Controls sets the global policy the resolver applies (`BRAIN_PREFERENCE`, `ALLOW_PAID_BRAIN`, `INCLUDE_LOCAL_FALLBACK`). They write different stores, so changing one and watching the other not move is the natural way to discover the split. The section now states which is which and links to the other. Files: `frontend/src/App.js`, `frontend/src/v5/screens/ControlsScreen.jsx`, `frontend/src/v5/screens/ProvidersScreen.jsx`, `frontend/src/__tests__/appShellRoutes.test.jsx`.

- **Platform Controls moved to the v5 dashboard, where it is actually reachable** (2026-08-09). The screen shipped in #1234 lived in `DashboardLayout`, which `App.js` mounts only under `/legacy/*`, while an authenticated user is redirected to `/v5` — so 109 controls existed, passed their tests, and could not be opened from the UI. Both entry points were broken too: the legacy sidebar item and the Settings card used `<NavLink to="/controls">`, and a leading slash resolves against the site root rather than the `/legacy` parent, so the catch-all route bounced them to `/v5`. Verified by rendering the real routing shape rather than reasoning about it: the link's `href` was `/controls`, `/controls` landed on `/v5`, and only `/legacy/controls` rendered the screen.
  - **The screen is now `frontend/src/v5/screens/ControlsScreen.jsx`**, registered in `V5App` and in the shell's SYSTEM section as an admin-only **Controls** item, so it has both a deep link (`/v5/controls`) and a way in. `/legacy/controls` redirects there so a bookmark still arrives somewhere real, and the superseded `pages/PlatformControlsPage.js` is deleted rather than left as a second copy.
  - **Rewritten with inline styles rather than Tailwind utilities**, matching every other v5 screen. This is not cosmetic: `.v5-root *` in the v5 theme sets `margin:0; padding:0` at the same specificity as a Tailwind spacing utility and is injected after the CSS bundle, so `px-4` inside that shell loses the cascade and would have rendered the whole screen flush. Behaviour is unchanged — the existing ten tests were repointed at the new component and pass untouched, which is what pins the rewrite as equivalent.
  - **The MCP card moved with it, for the same reason.** `McpCard` was mounted on the legacy Providers page, so the Render self-healing chain it reports was equally unreachable. It now renders at the top of v5's Providers → MCP tab, above the registry list from #1240 — the two are complementary: the list answers "is each server reachable", the card answers "is the one server whose job is watching the platform actually healing anything". Rewritten with inline styles for the same cascade reason, with its ten tests repointed and passing unchanged.
  - New `frontend/src/__tests__/v5ControlsRoute.test.jsx` pins reachability itself: `/v5/controls` resolves to the screen, an unknown deep link still falls back to chat, and the nav exposes the entry as admin-only. Files: `frontend/src/v5/screens/ControlsScreen.jsx`, `frontend/src/v5/V5App.jsx`, `frontend/src/v5/AppShell.jsx`, `frontend/src/pages/DashboardLayout.js`, `frontend/src/pages/SettingsPage.js`, `frontend/src/v5/components/McpCard.jsx`.

- **Providers → MCP no longer flags a working GitHub entry as broken** (2026-08-09). Reported from the live screen: `github` rendered red with status `error`, and Playwright's reason gave a variable name rather than a fix.
  - **`error` was wrong for a server the backend structurally cannot dial.** `mcp_registry` mapped every unreachable server to `error`, contradicting its own docstring, which said a stdio server "must not read as a fault". GitHub is reached by the backend through `agent/github_tools.py`, not MCP; its stdio entry exists for coding sessions via `.mcp.json`. Nothing was broken, but the dashboard sent an operator hunting for a break. Specs now carry `dialable`, and an undialable server reports `unavailable` — muted on the screen, since red is reserved for a server we expected to reach and could not. A `dialable` server that fails is still an `error`; a regression test pins that the softer status cannot swallow a genuine fault.
  - **Reasons now name the fix.** Playwright's `unconfigured` row said only "PLAYWRIGHT_MCP_URL is not set", leaving the operator to go and find out what to point it at. It now names the command (`npx @playwright/mcp@latest --port 8931`) and what to set. Files: `packages/integrations/mcp_registry.py`, `frontend/src/v5/screens/ProvidersScreen.jsx`, `tests/test_mcp_registry.py`.

### Added

- **`WorkspaceTools.edit_file()` — precise string replacement (F1 roadmap)** (2026-08-13). Adds a `edit_file(path, old_string, new_string)` method to `WorkspaceTools` that replaces exactly one occurrence of `old_string` with `new_string`, rather than rewriting the entire file as `apply_diff` does. Modelled after Codebuff/Claude Code-style edits: the executor can make targeted changes deep in a large file without loading or retransmitting the unchanged content.
  - **Exact match first, whitespace-normalised fallback second.** The method checks for `old_string` verbatim; if not found it re-checks after stripping trailing spaces from every line (so a snippet with accidental trailing whitespace still matches). If neither finds the string the file is untouched and an error is returned — `{"error": "old_string not found in '...' ..."}` — so the executor knows to re-inspect rather than silently clobbering content.
  - **Returns `{"path", "diff", "error"}`.** `diff` is a unified diff of the change (empty when `error` is set); `error` is empty on success. The executor prompt now documents when `error` is non-empty the file was not changed.
  - **Registered in the capability registry and advertised in the tool prompt.** Agents can call `edit_file` alongside `apply_diff`; the description recommends `edit_file` for targeted edits on large files.
  - 7 new tests: exact match, `old_string` not found (file unchanged), whitespace-normalised fallback, missing file, delete via empty `new_string`, path-traversal rejection, and registry exposure.
  - Files: `agent/tools.py`, `agent/capability_registry.py`, `agent/prompts.py`, `tests/test_agent_tools.py`.

- **Anthropic beta headers: mid-conversation tool changes + server-side rate-limit fallback** (2026-08-13). Two production-opt-in Anthropic betas are now sent with every Anthropic API request, both defaulting to on and suppressible per deployment.
  - **`mid-conversation-tool-changes-2026-07-01`** — lets the platform swap the tool list between turns without triggering a cache miss on the full prompt. Without this beta, changing tools mid-conversation invalidates the cached prefix; with it, only the changed portion is evicted. Relevant for long-running agentic sessions that promote or retire tools as they progress. Controlled by `ANTHROPIC_TOOL_SWAP_BETA=false` to opt out.
  - **`server-side-fallback-2026-07-01`** — when a request hits Anthropic's own capacity limits, their infrastructure routes it to an available replica rather than returning a 529. The platform already has its own failover chain across four free-tier providers, but server-side failback means fewer 529s reach that chain in the first place and fewer retries are wasted. Controlled by `ANTHROPIC_SERVER_FALLBACK_BETA=false` to opt out.
  - Files: `packages/ai/router.py`.

- **Model catalog: DeepSeek V4 Pro (NVIDIA NIM), gpt-oss-120b (Cerebras), Kimi K2 + QwQ 32B (Groq)** (2026-08-13). Four new model candidates added across three providers, all currently on free or low-cost tiers.
  - **NVIDIA NIM** — `deepseek-ai/deepseek-v4-pro`: DeepSeek's latest multimodal MoE, available on free NIM tier. Added to the NVIDIA candidate list in `brain_config.py` and to the cost table at `$0.00/M tokens`.
  - **Cerebras** — `gpt-oss-120b`: Cerebras's open-weight 120B model running on Wafer-Scale Engine (~3000 tokens/s reported). Added to the Cerebras candidate list; cost table entry `$0.85/$1.20 per M input/output tokens` (paid tier — reflects current Cerebras published pricing).
  - **Groq** — `moonshotai/kimi-k2-instruct` (Moonshot AI's 1T-parameter MoE via Groq free tier) and `qwen-qwq-32b` (Qwen's reasoning model). Both added to the Groq candidate list and cost table at `$0.00/M tokens` (free tier).
  - Files: `packages/ai/brain_config.py`, `packages/ai/cost_tracker.py`.

- **One MCP registry, measured status, and Providers → MCP stops showing servers that do not exist** (2026-08-09). The Render integration exposed a pre-existing problem it then made worse: there were three unrelated ideas of "an MCP server" in the repo and none of them knew about the others.
  - **The dashboard was displaying fiction.** Providers → MCP renders `GET /api/mcp/servers`, which read a per-user CRUD table (`mcp_servers`). Nothing in the codebase ever *consumed* that table — grep confirms its only readers are its own four CRUD endpoints — and its `status` / `tools` were values a human typed in, never measurements. Worse, when the table came back empty the frontend substituted `MCP_SERVERS_DEFAULT`: four hardcoded entries (filesystem, github, postgres, brave-search) rendered as `connected` with invented tool counts. None existed in the deployment. Meanwhile the two servers that *were* real — the in-process server at `/mcp-internal` and Render — appeared nowhere.
  - **`packages/integrations/mcp_registry.py` is now the single source of truth.** Every MCP server the platform knows about is declared once as an `MCPServerSpec`; adding one is a single entry and nothing else. `GET /api/mcp/servers` returns platform-managed rows ahead of user rows, and their state is **measured on each request**: `configured` (required settings present, with the reason when absent), `reachable` (an actual `tools/list` round-trip), and the real advertised `tool_count`. Probes run concurrently behind an 8-second timeout so four servers cost one timeout on a bad day rather than four, and a probe that hangs, raises, or fails degrades to a status instead of breaking the page.
  - **Declared servers:** `internal` (in-process — files, search, git), `render` (services, deploys, platform logs, metrics), `playwright` (real-browser navigation and assertions), and `github`. A stdio server reports *why* it is not dialable — "available in coding sessions via `.mcp.json`, not dialable from the backend" — rather than showing as broken, because it is not broken.
  - **Playwright MCP** joins via `PLAYWRIGHT_MCP_URL`, letting an agent verify a deployed UI against the running app instead of inferring it from the diff. Unset by default and reported as `unconfigured` rather than silently absent; Chromium is too heavy for a 512MB free dyno to host beside the backend.
  - **The frontend no longer invents anything.** `MCP_SERVERS_DEFAULT` is deleted, an empty response renders as empty, and managed rows show their failure reason inline — "unconfigured" with no reason merely relocates the question. Managed rows offer Re-check instead of Connect/Remove, because their status is measured server-side and there is no stored record to toggle. The optimistic-status rollback on a failed toggle is no longer skipped for seeded rows, since silently sticking an unverified status is how the screen came to mislead in the first place.
  - **Render self-healing is now verified rather than merely filed.** `services/render_ops.py` previously fed only `ImprovementLoop`, which schedules a fix and forgets it. Findings now also reach `SelfHealingAgent.on_manual_report`, which dedups by signature, verifies the fix worked, regresses when it did not, and escalates when retries are exhausted — the treatment application errors already got through LogMonitor. Crucially `note_recurrence` fires on **every** observation, including ones inside the six-hour cooldown: without it a "fixed" deploy failure that keeps recurring would look healed forever, because the cooldown suppresses re-filing. Both paths are best-effort and cannot break the monitor. Files: `packages/integrations/mcp_registry.py`, `backend/server.py`, `frontend/src/v5/screens/ProvidersScreen.jsx`, `services/render_ops.py`, `packages/config/settings.py`, `render.yaml`, `docs/render-mcp.md`, `tests/test_mcp_registry.py`.

- **MCP section on the Providers screen + honest Render self-healing status** (2026-08-09). An MCP server provides *capability* rather than tokens, so it belongs beside the LLM providers rather than on a screen of its own. `frontend/src/v5/components/McpCard.jsx` adds that section, and it reports the Render connection as a three-stage chain because each stage only matters if the previous one holds: **Connection** (is `RENDER_API_KEY` set and an MCP endpoint configured — reachability is probed separately and shown as its own badge), **Monitoring** (is the ops loop enabled and ticking), **Self-healing** (do findings actually reach the improvement loop, which schedules the repair). Each stage is gated on the one above it: a stopped monitor produces no findings, so self-healing cannot be green while monitoring is red.
  - **The third stage was invisible, and that was the real gap.** `services/render_ops.py` detects platform failures — build errors, OOM kills, restart loops, error-log spikes — that never reach `agent/log_monitor.py`, because that watcher attaches to this process's root logger and the process was not alive to write them. Findings are handed to `ImprovementLoop.register_external_issue`, which schedules the fix through `scheduler.create`. When that loop is down, `_file_issue` logs and drops the finding — and every counter on the monitor stays at a healthy-looking zero. `get_status()` now reports `self_heal_ready` (is a repair actually schedulable) and `findings_dropped` (how many were detected with nothing to fix them), so "watching" and "healing" can no longer be confused for each other.
  - **A dropped finding still does not burn its cooldown.** `tick()` was rewritten from a short-circuit comprehension to an explicit loop so drops can be counted, keeping the existing rule that the six-hour cooldown is claimed only once a finding is genuinely filed — otherwise the window right after a restart, when platform findings matter most, would silence them until evening. Pinned by a regression test that drops a finding, restores the intake, and asserts the same finding is still filable.
  - **Read-only by design.** The card's "Scan now" button calls `GET /api/render/ops/scan`, which reports what Render thinks is wrong right now without filing anything; the background `tick()` remains the only thing that files. The write-permission state (`RENDER_MCP_ALLOW_WRITES`, default deny) is shown as a badge, because the difference between a monitor that can read production and one that can change it should not be buried in an env var.
  - The stage label "Connected" was renamed to "Connection" (and "Watching" to "Monitoring") so it no longer duplicates the status pill's wording — the collision was ambiguous on screen and silently matched the wrong element in tests.
  - Files: `services/render_ops.py`, `frontend/src/v5/components/McpCard.jsx`, `frontend/src/pages/ProvidersPage.js`, `frontend/src/api.js`, `tests/test_render_mcp.py`, `frontend/src/__tests__/mcpCard.test.jsx`. 6 new Python tests and 9 new Jest tests; 146 Python tests pass across the affected suites, full frontend suite 166/166, `npm run build` green.
- **Platform Controls: review hardening** (2026-08-09). Automated review of #1234 surfaced defects worth fixing rather than waving through.
  - **A failed settings read could have erased every override.** `load_overrides()` returns `{}` when storage is unreachable — correct for startup and for rendering the screen, because the platform must still boot on its environment defaults. It was catastrophic on the write path: `set_overrides()` read `{}`, added the one key being changed, and saved that back, wiping every other override the operator had set. Mutations now go through a strict `read_overrides()` that propagates the failure, so a write that cannot first read the current state does not happen at all. The remaining read-modify-write race between two simultaneous admins is documented rather than silently carried: the response returns the resulting state, so a lost write is visible.
  - **The worker's startup read had no timeout.** The web lifespan bounds `apply_from_db()` at 5s; `worker_main.py` awaited it bare, so an unreachable settings store would have held worker startup open indefinitely. Same bound now applies in both processes.
  - **The test fixture leaked settings into every later test.** `clean_overrides` restored `os.environ` but not the `settings` singleton, which every test shares — a test that flipped `GOVERNANCE_ENABLED` left it flipped for the rest of the run. Teardown now re-applies an empty override set so the singleton is rebuilt from the restored environment.
  - **The Settings card was visible to non-admins**, who would follow it to `/controls` and get a 403. Gated on `isAdmin` to match the sidebar entry, and the off-site `ExternalLink` icon swapped for `ChevronRight` since the destination is an internal route.
  - **The save bar was offset for the wrong sidebar.** It used `md:left-64` (256px at the `md` breakpoint); `DashboardLayout`'s sidebar is `lg:w-[280px]`, so the bar was misaligned at every width between the two. Now `lg:left-[280px]`.
  - `aria-label` added to the choice and number editors, matching the toggle's accessible naming; fenced blocks in `docs/platform-controls.md` given a language identifier; Ruff RUF005 and A002 (`help` shadowing the builtin) cleared.
  - **Split for the 800-line limit (AGENTS.md §8).** `control_registry.py` was 1253 lines. Types and builders moved to `packages/config/control_specs.py` (137 lines) and the table to `packages/config/control_catalogue.py`; `control_registry.py` is now 140 lines of lookup, grouping, and coercion and remains the import surface, so no call site changed. The catalogue is still over the limit and is recorded as a justified exception in AGENTS.md alongside the existing `ceo_dispatcher.py` one — it is one flat declarative table with no executable logic, and cutting it at a group boundary would only make "where is control X declared" a two-step question.
  - Deliberately **not** changed, with reasons: the module logger stays `agency-config` (matching `packages/config/settings.py`, the only other module in this package — `qwen-proxy` is the app-level name); `_actor()` keeps recording the admin's email, because an audit trail whose actor cannot be identified is not an audit trail; and the routers keep returning plain dicts, matching all ten existing backend routers rather than introducing response models in one of them.
  - Files: `packages/config/control_overrides.py`, `packages/config/control_registry.py`, `packages/config/control_specs.py`, `packages/config/control_catalogue.py`, `worker_main.py`, `frontend/src/pages/PlatformControlsPage.js`, `frontend/src/pages/SettingsPage.js`, `tests/test_platform_controls.py`, `docs/platform-controls.md`, `AGENTS.md`. 31 Python + 10 Jest tests still pass; frontend suite 157/157 and `npm run build` green.

- **Per-tool session call caps + budget API endpoint** (2026-08-09). Inspired by Claude Code v2.1.212's per-session WebSearch limit (200 calls/session) and subagent spawn cap (200/session), this extends `packages/governance/enforcement.py`'s `SessionBudget` with per-tool tracking and enforcement, matching the same pattern at the governance layer used by all 34 autonomous loops.
  - **`SessionBudget.per_tool_calls`** — a new `dict[str, int]` field tracks how many times each tool has been called in the session. `GovernanceGate.guard()` now increments both the aggregate `tool_calls` counter and `per_tool_calls[tool]` on every governed call.
  - **`SessionBudget.check_tool(tool)`** — a new method that checks only the cap for the named tool (`max_calls_<tool>` key in `limits`). Per-tool caps are deliberately split from the session-wide `check()` so exhausting one tool's allowance (e.g. `web_search`) never blocks unrelated tools (`read_file`, `apply_diff`) on the same session — the same isolation Claude Code provides.
  - **`_guard_inner` per-tool gate** — calls `check_tool(tool)` after the session-wide `check()` but before the policy evaluation, returning a `budget.tool.exhausted` denial with the specific limit key in the reason.
  - **Default caps** in the built-in policy: `max_calls_web_search: 200`, `max_calls_fetch_page: 200`, `max_calls_fetch_url: 200`, `max_calls_fetch_rss: 100`. These are observed-mode defaults so no existing loop is blocked on first deploy.
  - **`GET /api/governance/budget`** — new admin-only endpoint listing all live session budgets including the `per_tool_calls` breakdown. Surfaces which tools an agent has been calling, how many times, and how close each is to its cap — the visibility needed to decide when per-tool enforcement is safe.
  - **`GET /api/governance/budget/{session_id}`** — same for a single session; 404 when not tracked.
  - **`SessionBudget.to_dict()`** now includes `per_tool_calls` so the budget tracker's `all()` output is complete.
  - 13 new tests: per-tool counter population, per-tool cap enforcement, cross-tool isolation (exhausting one cap does not block another), cross-session isolation, missing-key-means-unlimited, and the two new API endpoints (admin-gating, not-found, and live session reads).
  - Files: `packages/governance/enforcement.py`, `packages/governance/policy.py`, `backend/governance_router.py`, `tests/test_governance_enforcement.py` (7 new tests), `tests/test_governance_api.py` (6 new tests).

- **Platform Controls: 109 feature switches moved from the Render environment into the dashboard** (2026-08-09). Choosing the agent runtime, the brain, or whether an autonomy loop runs meant editing `true`/`false` rows in Render's environment editor and redeploying. Those choices are now made from a new admin screen at `/controls`, with dropdowns where the setting is a choice between named options rather than a boolean.
  - **New `packages/config/control_registry.py`** — the declarative catalogue: 109 controls across eight groups (agent runtimes, brain & routing, autonomy loops, agent-loop budgets, governance & safety, platform ops, integrations, observability & proxy). Each entry carries its label, help text, kind (`toggle` / `choice` / `number`), code default, risk tier, required secrets, and whether a change is live or needs a restart. Multi-option settings are enumerated rather than free text: `RUNTIME_DEFAULT` lists all twelve adapters, `BRAIN_PREFERENCE` its five brains, `LLM_ROUTING_STRATEGY` its five strategies, `GOVERNANCE_SANDBOX_BACKEND` its four backends.
  - **The catalogue is checked against the source, not trusted.** `tests/test_platform_controls.py` greps every non-test Python file and fails if any control names an environment variable no code reads — a dead switch in the UI is worse than no switch. Choice defaults must appear in their own option list, and numeric defaults must sit inside their declared bounds.
  - **New `packages/config/control_overrides.py`** — resolution is `DB override → Render environment → code default`. Overrides persist in `app_settings` under one document and are re-applied at the top of the lifespan, deliberately before `start_background_services()` builds the runtime manager and starts the loops, so an override of `RUNTIME_DEFAULT` or `AGENCY_CEO_ENABLED` is in place before anything reads it. Applying an override writes `os.environ` only for catalogued keys; because a secret is not in the catalogue, the endpoint cannot set one.
  - **Two side effects that would have made this destructive, avoided.** Refreshing the `settings` singleton re-runs `Settings.__init__`, which mints a *new* random `jwt_secret` when `SECRET_KEY` is unset — saving an unrelated toggle would have signed every user out, so the previous secret is carried across. And the runtime routing policy is updated only for the keys that changed, because a blanket rewrite would clobber the manager's own `preferred_runtime_id = "e2b"` promotion and turn a governance save into a routing change. Both are pinned by regression tests.
  - **The UI does not overclaim.** Each control shows whether its current value comes from the dashboard, the Render environment, or the code default; controls whose readers capture the value at import time are badged "Restart required" instead of appearing to have taken effect, and a control needing an unset secret says so (`Inactive until E2B_API_KEY is set`). Reset drops the override and hands the key back to Render.
  - **New API**: `GET/PUT /api/admin/platform-controls` and `DELETE /api/admin/platform-controls/{key}`, admin-gated. A batch is validated in full before anything is written, so one bad field rejects the request rather than half-applying it.
  - **Both processes apply the overrides.** `worker_main.py` runs the same background services as the web process, so it loads and applies the same override document at its own startup — otherwise switching the CEO loop off in the dashboard would stop it on the web service while the worker kept running it. "Live" therefore means live in the process that handled the save; the worker picks a change up on its next restart, and `docs/platform-controls.md` says so rather than leaving it to be discovered.
  - Files: `packages/config/control_registry.py`, `packages/config/control_overrides.py`, `backend/platform_controls_router.py`, `backend/server.py`, `worker_main.py`, `frontend/src/pages/PlatformControlsPage.js`, `frontend/src/pages/DashboardLayout.js`, `frontend/src/pages/SettingsPage.js`, `frontend/src/api.js`, `tests/test_platform_controls.py`, `frontend/src/__tests__/platformControlsPage.test.jsx`, `docs/platform-controls.md`, `docs/configuration-reference.md`. 31 Python tests and 10 Jest tests, all passing; full frontend suite 157/157 and `npm run build` green.

- **Review hardening for the Prime Agent runtime and Continual Harness** (2026-08-08). Automated review of #1233 surfaced defects worth fixing rather than waving through; each is now pinned by a regression test.
  - **The child process no longer inherits the worker environment.** `env={**os.environ}` handed a CLI with a model-controlled `bash` tool every secret the worker holds — `GH_PAT`, `MONGO_URL`, `JWT_SECRET`, every provider key. It now receives an allowlist: PATH/locale settings plus the two proxy variables the extension actually needs.
  - **`--no-approve` was not the isolation it looked like.** It withholds trust from workspace-local *approved* resources but still loads `AGENTS.md`/`CLAUDE.md`, prompt templates, themes and skills out of the repository under test — model instructions from whatever repo the task is operating on. Untrusted workspaces now get the full set (`--no-context-files`, `--no-prompt-templates`, `--no-themes`, `--no-skills`), and a CLI too old to accept them makes the adapter refuse rather than quietly degrade.
  - **A crafted `.agency/harness.md` could inject text into the system prompt.** The spec file lives inside the workspace, so on third-party repos it is attacker-controlled. Entries are now injected only when the citation matches a lesson this platform actually recorded, and the check fails closed — an unreadable lesson store drops every entry rather than trusting unverified text.
  - **The `pi` fallback was advertised but unusable.** `health_check()` resolved it while `readiness_check()` re-resolved the hardcoded name `prime-agent` and blocked the run with `missing_binary`. The dependency now reports the binary that resolution actually picked.
  - **`agent_end` with no assistant message reported success** with empty output; it is now a failure. **Timed-out probes leaked processes** (`asyncio.wait_for` cancels the read but never signals the child) — both probe paths kill and reap. **`stream_execute()` had no deadline** and could hang forever on a stalled agent; it now enforces `spec.timeout_sec`.
  - **The `agency` provider now requires its extension** in preflight *and* at the top of `execute()`/`stream_execute()`, because direct callers like `scripts/compare_runtimes.py` never pass through preflight — and running `agency` without it silently falls back to ambient provider keys, the exact router bypass the extension exists to prevent.
  - **Harness spec:** the prompt-budget calculation ignored the newlines `"\n".join()` adds and could exceed `HARNESS_SPEC_MAX_CHARS`; a malformed `hits` value raised and discarded the entire batch; multiline lesson text silently truncated on the next read; concurrent runs could lose each other's promotions (now one lock plus a temp-file-and-`os.replace` write); and refinement failures were swallowed at `debug` instead of logged with context.
  - **Enrichment is keyed by workspace.** `get_enrichment()` cached a single global instance, so the first caller's workspace served every other one — the planner could read a different workspace's spec, including missing the lesson the current run had just promoted. `AgentRunner` now threads `self.tools.root` through injection.
  - **`scripts/compare_runtimes.py`** scored adapter success, not answer quality: a runtime returning confident nonsense earned 100%. Tasks now carry an `expect` substring that the output must contain, operator-supplied task files are validated before anything executes, and a single-runtime invocation is rejected since it is not a comparison.
  - **Config is documented where operators look.** `PROXY_API_KEY`, `AGENCY_PROXY_URL`, `PRIME_AGENT_*` and `HARNESS_SPEC_*` are now in `.env.example` and `docs/configuration-reference.md`, per AGENTS.md §5, instead of only in the runbooks. `render.yaml` also declares `INSTALL_PRIME_AGENT`: Render exposes a Docker service's env vars as same-name build args, so enabling the runtime without it would register an adapter whose binary was never installed — which also resolves the "unverified" note in the previous entry.
  - Deliberately **not** changed, with reasons: the module logger stays `runtime.prime_agent` (matching `runtime.opencode` / `runtime.claude_code`, not the `qwen-proxy` name the review suggested), and `os.environ` reads stay in the adapter and harness modules to match all eleven existing runtime adapters — centralising configuration is a real point, but it is a repo-wide refactor rather than something to smuggle into this diff.
  - Files: `runtimes/adapters/prime_agent.py`, `agent/harness_spec.py`, `agent/harness_enrichment.py`, `agent/loop.py`, `scripts/compare_runtimes.py`, `render.yaml`, `.env.example`, `docs/configuration-reference.md`, `docs/runbooks/{prime-agent,continual-harness}.md`, `tests/test_prime_agent_runtime.py`, `tests/test_harness_spec.py`, `tests/test_compare_runtimes.py`. 34 further tests; 193 pass across all affected suites.

- **Continual Harness: a repeated failure now becomes a standing instruction** (2026-08-08). The second half of the Prime Agent work — the idea was worth more than the binary. `agent/lessons.py` already recorded *why* steps failed, but `recent_lessons_block()` only surfaces the most recent N, so a lesson that stops recurring for a while falls out of the window and gets re-learned the hard way. New `agent/harness_spec.py` persists the ones that recur.
  - **Every entry cites its evidence.** An entry carries the signature of the lesson that produced it and the hit count at the time (`- [lesson:9f2c1a4b8e hits=3] During execute: …`). A proposal with no traceable lesson is discarded rather than written. That rule is the whole difference between learning and prompt drift: without it a self-editing prompt accumulates plausible-sounding advice nobody can trace to a real failure. `LessonStore.recent()` now returns `signature` so a citation is possible at all (additive — existing callers read `phase`/`lesson`/`hits` and are unaffected).
  - **Refinement is deterministic, not model-generated.** A lesson promotes once seen `HARNESS_SPEC_MIN_HITS` times (default 2). No LLM decides the contents, so it cannot hallucinate a rule, costs no tokens, and is fully testable. A failure seen once is an incident; seen twice it is a pattern.
  - **Off by default for writes.** With `HARNESS_SPEC_AUTO_REFINE` unset nothing is written, no spec file exists, `build_block()` returns `""`, and prompts are byte-identical to before. Reading defaults on so a hand-written `.agency/harness.md` works without flipping a flag. Storage is plain Markdown in the workspace: diffable in review, greppable when a prompt misbehaves, and hand-written prose above the generated block survives refinement.
  - Reuses the existing seams rather than adding new ones: refinement hooks in beside `record_step_failures` in `AgentRunner`, and injection goes through `HarnessEnrichment.build_full_enrichment()` → `_inject_enrichment()`. No second prompt-assembly path, no second lesson store. The spec block is deliberately uncached, unlike tools and skills — it can change mid-session, and a stale cache would hide a lesson from the very next run.
  - **Caught by a test, not by review:** the refine hook was first written as `refine(self.workspace_root)`, but `AgentRunner` has no such attribute (it holds `self.tools.root`). Wrapped in the surrounding best-effort `except`, that would have been a silent permanent no-op — the feature would have shipped doing nothing. Also fixed an over-strict entry regex that only matched hex signatures: correct for today's sha1 slices, but it would have silently stopped parsing the module's own entries if the signature scheme ever changed.
  - New `scripts/compare_runtimes.py` runs an identical task set through two runtimes and reports success rate, median duration, and tokens — the evidence gate for promoting a runtime past TIER_2. It records the provider behind every number and exits non-zero when a runtime never ran, so a stub run cannot later be mistaken for a capability benchmark and an unavailable runtime cannot be recorded as a green comparison.
  - **The internal_agent vs prime_agent comparison has not been run.** `internal_agent` needs a real provider key (NVIDIA/Groq/DeepSeek/…) and none is configured in the build environment, so it reported unavailable and nothing was measured. prime_agent scored 3/3 against a local stub, which measures integration mechanics only. The capability question stays open until the harness is run somewhere with real keys.
  - Files: `agent/harness_spec.py` (new), `agent/harness_enrichment.py`, `agent/lessons.py`, `agent/loop.py`, `scripts/compare_runtimes.py` (new), `docs/runbooks/continual-harness.md` (new), `tests/test_harness_spec.py` (new, 24 tests), `tests/test_compare_runtimes.py` (new, 8 tests).

- **Prime Agent is now a first-party runtime — and the exit-code trap that would have silently broken it** (2026-08-08). Adds `runtimes/adapters/prime_agent.py`, driving [Prime Agent](https://github.com/PrimeIntellect-ai/prime-agent) (and the `pi` CLI it wraps) headlessly via `-p --mode json`. Registered behind `RUNTIME_PRIME_AGENT_ENABLED`, default off, so no existing routing behaviour changes.
  - **The CLI exits `0` even when every LLM call failed.** This was found by probing the real binary against a local OpenAI-compatible stub, not by reading its docs — a run with an unreachable provider retries three times, emits `stopReason: "error"` throughout, and still terminates successfully. Copying the `success = returncode == 0` pattern used by the other subprocess adapters would have reported total failure as success on every run. Success is instead derived from the event stream: the **last** `agent_end` (auto-retries emit one per attempt, so taking the first is wrong), `auto_retry_end.success`, and the final assistant `stopReason`. The exit code is recorded in `metadata` and deliberately not consulted.
  - **stdin must be closed or the process never exits.** Even under `-p` the CLI waits on interactive input; the adapter passes `stdin=DEVNULL`. Without it the first real task hangs until the task timeout with no output to explain why.
  - `parse_event_stream()` is a module-level pure function precisely so it can be tested against captured fixtures with no binary, no network, and no event loop. `tests/fixtures/prime_agent_*.ndjson` are real captured CLI streams; the failure fixture pins the exit-code behaviour above as a regression test.
  - **No provider bypass (CLAUDE.md §3).** The CLI has no base-URL environment variable, so `runtimes/extensions/prime_agent/agency-provider.ts` registers an `agency` provider pointed at our own OpenAI-compatible proxy — verified end to end against a stub, which received `Authorization: Bearer` from the CLI's `"$PROXY_API_KEY"` env interpolation (the key never appears in the file). Preflight fails when the extension path is missing rather than letting the CLI fall back to ambient provider keys, which would bypass `packages/ai/router.py` silently.
  - **Workspace code is untrusted by default.** The adapter passes `--no-approve` unless `PRIME_AGENT_TRUST_WORKSPACE=true`; otherwise the CLI would load extensions and skills from inside whatever repo the task is operating on.
  - Flags are feature-detected from `--help` once per process: `--autonomous*` exist on `prime-agent` but not on plain `pi`, and `--cwd` exists on neither reliably, so the workspace is set as the subprocess working directory instead.
  - **Deployment.** `Dockerfile.backend` installs the binary only under `--build-arg INSTALL_PRIME_AGENT=true`, inside a single guarded `RUN` — a conditional `COPY` would have grown the default image regardless, since a layer cannot be undone by a later `rm`. `render.yaml` declares the variables on the **worker** service only: prime-agent executes model-generated shell with the container's permissions and upstream states it is not a security sandbox, so it stays out of the public web service. Two limits are documented rather than papered over in `docs/runbooks/prime-agent.md`: `/v1/chat/completions` is served by `proxy.py`, which no Render service currently runs, and whether Render forwards blueprint `envVars` as Docker build args is unverified.
  - Files: `runtimes/adapters/prime_agent.py` (new), `runtimes/extensions/prime_agent/agency-provider.ts` (new), `runtimes/manager.py`, `Dockerfile.backend`, `render.yaml`, `frontend/src/v5/screens/DoctorScreen.jsx`, `docs/runbooks/prime-agent.md` (new), `docs/runbooks/agent-runtime-setup.md`, `docs/support-matrix.md`, `tests/test_prime_agent_runtime.py` (new, 27 tests), `tests/test_runtimes.py`, `tests/fixtures/prime_agent_{success,provider_failure}.ndjson` (new).

- **Runtime dispatch is now governed — the last routable-around gap in the governance layer** (2026-08-07). `AgentRunner._dispatch_tool` (#1223) and the MCP HTTP surface (#1226) were both governed, but the *choice of executor* was not. That left the earlier controls partly routable around: an agent whose policy group declares `filesystem.write: []` could not write files in-process, but nothing stopped it dispatching the same instruction to a runtime adapter that writes files in its own container. The restriction was real and the workaround was one `TaskSpec` away.
  - New `runtime` policy surface (the 13th), evaluated in `runtimes/routing.py::route_and_execute` — the single seam every adapter execution passes through, the same shape as `_dispatch_tool`. Deliberately distinct from the existing `docker` surface: that one governs sandbox lifecycle, this one governs which executor an agent may hand work to.
  - Guarded **after** runtime selection rather than before, so the audit row records the runtime that was actually chosen — and therefore the one a policy would need to name — instead of only the requested preference.
  - **Three behaviours, stated separately because they differ:** in `observe` mode the verdict is recorded and the dispatch proceeds; in `enforce` mode a denial returns a failed `TaskResult` through `route_and_execute`; and an error inside the governance layer itself fails **open** (logged, dispatch allowed) — a control that can take out task execution is a worse liability than the risk it mitigates.
  - A blocked dispatch returns a failed `TaskResult` rather than raising. Callers of `route_and_execute` already handle an unsuccessful result; a new exception type would propagate somewhere none of them expect. The reason travels in `output` (which callers surface) and structurally in `metadata.governance_blocked` / `metadata.governance_rule_id`, so a caller can branch without string-matching prose.
  - The shipped `research` group now declares `runtime.allow: [internal_agent]`. Without it the group's `filesystem.write: []` was advisory in practice — restricting the executor is what makes the filesystem restriction hold.
  - Identity comes from `spec.context` (`agent_name` / `agent_owner`), which the agency already threads through; a task with no agent attached resolves to the least-privileged default group rather than an unrestricted one. Fails open like every other governance seam — a control that can take out task execution is a worse liability than the risk it mitigates.
  - **Caught by a test, not by review**: the blocked-dispatch path originally passed `error=` to `TaskResult`, which has no such field — a `TypeError` that `compileall` cannot see and that would only have fired on the first real denial. There is now a regression test asserting the structured metadata. Files: `packages/governance/policy.py`, `runtimes/routing.py`, `config/agent_policy.yaml`, `docs/governance/{README,threat-model,final-report}.md`, `tests/test_runtime_governance.py` (new, 13 tests).

- **Governance dashboard, MCP-surface enforcement, and backend-only secret scoping — the three follow-ups from the governance audit** (2026-08-07). Closes the highest-priority gaps left open by #1223.
  - **`GovernanceScreen` (`/v5/governance`, admin-only).** The layer shipped in `observe` mode on the reasoning that an operator should watch what the rules *would* have blocked before enforcing — but the only way to read that was an authenticated `curl`, which made the observe phase unusable for anyone not at a terminal. The screen leads with the number that actually decides the question (`would_block`), then the decisions behind it with the rule that fired, pending approvals with approve/deny buttons, and the sandbox backend that is genuinely in force. A `local` backend is rendered in red with the fix (`Set E2B_ENABLED + E2B_API_KEY, or run a Docker daemon`) rather than as a neutral value — a dashboard that renders "no isolation" calmly is one that lets an operator believe agents are contained when nothing isolates them. One failing endpoint never blanks the screen (`Promise.allSettled`), and the screen polls only while approvals are pending, since an agent is blocked on those and they expire into denials. Files: `frontend/src/v5/screens/GovernanceScreen.jsx`, `frontend/src/api.js`, `frontend/src/v5/V5App.jsx`, `frontend/src/v5/AppShell.jsx`, `frontend/src/__tests__/governanceScreen.test.jsx` (13 tests).
  - **Threat T11 closed: the MCP HTTP surface is now governed.** `mcp_server/` exposed `clone_repo`, `write_file`, `run_command`, `git_push` and `delete_workspace` over HTTP with no policy evaluation, no identity, and no audit row — a strictly *larger* capability than the governed in-process path, reachable by a shorter route. `mcp_server/governance.py` puts every `tools/call` through the same policy engine and audit trail. Identity propagates across the process boundary via `X-Agent-*` headers set by `agent/mcp_client.py`; the runner attaches its identity to the client so MCP-executed work is attributed to the real agent instead of `agent:unknown`. Those headers are a hint, not a credential — anyone holding `MCP_SECRET_TOKEN` could claim any agent id — but that can only move a caller *between groups*, never past the organization baseline, because `evaluate()` returns on a baseline DENY before any group rule is read (pinned by a test that tries exactly that). Approval-gated actions are refused rather than parked on this surface: it is a JSON-RPC request with a client timeout and no UI attached, so holding the socket for the approval TTL would convert a governance decision into a hung request. `GET /health` now reports whether governance is active, so a governed server is distinguishable from an ungoverned one without reading container logs.
  - **The MCP image previously could not have imported the governance layer at all** — `mcp_server/Dockerfile` copied only `mcp_server/` and installed three packages. It now vendors `packages/{config,governance,security}` plus `config/agent_policy.yaml` and PyYAML (without which the engine silently falls back to its embedded default and the operator's policy file is ignored — a policy that does not apply is worse than a missing one, because it looks applied). The import stays optional and guarded so an older image still serves rather than crash-looping, and the degraded state logs one loud warning naming the consequence.
  - **`.env.backend-secrets` scopes backend-only credentials out of the `hermes` sidecar.** `hermes` received the whole of `.env` — including the GitHub PAT and the JWT signing secret — despite never issuing a JWT, authenticating an admin, pushing to GitHub, or talking to Telegram. **This also corrects an overstatement in the shipped threat model**: it implied the exposure was broader than it is, when in fact the services that execute model-authored code (`opencode`, `goose`, `aider`, `jcode`, `task-harness`, `mcp-server`) were *already* scoped to explicit `environment:` lists and never received `.env`. `hermes` deliberately keeps the LLM provider keys because it runs the same agent brain and needs them — moving a credential a service genuinely needs just produces a broken service and an operator who reverts the whole change. The split is **inert until adopted**: the compose entry is `required: false`, so a single-file `.env` keeps working exactly as before and nothing is scoped until the operator moves the values. Requires Compose v2.24+ for the long-form `env_file` syntax.
  - **An audit row could misreport which policy group governed a call.** `AgentIdentity` defaults its `policy_group` to the agent's own id, which usually has no group of that name and resolves to `default` — so the row recorded the *requested* group while its own `rule_id` named the *effective* one. Both the in-process gate and the MCP adapter now record the group that actually governed. Found by a test asserting an unidentified caller lands on the least-privileged group. Files: `mcp_server/governance.py` (new), `mcp_server/server.py`, `mcp_server/Dockerfile`, `agent/mcp_client.py`, `agent/loop.py`, `packages/governance/enforcement.py`, `docker-compose.yml`, `.env.backend-secrets.example` (new), `.gitignore`, `docs/governance/{README,threat-model,migration,final-report}.md`, `tests/test_mcp_governance.py` (new, 17 tests).

- **The stuck-phase diagnosis no longer depends on Render being reachable — and `services/render_ops.py` turns out to have been silently dead on any single-service deployment** (2026-08-05). `packages/integrations/render_mcp.py` talks to the `agency-render-mcp` **sidecar** at `RENDER_MCP_URL` (`render.yaml` sets `http://agency-render-mcp:10000/mcp`), not to `api.render.com` directly. `render.yaml` declares five services, so an operator running only `local-llm-server` has no such host: `is_render_mcp_configured` still passes (the API key is set and the URL string is non-empty), the ops loop starts, and every tick then fails to connect, warns, and swallows it. On such a deployment platform monitoring — deploy failures, OOM kills, memory pressure — is therefore doing nothing. (A connection failure alone does not prove the sidecar is the cause; it is the most likely one, and the runtime message says so rather than asserting it.) That is a plausible large part of why the 2026-08-02/03 crash loops were only ever noticed by a human reading the dashboard.
  - The incident tracker no longer relies on that path for its primary answer. `agent/loop.py::_timed_phase` now reports each phase boundary straight into `agent/operational_incidents.py` (`note_phase_start`/`note_phase_end`), so the phases in flight are known **in-process** — no network, no API key, no sidecar. Reading these markers back out of Render's log API was round-tripping the process's *own* log lines through an external service to learn something it already knew.
  - This also makes the answer exact rather than inferred: instead of counting unbalanced `phase_start`/`phase_end` pairs in a log window, an incident reports the phases actually open and how long each has really been open (`Stuck phase: tool_dispatch — open for 412.0s and still not returned`). Nested phases are ranked by raw start time, newest first — rounding elapsed seconds first would tie two phases opened inside one clock tick and name the outer wrapper instead of the inner call that stalled.
  - Open-phase tracking is bounded (`_MAX_OPEN_PHASES`, oldest dropped) so a missed end marker cannot grow the map, and every helper swallows its own errors — these run inside the agent run loop and must never break it.
  - Render logs remain as *enrichment*, valuable for windows the process could not log because it was killed. When they are unreachable the incident body now says so and points at the likely cause (sidecar not deployed → `render_ops.py` also inert) instead of reporting a bare connection error. Files: `agent/operational_incidents.py`, `agent/loop.py`, `tests/test_operational_incidents.py`.
- **Runtime governance for autonomous agents: identity, policy, approvals, audit trail, and hardened sandboxes — modelled on Docker AI Governance, adapted to a topology that cannot run Docker** (2026-08-06). An audit of the whole repository against every capability in [Docker AI Governance](https://www.docker.com/blog/docker-ai-governance-unlock-agent-autonomy-safely/) found three things: agent actions were completely anonymous (`AgentRunner._dispatch_tool` received a tool name and an argument dict with no notion of *which* agent asked, so no per-agent restriction was even expressible), the container supply chain was entirely unscanned (no Trivy, Scout, Syft, SBOM, cosign or hadolint anywhere across 11 Dockerfiles and 40 workflows — CodeQL and Bandit cover source only), and Docker AI Governance itself **cannot be deployed on this platform's production topology at all**: `render.yaml` deploys with `env: docker`, so the backend *is* a container on a managed host with no Docker socket and no ability to create sibling containers. A Docker-only design would have shipped a security control that works on a laptop and silently does nothing in production. New `packages/governance/` implements the *architecture* rather than the product — one policy model, one chokepoint, structured evidence — with the isolation surface made backend-pluggable so it engages with whatever the deployment can actually provide (`docker` self-hosted/CI, `e2b` micro-VMs in production over HTTPS, `local` when neither exists, reported honestly as `isolation: none`).
  - **Everything ships in `observe` mode and changes no behaviour.** Rules are evaluated and audited; nothing is blocked. Required by CLAUDE.md §0, and the only honest way to enable a policy engine in a system running 34 autonomous loops — you cannot know a rule is correct until you have seen what it would have caught. `GET /api/governance/metrics` exposes the `would_block` count that tells an operator when `mode: enforce` is safe.
  - **`packages/governance/identity.py`** — `AgentIdentity`: a stable `agent_id` (slug, joins policy and audit history) plus an ephemeral per-run `session_id` (what budgets and credential grants key on). Pure dataclass, no I/O, cannot fail. `resolve_identity_for_runner()` derives one from an existing `AgentRunner` and caches it, so no call site had to be re-threaded first.
  - **`packages/governance/policy.py`** — declarative engine over all 12 surfaces this platform exposes (tool, filesystem, network, credential, shell, github, docker, database, mcp, browser, memory, llm_provider). Baseline rules are **structurally** non-overridable: `evaluate()` returns on a baseline DENY *before* reading any group rule, so a group's allow-list is never consulted on that path. A malformed policy file degrades to an embedded default (a real policy with real baseline denies, not allow-all) rather than denying everything — a YAML typo must not take the agency down while looking like a security feature.
  - **`packages/governance/enforcement.py`** — the only module that acts on a decision. Wired in by renaming `_dispatch_tool` to `_dispatch_tool_unguarded` (**byte-identical body**) and adding a thin wrapper: that method has ~170 lines of branches, every one an early `return`, so an inline guard would have to be repeated per branch to be complete and one missed branch is a silent hole. The wrapper makes the behavioural question small enough to verify, and four tests pin its transparency.
  - **Cost governance is counters, not rules** — a policy file cannot stop a loop. `SessionBudget` enforces six per-session ceilings (tool calls, USD, tokens, duration, recursion depth, retries) against the failure modes this repo has already suffered (the schedule-backlog OOM crash loop, 2026-08-02/03). A *missing* limit key means unlimited, never zero — absence must not silently block everything.
  - **`packages/governance/audit.py`** — all 20 required fields (who/what/when/why/where/cost). Secrets are redacted in `__post_init__`, **before** the event is stored anywhere: redacting at render time would leave the secret in the ring buffer, one serialisation bug from a response body, and this repo already leaked a live MongoDB password into a log line (2026-08-01). Covers 7 provider token shapes, connection URIs and secret-marker keys, and **fails closed**. Bounded ring buffer for the API; a one-line-JSON `governance.audit.events` logger is the durable, SIEM-shippable record.
  - **`packages/governance/approvals.py`** — TTL-bounded, and **expiry denies**: an approval gate that opens when ignored is theatre. First decision wins so a retried webhook cannot flip an approval; `resolved_by` is always recorded. `GOVERNANCE_AUTO_APPROVE` is an off-by-default local-dev escape hatch, audited as `resolved_by=auto-approve` when it fires.
  - **`packages/governance/sandbox.py`** — 8 profiles (development, browser, python, node, security, documentation, review, docker), every one `cap_drop=ALL` + `no-new-privileges` + non-root `65532:65532` + cpu/memory/pids limits + TTL, read-only rootfs and `network=none` by default. `--memory-swap` is pinned to `--memory`, without which a memory-capped container silently swaps instead of being limited. Per-profile `env_allowlist` means an undeclared secret name is dropped even if the caller passes it. The `browser` profile adds back exactly `SYS_ADMIN`/`SYS_CHROOT` rather than going `privileged`, and the `security` profile has no egress at all (a scanner that reads the whole repo *and* can reach the internet is an exfiltration path with a job title). The E2B backend delegates entirely to the existing `services/e2b_sandbox.py` — no sandbox mechanics reimplemented, per the no-duplicate-logic rule.
  - **`.github/workflows/supply-chain.yml`** — the highest benefit-to-effort gap found, closed with one file: Trivy image scan → SARIF → code scanning, Trivy fs scan for dependency CVEs, CycloneDX SBOM (90-day retention), hadolint, and `scripts/check_container_posture.py`. The gate fails only on a **fixable** CRITICAL — the narrowest gate that always means "a patched version exists and we are not on it". Trivy over Docker Scout because Scout needs an authenticated Docker Hub account in CI, and a scanner nobody can run is not a control. Deliberately unscheduled so `loop_registry audit --check` stays green.
  - **Three real defects were found by testing rather than assumed away**, and each has a regression test: (1) empty allow-lists were dropped as falsy, so `write: []` — the strictest expressible rule — silently became "unrestricted"; (2) tool allow-lists were bypassed by any tool carrying a subject, so `tool.allow: [read_file]` never refused `write_file` (which applies to nearly every tool, since most carry a path or URL) — both surfaces are now evaluated and the stricter verdict wins; (3) the `/policy/simulate` endpoint judged differently from the enforcer, so it would have lied to exactly the operator using it to decide whether enforcement was safe — `evaluate_call()` is now shared by both.
  - **What was deliberately not implemented, and why**: Docker MCP Gateway (needs a gateway deployed beside a backend that cannot run containers; its value — one authenticated, logged chokepoint — was implemented natively at one the platform already has), Docker Secrets (requires Swarm), SAML/SCIM (needs an enterprise IdP; `AgentIdentity` carries `owner`/`roles` so one can populate them later), image signing (no registry push in this repo and no verifying admission controller — a signature nothing verifies is theatre), and seccomp/AppArmor profiles (plumbed and passed when set, but authoring one that could not be tested against a running container would be fabricated assurance). Container hardening for the *existing* compose stack ships as an opt-in `docker-compose.hardening.yml` overlay rather than a default, because `read_only` and `cap_drop` break containers in ways that only appear at runtime and no Docker daemon was available to verify them.
  - Known limitation, stated rather than buried: governance covers `AgentRunner._dispatch_tool`, which is the path all 34 autonomous loops run through — but the `mcp_server/` HTTP surface and direct `WorkspaceTools` calls are **not** governed. Extending the gate there is the highest-value follow-up. Files: `packages/governance/` (6 modules), `agent/loop.py`, `agent/web_reach.py`, `backend/governance_router.py`, `backend/server.py`, `packages/config/settings.py`, `config/agent_policy.yaml`, `config/sandbox_profiles.yaml`, `docker-compose.hardening.yml`, `scripts/check_container_posture.py`, `.github/workflows/supply-chain.yml`, `docs/governance/` (6 documents), `docs/configuration-reference.md`, `.env.example`, `tests/test_governance_{policy,sandbox,enforcement,api}.py` (132 tests).

- **Skill/Procedural Memory: agents now learn from their own successful step patterns and reuse them when planning similar tasks** (2026-08-07). Adds `agent/procedural_memory.py` — a lightweight in-memory store (optional MongoDB/SQLite persistence) that records `(goal_summary, step_description, action_summary)` triples after each verified `apply_diff`. Before planning, `AgentRunner._generate_plan()` retrieves the top-3 overlapping records and passes them to the planner as "Relevant past successes" examples via `build_planning_prompt()`. Retrieval uses a Jaccard-style keyword-overlap score (no vector DB, no new runtime dependencies); records below `MIN_SCORE=0.15` are suppressed, and the store is capped at `MAX_RECORDS=500` with oldest-first eviction. 28 unit tests cover tokenisation, scoring, deduplication, eviction, use-count tracking, and the module-level singleton. Files: `agent/procedural_memory.py` (new), `agent/loop.py`, `agent/prompts.py`, `tests/test_procedural_memory.py` (new, 28 tests).

- **AnthropicProvider: `response_format` / JSON mode now honoured for Anthropic models** (2026-08-07). The new LLM-router `AnthropicProvider` (`packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`) previously ignored `LLMRequest.response_format` entirely — Anthropic's API rejects that field in the request body, and the provider offered no fallback. The fix adds `_json_instruction()`, a static method that converts `json_object` and `json_schema` format descriptors into a system-prompt instruction (the same pattern the legacy router uses via `packages/ai/structured_output.py`, kept inline here to avoid cross-package coupling). The instruction is appended to `system_parts` in `build_payload()` before the request is sent; `response_format` is never forwarded to the API body. 10 new tests cover both format types, unknown types, absence of the field, and interaction with prompt-caching list-style system blocks. Files: `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py` (10 new tests).

- **Recurring operational failures now diagnose and file themselves, instead of requiring an operator to read the dashboard and paste logs by hand** (2026-08-05). For an entire debugging session production emitted `Execution timed out after 600s` and `blocked after 5 failed dispatch attempts (No runtime available)` every few minutes for hours, and *nothing inside the platform reacted* — the only reason anyone knew was a human copying logs into a chat. Two independent defects had to hold for that, and both are fixed:
  - **The operational branch dropped the record entirely.** `agent/log_monitor.py::_OPERATIONAL_PATTERNS` matches `"timed out"`, `"blocked after"`, `"all runtimes failed"`, `"dispatch attempt"`, `"policy prevents paid escalation"` — i.e. exactly those errors — and `return`ed before doing anything. The filter's *reasoning* is sound and is preserved (an LLM editing source files cannot fix a saturated free tier, and one fix task per timeout is the documented `timeout → fix task → slow run → timeout` amplification loop), so these still create **no** code-fix task. They are now counted rather than discarded.
  - **Signatures could never accumulate.** `_sig()` keys on `message[:120]` verbatim, so `Task task_c7022c0b… timed out` and `Task task_e2d70c78… timed out` hash differently — every occurrence looked like a first occurrence and no repetition was visible even in principle. The new tracker normalises volatile parts (task/job/cycle ids, UUIDs, hex digests, bare numbers) *before* hashing, so repetitions of one failure mode group.
  - **New:** `agent/operational_incidents.py`. Once a normalised signature recurs `OPS_INCIDENT_THRESHOLD` times (default 4) within `OPS_INCIDENT_WINDOW_SECONDS` (default 30m), it gathers the evidence a human would have — Render platform logs for the window via the existing Render MCP client, plus a `phase_start`/`phase_end` balance analysis over `agent/loop.py::_timed_phase`'s markers that **names the agent phase that never returned** — and files **one** issue through `ImprovementLoop.register_external_issue`, the same intake `services/render_ops.py` already uses (one pipeline, one dedup path). Anti-storm properties are hard bounds: recurrence threshold, per-signature cooldown (`OPS_INCIDENT_COOLDOWN_SECONDS`, default 6h), and a global hourly cap (`OPS_INCIDENT_MAX_PER_HOUR`, default 3). Evidence gathering degrades rather than fails — without `RENDER_API_KEY` the incident is still filed, noting the platform view was unreachable. Nothing on the path can raise into the `logging.Handler` it is called from.
  - Nested phases are ranked by *innermost* (last-started) rather than by occurrence count: a stuck `tool_dispatch` leaves its `execute_step` wrapper unfinished too, and counting would tie and name the wrapper — the symptom rather than the cause.
  - **Everything failure-derived is scrubbed before it reaches the issue.** Incident bodies copy failure samples and Render log excerpts verbatim, and this repository has already had a live MongoDB password reach a log line that way (2026-08-01, below). `scrub()` chains both shared helpers — `redact_connection_url` for `user:pass@host` URIs and secret query parameters, `mask_secret` for loose API keys and bearer tokens — and fails *closed*, withholding the value entirely if a helper is unavailable rather than passing it through. Scrubbing happens at incident construction, not at render time, because the normalised signature also feeds the issue **title**.
  - **A failed filing releases its reservation.** Admission (cooldown + hourly-cap slot) is granted synchronously in `_may_file`, but the filing itself runs later and can fail — the practical case being process startup, when the `ImprovementLoop` is not yet running. Without the rollback a single failed attempt would silence the signature for the full 6h cooldown, reproducing the "no signal at all" behaviour this module exists to remove. `incidents_attempted` and `incidents_filed` are now separate counters, so the metrics can no longer report an incident that never reached the intake.
  - **Signature state is pruned.** The process is long-lived and every matching ERROR line adds a signature, so `_occurrences`/`_samples`/`_first_seen`/`_filed` would otherwise only grow. A signature is evicted once its occurrence bucket has aged out of the window *and* its filing cooldown has expired — evicting a still-cooling signature would release its cooldown early and let the same failure re-file.
  - In-flight diagnosis tasks are held in a module-level set: `loop.create_task` is only weakly referenced by the loop, so the collector could otherwise reclaim a diagnosis mid-flight and drop it silently.
  - Tuning bounds live on the `packages/config/settings` surface rather than `os.environ.get()` calls in the module, per CLAUDE.md §3, and are documented in `docs/configuration-reference.md` and `.env.example`.
  - The counters are exposed on the existing `GET /api/metrics/self-heal` under `operational_incidents`, so the state is checkable without reading logs. Files: `agent/operational_incidents.py`, `agent/log_monitor.py`, `backend/server.py`, `packages/config/settings.py`, `docs/configuration-reference.md`, `.env.example`, `tests/test_operational_incidents.py` (29 tests).

- **Phase-level timing logs in the agent plan/execute/verify loop, so a task that hits the 600s execution timeout names its own stuck phase** (2026-08-04). Production task errors showed a growing pattern of `Execution timed out after 600s` (the outer `asyncio.wait_for` in `tasks/service.py`) with nothing in between the start of a run and that final message — no visibility into whether the hang was in the planner LLM call, the executor's tool-selection loop, a specific tool dispatch (e.g. one of the Web Reach tools added in #1187), file generation, or verification. `agent/loop.py` gains a small `_timed_phase(phase, **context)` async context manager, used to bracket the six LLM-call/tool-dispatch chokepoints in `AgentRunner.run()`/`_execute_step()`: `planning`, `execute_step`, `tool_selection`, `tool_dispatch` (tagged with the tool name), `execute_generate`, and `verify`. Each logs a `phase_start` on entry and a `phase_end elapsed_s=...` on exit (including on exception, via `finally`) — a hang now shows a `phase_start` with no matching `phase_end`, naming exactly which call never returned, instead of only the terminal timeout line. No behavioral change; logging only. Files: `agent/loop.py`, `tests/test_agent_runner.py`.

- **`output_config.effort` support for Anthropic adaptive-thinking models** (2026-08-04). Claude Sonnet 5, Opus 5, Fable 5, Opus 4.8, and Opus 4.7 accept an `effort` parameter (`"low"` | `"medium"` | `"high"` | `"xhigh"` | `"max"`) that controls how many tokens the model spends thinking — replaces the deprecated `thinking.type="enabled"` budget approach on those models. Callers can set `LLMRequest.effort` per-request, or configure `ProviderConfig.default_effort` for a provider-wide default. Neither is required — omitting both keeps the API default (`"high"`). Files: `packages/llm/types.py`, `packages/llm/config.py`, `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py` (13 new tests).

### Fixed

- **Six tools were advertised to the executor and then rejected by its own parser — Web Reach among them, so the agency's internet access has never worked from the agent loop** (2026-08-07). `agent/models.py::ToolCall.tool` was a static `Literal` listing 32 tool names. It did not contain `execute_skill`, `recommend_skills`, `fetch_url`, `web_search`, `youtube_transcript`, or `fetch_rss` — all six of which the executor is explicitly told to use: the first two by `harness_enrichment._ALWAYS_TOOLS`, and the Web Reach four by `agent/prompts.py::build_tool_prompt`, which even ships a worked example (`{"tool": "web_search", "args": {"query": "fastapi motor event loop closed error"}}`). Selecting any of them raised a pydantic `literal_error` inside `_coerce_tool_call`. That is not a soft failure: `_execute_step` counts it as a **tool-selection error**, retries, and after four of them returns the step as `failed` with `failure_phase: "tool_selection"`. So a step that tried to look something up on the internet burned its whole retry budget and failed, which is one of the failure shapes in the 2026-08-02/06 incident logs. It also means CLAUDE.md §6's "primary mechanism for closed-loop self-healing and learning" was unreachable in practice since Web Reach shipped.
  - The static list was the trap, not the omission: registering a tool, advertising it in the prompt, and listing it in the model are three edits in three files, and only the third is invisible when skipped. `ToolCall.tool` is now a `str` validated against `CORE_TOOL_NAMES` (the hardcoded dispatch branches) **plus whatever the capability registry currently exposes**, resolved per call because the registry is populated lazily. Validation is narrowed, not removed — an unregistered name is still rejected — and a broken registry degrades to the core set rather than breaking parsing.
  - `harness_enrichment`'s always-available list is hoisted to module scope as `ALWAYS_TOOLS` purely so a test can assert the invariant that binds the two files: everything advertised must parse. Files: `agent/models.py`, `agent/harness_enrichment.py`, `tests/test_agent_tool_governance.py`.

- **`execute_skill`, `recommend_skills`, and `spawn_subagent` bypassed the governance gate entirely** (2026-08-07). `_execute_step` called their implementations directly instead of going through `_run_tool` → `_dispatch_tool`, which is the only place `packages/governance/enforcement.py` runs. Governance could therefore neither deny nor audit them — the two capabilities where that matters most, since one executes arbitrary registered code and the other creates a whole new agent with its own tool access. This was a real hole in the coverage claimed by the governance work merged the day before, not a theoretical one. All three now route through the same seam as every other tool; `spawn_subagent` gained the dispatch branch it needed to be reachable that way. The `spawn_subagent` call site no longer assumes a dict result, because a denial or a tool error arrives as a string where `run()`'s dict was previously guaranteed — the old code would have raised `AttributeError` inside the executor loop on the first denial. Files: `agent/loop.py`, `tests/test_agent_tool_governance.py` (11 tests).

- **A failed sub-agent was reported to the model as a success** (2026-08-07). `_spawn_subagent` returns an error dict with no `summary` key on two reachable paths — an empty instruction, and the `MAX_SUBAGENT_DEPTH` guard. The executor's call site defaulted its scratchpad note to `"subagent completed"`, and the scratchpad is injected into the *next* tool-selection prompt, so the model planned its following step believing a delegation had succeeded when it had failed. Observation and scratchpad note now share one fallback: the error dict itself. Files: `agent/loop.py`, `tests/test_agent_tool_governance.py`.

- **Model-generated JSON no longer splats into the sub-agent spawn** (2026-08-07). The new `spawn_subagent` dispatch branch expanded `**args` — a dict built from the executor model's response — directly into `_spawn_subagent`. Harmless today, because that method declares keyword-only parameters plus `**kwargs`, but any parameter it later gains (a workspace root, a model override, a token) would silently become model-controllable with no edit at the call site, on the highest-privilege tool in the loop. The branch now reads `instruction`, `max_steps`, and `role` explicitly and forwards only the `command`/`task`/`text` aliases the method resolves itself, matching every other branch in the dispatch table. Files: `agent/loop.py`, `tests/test_agent_tool_governance.py`.

- **Procedural-memory failures are logged instead of silently swallowed** (2026-08-07). Two `except Exception: pass` blocks in `agent/loop.py` discarded every error from `record_success` and `retrieve_relevant`, so a persistently broken store was indistinguishable from "no relevant memories". Both now log at `WARNING` with `exc_info=True` — not `DEBUG`, because production log thresholds start at `INFO` and a debug line would be discarded, leaving the store exactly as invisible as before. The best-effort semantics are unchanged; the exception is still suppressed. Files: `agent/loop.py`.

- **Claude Sonnet 5 and Fable 5: prevent HTTP 400 caused by sending temperature/top_p/top_k** (2026-08-04). Anthropic's new adaptive-thinking models (`claude-sonnet-5`, `claude-fable-5`, `claude-mythos-5`) reject non-default sampling parameters outright. The Anthropic provider now detects these models and omits `temperature`, `top_p`, and `top_k` from both the base payload and `request.extra`. Files: `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py`.

- **Claude Opus 5, Sonnet 5, Fable 5, Opus 4.7/4.8: prevent HTTP 400 from legacy extended thinking** (2026-08-04). Sending `thinking: {type: "enabled"}` to models that only support adaptive thinking returns a 400.
