The provider now skips the extended-thinking block for all models in `_NO_EXTENDED_THINKING_MODELS`, while preserving it for older models that still use it (Opus 4.6, Sonnet 4.6, Haiku 4.5, etc.). Files: `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py`.

- **Correction to the fix below: the NVIDIA 404s were never reaching `packages/ai/failover_client.py` at all — production runs `LLM_ROUTER_ENABLED=true` (set outside this repo, in Render's dashboard, not `render.yaml`), so every call goes through `packages/llm/router.py` and the earlier fix was patching dead code for this failure** (2026-08-03). Confirmed the moment the pasted production errors kept recurring, unchanged, after that fix had already deployed — the literal error text `"nvidia: nvidia HTTP 404: "` only matches `packages/llm/providers/base.py::classify_error()`'s `f"{provider_id} HTTP {status}"` formatting (via `packages/llm/compat.py::failover_chat_completion_via_router`'s `f"{attempt.provider}: {attempt.error}"` wrapping), never the legacy path's `f"{provider.id} {resp.status_code}: ..."` (no `"HTTP"` in it). The real cause: `render.yaml` sets `NVIDIA_BASE_URL=https://integrate.api.nvidia.com` (no `/v1`) on the assumption that the client appends it when missing — which the legacy `packages/ai/router.py::_openai_url()` does, but the newer `OpenAICompatible._url()` does not. Every NVIDIA request therefore hit `.../chat/completions` instead of `.../v1/chat/completions` and 404'd identically for *every* model, including the previously-verified-live `meta/llama-3.3-70b-instruct` — a wrong URL, not a missing model, exactly the case `tests/test_llm_router_resilience.py::test_404_without_a_model_hint_is_permanent` already correctly classifies as permanent (that test's reasoning was right; the bug was upstream of it, in URL construction). `_url()` now inserts `/v1` when the base is a bare host with no path of its own, mirroring the legacy helper. Files: `packages/llm/providers/base.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py`.

- **NVIDIA NIM task dispatch stuck retrying the same 3 dead model ids forever: "All 3 brain provider attempt(s) failed: nvidia HTTP 404" repeating every few minutes, blocking agent tasks with "policy prevents paid escalation"** (2026-08-03). `_try_provider()` (`packages/ai/failover_client.py`) already cascades correctly through a provider's candidate models on failure, and `_disable_unless_key_serves_other_models()` already exists to query the account's real `/v1/models` list before disabling anything — but both are gated behind `_looks_unknown_model()`, which only recognised the substrings `"model_not_found"` or `"does not exist"` in the response body. NVIDIA returns a bare `404` with an *empty* body on an unknown model id, so neither substring ever matched, the self-healing path never ran, and every call retried the same three (likely stale) candidate ids — `z-ai/glm-5.2`, `z-ai/glm-5.1`, `meta/llama-4-maverick-17b-128e-instruct` — capped by `_MAX_MODELS_PER_PROVIDER=3`, without ever reaching the later, previously-verified-live `meta/llama-3.3-70b-instruct` (4th in `config/models.yaml`'s candidate list). `_looks_unknown_model()` now also treats a bare `" 404:"` marker as an unknown-model signal — safe because every other 4xx this chain can produce (429/419/413/401/403/402) returns before reaching this fallthrough, so a `404` reaching here is unambiguous. This does not change which model NVIDIA is actually serving today (unverifiable from this sandbox, no outbound network access) — it makes the system self-correct via live discovery instead of looping on a static list that has already drifted at least once before (see `render.yaml`'s comment referencing issue #656, an earlier 404 on this same model). Files: `packages/ai/failover_client.py`, `tests/test_model_discovery.py`.

- **Root cause found for the recurring dashboard-login 503 / OOM crash loop: the only defence against unfired one-shot schedules was a 7-day age-out, far too slow to contain a crash loop that compounds every 1-2 minutes** (2026-08-03). The 2026-08-02 fix below purged the backlog and warned "if the count climbs back... the leak is still active" — it did: confirmed via Render's Events tab (`Instance failed: ... Exited with status 137`, `HTTP health check failed`, recurring every 1-2 minutes) and a fresh `Force-cleanup: total=1137 deleted=0 deduped=0 expired=0` log line, regrown from near-zero within ~5.5 hours of the prior purge (1075 of the 1137 rows `agency=`-prefixed). `force_cleanup()`'s only cleanup path for a never-fired `run-once` job was `SCHEDULE_RUN_ONCE_TTL_DAYS` (default 7 days) — correct for the general case, but every agency-directive job is created with `cron="* * * * *"` (`agent/agency.py::_dispatch_directive`), a trigger that gets a fresh fire opportunity every 60 seconds; APScheduler never retroactively fires a missed cron match, so missing many consecutive minutes is provable death, not a guess. `force_cleanup()` now also expires `cron="* * * * *"` run-once orphans after 10 minutes, checked ahead of (and narrower than) the general 7-day rule — every other cron pattern, e.g. the improvement loop's daily `"0 9 * * *"` low-priority fix jobs, is untouched and still gets the full 7-day grace period. `render.yaml`'s `PURGE_BACKLOG_ON_BOOT` nonce is bumped again (`"purge-2026-08-02b"` → `"purge-2026-08-03"`) to clear the current 1137-row backlog immediately; this time the code fix ships in the same deploy, so nothing should regrow it. Files: `packages/scheduler/scheduler.py`, `render.yaml`, `tests/test_schedule_backlog_drain.py`.
- **Dashboard login started returning 503, same day as the OOM-crash-loop fix below that explicitly warned this could recur** (2026-08-02). `render.yaml`'s `PURGE_BACKLOG_ON_BOOT` nonce was still `"purge-2026-08-02"` — the value already consumed by that fix — and `_maybe_boot_purge()` only fires once per distinct nonce value, so even if the same agency-directive backlog built back up, the existing value would not trigger another purge. Bumped to `"purge-2026-08-02b"` to force one. This re-applies the same escape hatch as before, not a root-cause fix — the actual leak (something creating run-once jobs faster than a healthy instance drains them) still needs its own investigation if 503s continue after this deploys. Files: `render.yaml`.

### Added

- **claude-opus-5 added to the Anthropic and Aerolink provider catalogs; model catalog synced with current ecosystem** (2026-08-03). Daily automation scan identified four drift items between `config/models.yaml` and `packages/ai/brain_config.py`, and one missing model in the catalog: (1) `claude-opus-5` (the most capable Opus-tier Claude model, released as part of the Claude 5 family) was missing from both the `anthropic` and `aerolink` providers — only `claude-opus-4-8` was listed as planner/judge. Added `claude-opus-5` as the first candidate and new planner/judge default for both providers. (2) `brain_config.py::PROVIDER_CANDIDATES["anthropic"]` still referenced three retired 2024 model IDs (`claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022`, `claude-3-5-haiku-20241022`, `claude-opus-4-5`) — replaced with the current Claude 5 family (`claude-opus-5`, `claude-sonnet-5`, `claude-fable-5`, `claude-opus-4-8`, `claude-haiku-4-5-20251001`, `claude-sonnet-4-6`). (3) `brain_config.py::PROVIDER_CANDIDATES["groq"]` included the deprecated `mixtral-8x7b-32768` — removed; the Groq Llama 4 models (`llama-4-maverick-17b-128e-instruct`, `llama-4-scout-17b-16e-instruct`) were found to be deprecated on Groq (deprecated March 2026) and are not added; Groq presets and candidates now use `llama-3.3-70b-versatile` as the primary model. (4) `brain_config.py::PROVIDER_CANDIDATES["nvidia"]` was missing the Llama 4 NIM models (`meta/llama-4-maverick-17b-128e-instruct`, `meta/llama-4-scout-17b-16e-instruct`) — added (NVIDIA NIM continues to support them under the `meta/` prefix). 20 new regression tests in `tests/test_daily_automation_2026_08_03.py` verify model-catalog/brain_config parity and guard against the stale-model-ID pattern recurring. Files: `config/models.yaml`, `packages/ai/brain_config.py`, `tests/test_daily_automation_2026_08_03.py`.

- **Agents can now read the open web — pages, YouTube transcripts, search results, and RSS/Atom feeds — with no new API key, secret, or external CLI dependency** (2026-08-02). Requested capability: give the agency the internet-access ability described in github.com/Panniantong/Agent-Reach. That project orchestrates third-party CLI tools (twitter-cli, bili-cli, gh, mcporter, Node.js) this repo's containers don't ship and that need per-platform credentials with no existing secret slot — so the capability is implemented natively instead: `agent/web_reach.py` (`WebReach`) provides `fetch_page()` (direct fetch → canonical URL → Jina AI Reader → Nitter → Wayback Machine fallback chain), `youtube_transcript()`, `search_web()` (DuckDuckGo HTML, no key), `fetch_rss()` (stdlib XML, no new dependency), and `doctor()` for per-backend health reporting. The page-fetch and YouTube-caption logic is not reimplemented — it's loaded dynamically from the already-tested `.github/scripts/fetch_url.py` / `video_transcript.py` (single source of truth, per the constitution's "never duplicate logic" rule). Login-gated platforms (Twitter/X, Reddit, Facebook, Instagram, LinkedIn, Xiaohongshu) are out of scope, matching the "no half-finished implementations, no new secrets outside config" rules; GitHub is already covered by the existing `github_*` tools.
  - **Wired into every agent, not just a standalone module.** The four capabilities are registered as tools in `agent/capability_registry.py` (`_register_web_reach_tools`), which the existing registry-first dispatch in `agent/loop.py::_dispatch_tool` already picks up automatically. The executor's tool-call prompt (`agent/prompts.py::build_tool_prompt`) now lists `fetch_url`/`youtube_transcript`/`web_search`/`fetch_rss` alongside the filesystem/GitHub tools — registering a tool alone doesn't make the model call it, the prompt has to advertise it too. Because every scheduled fix (self-healing, `agent/improvement_loop.py`) and every CEO-directed task ultimately runs through this same executor loop, this one prompt change is what gives the whole agency — not just one workflow — the ability to look something up (an error message, a library's current API, a changed dependency) before acting, instead of guessing from stale training data.
  - **SSRF-hardened.** These tools take URLs an LLM can construct from content it read (an issue body, a fetched page) — a classic confused-deputy vector, and unlike the CI-only `fetch_url.py` (a throwaway GitHub Actions runner with no internal network to reach), `agent/web_reach.py` runs inside the production backend process alongside the database and admin API. `unsafe_target_reason()` resolves the hostname and rejects private/loopback/link-local/reserved targets before any request, and `WebReach._safe_get()` re-validates every redirect hop rather than trusting `httpx`'s `follow_redirects=True`, closing the standard "public host 302s to an internal address" bypass for both direct-httpx code paths. RSS parsing reuses the same `ET.fromstring(...) # nosec B314` pattern already established in `agent/trend_watcher.py` rather than adding a new XML dependency.
  - Files: `agent/web_reach.py`, `agent/capability_registry.py`, `agent/prompts.py`, `agent/CLAUDE.md`, `tests/test_web_reach.py`.

### Security

- **A live MongoDB connection string, including its plaintext password, was logged on every boot** (2026-08-01). `services/company_graph_store.py` logged the raw `MONGO_URL` in two places — `CompanyGraphStore.__init__` and `MongoDBStore._get_db()` — and a production `mongodb+srv://` URI carries its password inline as `user:password@host`. Anyone with log access (or anyone who copied a log excerpt, as happened while investigating an unrelated 503) got the database credential in plaintext. Both call sites now go through a shared `redact_connection_url()` (`packages/security/redact.py`), which strips the `user:pass@` segment before the line is ever built, leaving the host/db visible for debugging. It also strips secret-bearing query parameters (e.g. `tlsCertificateKeyFilePassword`) — a userinfo-only redaction would have left those exposed. The same pattern in `scripts/register_agent_runtimes.py` (a manually-run script, not part of the boot path, but the same class of bug, and previously a second copy of the regex) now imports the one shared helper instead of duplicating it. Rotate the exposed MongoDB Atlas password — this fix stops the leak going forward, it does not un-expose a credential already printed. Files: `packages/security/redact.py`, `services/company_graph_store.py`, `scripts/register_agent_runtimes.py`, `tests/test_company_graph_store_credential_redaction.py`.

### Fixed

- **A new, faster crash loop appeared after the startup-timing fixes above shipped: `Instance failed: ks2tt — Exited with status 137` (OOM) roughly every 1-2 minutes, instead of the old 5-minute health-check-timeout pattern** (2026-08-02). Production logs showed `Force-cleanup: total=1007 deleted=0 deduped=0 expired=0` — and with the run-once TTL fix (7-day expiry) in place since 2026-07-20, `expired=0` on a 1007-row backlog means none of those rows were older than a week: an active leak, not 2026-07-03 leftovers. The mechanism is a resonant spiral distinct from the one fixed on 2026-08-01: the OOM cycle is now tight enough (~1-2 min) that agency-directive run-once jobs (`cron: "* * * * *"`) get created but the process is killed before they can fire and self-delete, so every restart adds more never-fired rows without removing any — which makes the next boot's scheduler hydration heavier, deepening the loop. `SCHEDULER.purge_all()` (`packages/scheduler/scheduler.py`) already exists as an operator escape hatch for exactly this class of backlog and is wired to the `PURGE_BACKLOG_ON_BOOT` env nonce (`backend/server.py::_maybe_boot_purge`) — it fired once for the 2026-07-03 incident but was never re-triggered for this newer backlog. Bumped the nonce (`purge-2026-07-03` → `purge-2026-08-02`) to trigger a fresh wipe on next boot; platform loops recreate their own schedules on demand, so this is safe. This breaks the spiral but is not itself a fix for whatever is now creating agency-directive jobs faster than a healthy instance would need to — watch whether the count climbs back past a few hundred within a day of this deploying. Files: `render.yaml`.
- **502/503s kept recurring even after the startup crash-loop fix deployed — the CEO agency's 5-minute cycle was freezing the event loop with two blocking git subprocess calls** (2026-08-01). Confirmed live via Render's Events tab, after the earlier startup fix (below) had already gone out: "Instance failed: HTTP health check failed (timed out after 5 seconds)" / "Service recovered" pairs recurring roughly every 5 minutes — matching `Agency`'s default `tick_minutes=5` exactly, and pointing at something happening periodically at runtime rather than only at boot. `Agency._ceo_assess_llm()` runs as a coroutine on the FastAPI main event loop (dispatched from the CEO's background thread via `run_coroutine_threadsafe`), and called `_build_ceo_prompt()` directly — a plain synchronous function that shells out to `git log` and `git diff --stat` via two `subprocess.run()` calls, each with its own 5s timeout and no thread offload. On every CEO cycle this could freeze the entire event loop — including `/api/health` — for up to 10 seconds, independent of and outside the separate startup-time issue. `_build_ceo_prompt()` is now called via `asyncio.to_thread()`, moving the blocking git calls (and the rest of the pure string-building function) off the event loop. Files: `agent/agency.py`, `tests/test_agency.py`.
- **502/503s continued after the scheduler-hydration fix — `start_background_services()` was still a bare, unbounded await, and Hermes's own readiness wait alone could hold the port closed 4x past Render's health-check timeout** (2026-08-01). Confirmed live via Render's Events tab: a repeating `Instance failed: HTTP health check failed (timed out after 5 seconds)` / `Service recovered` cycle, minutes apart, well after the earlier fix had deployed. Render's own health-check timeout is 5 seconds; `_HERMES_STARTUP_TIMEOUT_SEC` was 20 — an in-process uvicorn server with no network dependency that normally binds in milliseconds, but on any slow boot (free-tier CPU contention, a cold import) held uvicorn's listening socket closed for up to four times Render's own budget, on every restart. Two fixes: `_HERMES_STARTUP_TIMEOUT_SEC` drops to 2.0s — Hermes is an optimisation that already falls back cleanly to `internal_agent` on any failure here, so there was no reason it could cost the whole app its liveness. And `start_background_services()` itself — which also runs `RuntimeManager.start()`'s health probes and several other sequential awaits with no bound of their own — is now awaited through the same `_warmup_step()`/shared-deadline mechanism as bootstrap, rather than as a bare `await`; a fast boot behaves exactly as before, a slow one finishes in the background while the port opens on time. Files: `services/background.py`, `backend/server.py`, `tests/test_background_services.py`, `tests/test_startup_warmup.py`.

### Added

- **Anthropic prompt caching and extended thinking in the new LLM routing layer** (2026-08-01). The legacy `packages/ai/router.py` has supported both features since mid-2025, but the new unified LLM router (`packages/llm/`, shipped in #1168) lacked parity — a `claude-sonnet-4-6` request through the new path never sent the `anthropic-beta` header, so Anthropic-side caching never activated and every system prompt was billed as uncached input. Both are now wired into `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py` using the same `ProviderConfig` data model the rest of the routing layer uses.
  - **Prompt caching** wraps the system prompt as a typed content block with `cache_control: {type: ephemeral}` rather than sending it as a plain string — that block is what actually triggers Anthropic's server-side cache; prompt caching itself is stable/GA and needs no beta header. `anthropic-beta: prompt-caching-2024-07-31` is still sent for compatibility with older model generations that predate general availability. Caching is on by default and can be disabled per-deploy via `ANTHROPIC_PROMPT_CACHING=false`.
  - **Extended thinking** adds `anthropic-beta: interleaved-thinking-2025-05-14` and injects `thinking: {type: enabled, budget_tokens: N}` when `ANTHROPIC_THINKING_BUDGET` is a positive integer meeting Anthropic's constraints (see Fixed, below). It also overrides `temperature` to `1`, which the Anthropic API requires for extended thinking — any lower value is rejected. Off by default (`thinking_budget: 0`).
  - Both feature flags are declared in `config/llm/providers.yaml` as `${VAR:-default}` entries on the `anthropic:` block, making them env-overridable without touching Python. `ProviderConfig` gains two new fields (`prompt_caching: bool = True`, `thinking_budget: int = 0`) with safe defaults that leave non-Anthropic providers unaffected.
  - **`claude-sonnet-4-6`** is now declared in `config/llm/models.yaml` with full capabilities (200k context, 64k max output, tools, images, reasoning). The model was already the active default in the deployed Anthropic client but had no declared entry in the config, so the router fell back to cautious auto-discovered defaults. `claude-sonnet-4-5` retains its entry at lower priority; `claude-haiku-4-5` is added as a fast, lower-cost option. Files: `packages/llm/config.py`, `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`, `config/llm/providers.yaml`, `config/llm/models.yaml`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_config.py`.

### Fixed

- **Four defects in the prompt-caching/extended-thinking change above, all caught by review before merge** (2026-08-01).
  - **An unvalidated `thinking_budget` could 400 every request.** Anthropic requires `budget_tokens >= 1024` and, without tool use, `budget_tokens < max_tokens` (interleaved/tool-use thinking is exempt — the budget there spans the whole turn). `AnthropicProvider._thinking_config()` now checks both before enabling the feature and logs + falls back to no-thinking on either violation, rather than sending a payload Anthropic guarantees will reject.
  - **`request.extra` could silently defeat the enforced `temperature=1`.** `payload.update(request.extra)` ran after the thinking block was set, so a caller-supplied `temperature` or `thinking` key in `extra` would overwrite Anthropic's hard requirement. `extra` is now merged before the provider-enforced fields, so the forced values always win.
  - **A pre-configured `anthropic-beta` header was overwritten, not merged.** `auth_headers()` assigned the computed beta list directly over `extra_headers["anthropic-beta"]`, silently dropping any other beta capability an operator had configured. It now parses, merges, and de-duplicates against whatever was already there.
  - **`build_payload()` exceeded this repo's 50-line function limit** (`CLAUDE.md` §9). Split into `_convert_messages()` (plus `_convert_tool_result()` / `_convert_assistant_tool_calls()`), `_build_system()`, and `_thinking_config()`.
  - **`claude-haiku-4-5`'s catalog entry understated its capability and price.** Anthropic's current docs show Haiku 4.5 supports manual extended thinking and prices at $1/$5 per million tokens (introductory, through Aug 31 2026); `models.yaml` had `supports_reasoning: false` and $0.80/$4.00. Corrected. Files: `packages/llm/providers/anthropic.py`, `config/llm/models.yaml`, `tests/test_llm_router_providers.py`.

- **502/503s persisted after the 1178-row backlog fix — scheduler hydration on the startup critical path was never bounded** (2026-08-01). Production logs after that fix deployed still showed the crash-loop signature (`Force-cleanup: total=1146 deleted=0 deduped=0`, `database bootstrap exceeded the 6.0s warm-up budget`, old- and new-process log lines interleaved in the same window). The previous fix repaired *cleanup* — `nuclear_cleanup`'s wrong collection and `force_cleanup`'s inability to expire an unfired one-shot — but a backlog generated *during* an active crash loop is recent, and the only expiry left for an unfired one-shot is `SCHEDULE_RUN_ONCE_TTL_DAYS` (default 7 days), so cleanup could not touch it in time.
  - **`services/background.py::start_background_services()` awaited `scheduler.attach_persistence_async(ScheduleStore())` directly, unbounded.** #1171 shared a 6s deadline across database bootstrap, the reliability hooks, and the app-settings cache warm specifically because uvicorn does not open its listening socket until lifespan startup returns — but this call sits between those steps and was never wrapped in it. Hydration reads every persisted schedule and re-registers each with APScheduler, so its cost scales with the row count. As the backlog grew, this step alone pushed boot time past Render's health-check timeout, Render killed and restarted the container, and `agent/agency.py`'s CEO loop fires a fresh cycle "immediately on startup" — dispatching another round of one-shot `agency:`/`fix:` directives before the previous round's next-minute cron trigger ever got the chance to fire and self-delete. Each restart made hydration slower and the backlog bigger: a resonant crash loop, not a one-time leak.
  - **Fix:** the warm-up-step pattern (`backend/server.py`'s `_warmup_step`/`_defer_to_background`) is extracted into `services/warmup.py` so both the FastAPI lifespan and `services/background.py` share one implementation instead of two. Scheduler hydration now runs through `warmup_step()` with its own 3s budget (`_hydrate_scheduler_bounded()`): a fast hydration behaves exactly as before, and a slow one — regardless of backlog size — is deferred to the background so the port still opens and health checks still pass, breaking the resonance. Files: `services/warmup.py`, `services/background.py`, `backend/server.py`, `tests/test_scheduler_hydration_bounded.py`.

- **Production 503s: a 1178-row schedule backlog that two cleanup passes were running against and structurally unable to drain** (2026-08-01). The origin was in a restart loop on the 512MB free tier, and Cloudflare served "the origin web server returned an invalid or incomplete response" across the dashboard. Production logged the cause on every cron tick, in a line that had been reporting it for a long time: `Force-cleanup: total=1178 deleted=0 deduped=0`. `tests/test_schedule_growth_invariants.py` already documents this failure class from the 2,873-row incident of 2026-07-03 — the fix written then never took effect, for a reason no test covered.
  - **The boot sweep was cleaning the wrong collection.** `nuclear_cleanup` resolved its target as `db.schedules`, then `db["scheduled_jobs"]`, then `db.scheduled_jobs`. Schedules live in `agent_schedules` (`agent/schedule_store.py::_COLLECTION`). On MongoDB the mistake was undetectable from the outside: `getattr(db, "schedules")` returns a lazily-created collection object rather than `None`, so the first branch always won, every `delete_many` ran against an empty collection, and the summary reported a clean sweep on each boot. The collection name is now taken from the store's own constant so the two cannot drift apart again.
  - **The per-tick sweep could not expire an unfired one-shot.** `force_cleanup` removed run-once jobs only once `run_count > 0`. A one-shot is scheduled for a specific minute; if the process is down then, or the row is restored from the store afterwards, the cron trigger has no future match and the job never fires — so its count stays 0 permanently. Its name is unique, so the dedup pass skips it; it carries no `agency` tag, so the stuck-task rule skips it. Immortal by construction, one row per one-shot, forever. Unfired one-shots now age out on the same `SCHEDULE_RUN_ONCE_TTL_DAYS` window the boot sweep already used, borrowed from that module rather than restated, so the two passes cannot disagree about when a schedule is dead.
  - **A backlog that will not drain is now a WARNING naming its source.** The previous failure stayed invisible because a four-digit total sat at INFO beside two zeroes and nothing escalated. Past 200 remaining schedules the sweep now warns and reports the top name prefixes, so the next occurrence names its own cause.
  - **On SQLite the boot sweep stays inert, deliberately.** It raises `refusing to bind to non-whitelisted table` there, and allowlisting `agent_schedules` in `packages/storage/sqlite.py` is the obvious-looking fix that must not be made: that store and `agent/schedule_store.py` both default to `.data/agency.db`, `_init_schema()` creates every allowlisted name as `(id, data)`, and the scheduler's table is `(job_id, doc, updated_at)`. Whichever initialises first wins the `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, so the "fix" can leave schedule persistence writing into a table without its columns — breaking the scheduler outright instead of leaving one sweep quiet. SQLite drains via `force_cleanup`, which goes through the scheduler's own store and needs nothing from that allowlist. A test pins the exclusion.
- **`ollama` was in the production provider chain on a host with no Ollama** (2026-08-01). Shipping `config/` (previous release) made `providers.yaml` real in production for the first time, which surfaced a latent defect in it: `ollama` was the only local provider with no `enabled` gate, and because it needs no API key nothing else could exclude it. It carried `priority: 10`, the lowest number in the file, so it sorted ahead of NVIDIA, Groq, and Cerebras on every request; and `timeout_sec: 300`, written for a local daemon loading a cold 30B model, which against an unreachable host is a five-minute hang per attempt — `OLLAMA_BASE` is `sync: false` in `render.yaml` and points at a rotating ngrok tunnel. Production confirmed the effect: `llm.router: 8 provider(s) ready: ... nvidia, ollama, openrouter`. Now `enabled: ${OLLAMA_ENABLED:-false}` like its four siblings, with the timeout capped at 60s and overridable. A test asserts no keyless provider in that file is ever left ungated again. Files: `packages/scheduler/cleanup.py`, `packages/scheduler/scheduler.py`, `config/llm/providers.yaml`, `tests/test_schedule_backlog_drain.py`, `tests/test_dockerfile_ships_config_dir.py`.

### Fixed

- **Render deploys failing intermittently, and the router config never reaching production at all** (2026-07-31). Two independent silent failures, both found while chasing a failing deploy.
  - **The bounded startup warm-up could kill the process it was meant to keep alive.** Wiring the feature stores lived inside `ensure_bootstrap()`, so when a cold database pushed bootstrap past its 6s budget and the warm-up deferred it to the background, `set_task_store()` had not run. The very next lifespan line — `start_background_services(task_store=get_task_store(), ...)` — then constructed a `TaskStore` with no database, which raises outside tests by design ("cannot start without a persistent backend"). The lifespan raised, uvicorn exited with `STARTUP_FAILURE`, and the deploy failed. Intermittently, because it needed the database to be *slow* rather than broken — which is precisely a cold Atlas waking up, the case the warm-up budget exists for. Store wiring is now a separate step performed on demand: the lifespan asks for the task store and wires one only if asking fails. An earlier attempt wired eagerly at the top of the lifespan and traded one bug for another — it replaced stores callers had already registered, including the ones tests inject before starting a client, so their data silently vanished into a fresh empty store (caught by `tests/test_ceo_direct_dedup.py` in CI). Reproduced and pinned by `tests/test_startup_warmup.py`.
  - **`Dockerfile.backend` never copied `config/`.** The six `config/llm/*.yaml` files — providers, models, routing, keys, health, cache — were absent from every image ever deployed. Because the router treats each file as optional and falls back to env-derived defaults, nothing logged an error: it simply ran on different numbers than the repository stated. Concretely, `groq.tokens_per_minute` resolved to `0` ("unmetered"), so `_usable_window` returned the advertised 131,072-token context window and requests were still sized past Groq's real 12,000 TPM ceiling — the bug fixed in #1172 was still live in production, because only the code half of that change reached the image. The same Dockerfile builds the web service and the worker, so one `COPY` fixes both.
  - **A change to `config/` could not trigger a deploy.** `.github/workflows/deploy-backend.yml` fires the Render hook on a `paths:` filter that did not list `config/`, so retuning a provider's `tokens_per_minute` would have shipped in the image but never deployed on its own — the same silent class of failure, one layer up. Caught by the existing `tests/test_deploy_trigger_covers_image.py` guard as soon as the `COPY` was added, and covered going forward by `tests/test_dockerfile_ships_config_dir.py`. Files: `backend/server.py`, `Dockerfile.backend`, `.github/workflows/deploy-backend.yml`, `tests/test_startup_warmup.py`, `tests/test_dockerfile_ships_config_dir.py`.

- **Agent tasks blocked with "All 6 brain provider attempt(s) failed" — a per-minute token budget was treated as a sort key rather than a ceiling** (2026-07-31). Reported from a live task: `nvidia HTTP 404; groq HTTP 400: model unavailable; groq HTTP 413: Request too large ... on tokens per minute (TPM): Limit 12000, Requested 17599`. The agent path was already on the router — `agent/loop.py::_chat_text` dispatches through `failover_chat_completion`, and the "model unavailable" wording in that error is produced only by `packages/llm/providers/base.py` — so this is what the routing layer was doing, not what it was bypassing. Three defects, each of which alone was enough to block the task.
  - **A declared per-minute token budget is now an input ceiling, not just an ordering hint.** `tokens_per_minute` and `max_tokens_per_minute` existed in the config and only the `token_budget_optimized` strategy read them. Groq serves `llama-3.3-70b-versatile` behind a 131k context window and a 12,000 TPM account budget, so a 17.6k-token planning prompt looked like it fit, was sent, and was refused — and because nothing shrank it, the next free provider refused it too. The usable window is now `min(context_window, tokens_per_minute)`, applied both when ranking candidates and when fitting context, so an oversized request is routed around a metered provider when a roomier one exists and compressed to the ceiling when it does not. `config/llm/providers.yaml` declares Groq's 12,000 as `${GROQ_TOKENS_PER_MINUTE:-12000}` — the value is the account's, not the model's, so it is env-overridable after a tier upgrade.
  - **A `TransientError` whose status was outside `retry_statuses` was laundered into a `PermanentError`, discarding its `scope` and `status`.** The routing loop reads `scope` to decide what happens next, so a key-scoped failure stopped rotating to the sibling key and a model-scoped one stopped being attributed to the model. This hit every transient status not on the retry list — including 410, the decommissioned-model case this router was built for (`CLAUDE.md` §7), and Groq's 413. Typed errors now reach the loop intact; only genuinely unknown exceptions are re-wrapped.
  - **Groq reports its tokens-per-minute ceiling as an HTTP 413**, which read as a permanent payload refusal when it is a rate limit that clears within the minute. A 413 whose body names a per-minute budget is now classified key-scoped and transient, exactly like a 429; a 413 without that signal stays permanent. `_penalise` keys the rate-limit path on the scope rather than the literal status 429, so the cooldown-and-rotate treatment follows the classification instead of the status code. Files: `packages/llm/router.py`, `packages/llm/providers/base.py`, `config/llm/providers.yaml`, `tests/test_llm_router_tpm.py`.

- **Login failing with a browser "Network Error", and the Render health check timing out — one cause, plus two behaviour regressions found by running the suite with `LLM_ROUTER_ENABLED=true`** (2026-07-31).
  - **Startup warm-up is now bounded, which is what made the app unreachable.** Uvicorn does not open its listening socket until the lifespan startup returns — `Server.startup()` awaits `lifespan.startup()` and only then calls `loop.create_server()`. Every awaited warm-up step therefore ran with the port closed, so a slow warm-up did not produce a slow app but an invisible one: Render's health check timed out at 5s, marked the deploy unhealthy, restarted it, and the restart ran the same warm-up again. Requests landing in that gap had nothing listening to answer them, which is exactly what `POST /api/auth/login` reported as a browser-level "Network Error" rather than a 401. Bootstrap, the reliability hooks, and the app-settings cache warm now share one 6s deadline; whatever overruns finishes in the background while the app starts serving. Steps that fit the budget behave exactly as before.
  - **The fifteen boot indexes are created concurrently** instead of one awaited round-trip at a time. They are independent of each other, so on MongoDB Atlas the old form spent fifteen serial network round-trips with the port still closed.
  - **`/api/auth/login` no longer awaits a cold bootstrap unbounded.** Seeding indexes and default rows is not a precondition for checking a password, but the handler awaited it with no ceiling, so the first login after a restart paid the entire cold-start cost inside the request. Bootstrap is now bounded at 3s and the credential lookup keeps its own error handling — a bootstrap failure no longer forces the env-admin fallback when the database itself is reachable. A local `import asyncio` inside `lifespan()` was also shadowing the module-level import for the whole function.
  - **The router ignored the durable provider on/off switch.** `services/brain_failover.py` owns one persisted set of disabled providers — it survives a Render deploy and is what the Providers screen renders — and `LLMRouter` neither read it nor wrote to it. With `LLM_ROUTER_ENABLED=true` that meant a provider an operator switched off was still routed to (billing a paid provider that was deliberately disabled), and a provider whose key had been revoked or whose account was out of credit was retried on every request forever, because the in-memory circuit breaker reopens on a timer. `packages/llm/disabled.py` bridges to that one store in both directions, reusing the legacy billing-signal list rather than copying it.
  - **A 413 aborted the whole chain instead of failing over.** The router grouped 413 with 414/422 as "the request is malformed, every provider will reject it identically". That is true of 414 and 422 and false of 413: "payload too large" describes one provider's context window, so a prompt that overflows a 32k model fits a 200k one. `tests/test_brain_failover_413.py` has pinned the fail-over behaviour on the legacy path since before the router existed; enabling the flag silently turned that recovery into a hard failure.
  - **Legacy-path tests now pin `LLM_ROUTER_ENABLED` off.** Four files script the legacy failover chain's own manager and httpx transport, which `LLMRouter` uses neither of — with the flag set in the ambient environment they routed past every stub and asserted nothing. Likewise `tests/test_unit8_model_catalog.py` pins `FREELLM_CATALOG_INCLUDE_ROUTER` to its baseline and gains a test for the widened shape. Running the full suite under production's flags now reproduces the flags-off baseline exactly, where it previously produced 37 extra failures. Files: `backend/server.py`, `packages/llm/disabled.py`, `packages/llm/router.py`, `docs/llm-router/`, `tests/test_startup_warmup.py`, `tests/test_llm_router_disabled.py`.

### Added

- **Review fixes for the Render MCP integration — one real dedup bug, plus transport and supply-chain hardening** (2026-07-31). Follow-up to the two Render MCP entries in this same release, from the PR review. Three defects, each with a regression test verified to fail against the previous code.
  - **The 6-hour cooldown was a no-op for `error_logs` and `memory_pressure`.** `RenderFinding.signature` fell back to an hour-truncated `bucket` when no `deploy_id` was present, so the dedup key changed every hour and could never be blocked by a six-hour cooldown: a sustained error spike or continuous memory pressure filed four times more `ImprovementLoop` issues than the module documented, and `_cooldowns` grew one entry per hour per ongoing condition with no eviction. Nothing time-derived belongs in a dedup key; only `deploy_id` discriminates now, because a *different* failed deploy genuinely is a different problem. `bucket` stays in `evidence` for display. The original test only covered the deploy case, which carries a stable `deploy_id` — that is why it never caught this.
  - **A Streamable-HTTP response split across several `data:` lines was unparseable.** SSE permits one event to carry multiple `data:` lines, joined with `\n` before decoding; the parser decoded each line on its own, so every `json.loads` failed, `_parse_body` raised, and the circuit breaker opened. Render's log dumps and metric payloads are precisely the large responses a server splits. Lines are now buffered to the event boundary, with CRLF endings and a final event lacking its trailing blank line both covered.
  - **`_ensure_handshake` was not concurrency-safe.** The admin routes and the ops loop share one client, so two first calls could both run `initialize`; a session-managing server issues two sessions and `_capture_session` keeps only the last, leaking the other. Now double-checked under an `asyncio.Lock`, keeping the post-handshake fast path lock-free and a failed handshake retryable.
  - **The sidecar image is pinned by digest, not `:latest`.** That container proxies the Render API key to every service in the workspace, so a silent upstream change is a supply-chain change to the most privileged thing deployed — it must arrive as a reviewed diff rather than a rebuild. `.mcp.json` pins the same digest so coding sessions and production run the same build; the bump procedure is documented in `Dockerfile.render-mcp`.
  - **`/api/render/*` no longer echoes exception text to clients.** Transport errors quote upstream URLs and can carry request context, so the client gets a fixed 503 detail and the cause goes to `log.exception` — reachable by operators through `/api/render/ops/status`, behind the same admin gate. A rejected MCP notification is also logged at WARNING with its status instead of at DEBUG, since it leaves the session degraded while the caller proceeds.
  - **Documentation and fixtures corrected to match implementation.** The sidecar is the fifth Render service, not the fourth; the loop registry said 15 minutes / 96 runs after the interval moved to 600s / 144; the first changelog entry's "off by default" is superseded; the seven `RENDER_*` variables were documented but missing from `.env.example`; two fenced blocks in the guide lacked a language. Files: `services/render_ops.py`, `agent/mcp_client.py`, `packages/integrations/render_mcp.py`, `backend/render_router.py`, `Dockerfile.render-mcp`, `.mcp.json`, `.env.example`, `loops/registry.yaml`, `docs/render-mcp.md`, `docs/configuration-reference.md`, `tests/test_render_mcp.py`.

- **The Render MCP server is now deployed, wired privately, and monitoring is on by default** (2026-07-31). Follow-up to the Render MCP integration in this same release, which shipped the client but left the endpoint and the flag as operator homework. Autonomy that needs a human to remember a checklist after an incident is not autonomy.
  - **`agency-render-mcp` is a fifth Render service.** Built from `Dockerfile.render-mcp`, a thin wrapper over `ghcr.io/render-oss/render-mcp-server`. The wrapper exists because upstream deliberately uses `ENTRYPOINT` (not `CMD`) so user args append to the binary — whether a platform's docker-command override appends or replaces is platform-specific, and getting it wrong yields a container that either ignores `-t http` and serves stdio to nobody, or fails to start. Both are pinned here instead. Running it in-process was never an option: it is a Go binary, and reimplementing Render's API surface in Python is the duplicate-logic path CLAUDE.md §2 rules out.
  - **The backend reaches it over Render's private network** at `http://agency-render-mcp:10000/mcp`, not its public URL. `PORT` is declared as `10000` because the binary hardcodes `:10000` and ignores `$PORT` — the variable tells Render where to route, not the server what to bind. No `healthCheckPath`: the server's mux serves `/mcp` and the OAuth metadata routes only, and pointing a health check at `/mcp` would open an SSE stream that never closes, so Render's port check is what confirms liveness. `OAUTH_ENABLED=false` is explicit because `pkg/oauth` fails the process at boot on a partial OAuth config.
  - **The sidecar holds no secrets at all.** In HTTP mode the Render API token is read per-request from the caller's `Authorization` header (upstream `pkg/authn/authn.go`), never from its own environment — verified against source, as was the fact that `config.DefaultAPIConfig()` cannot error, so the process boots with no key present. The only credential involved is the backend's, sent per call. Nothing in the service's `envVars` is `sync: false`, and a test asserts it stays that way.
  - **`RENDER_OPS_ENABLED` now defaults to `true`** in both `packages/config/settings.py` and `render.yaml`. The `RENDER_API_KEY` requirement in `is_render_ops_enabled` stays, and is what makes that default safe rather than being a second off-switch: the key cannot be committed, so without the credential check an install that never configured Render would fail a tick every ten minutes forever. Instead it stays dormant — one log line at startup, no retries.
  - **The poll interval drops to 600s.** A free-plan service sleeps after roughly 15 minutes idle, so the previous 900s poll would have landed on a cold start almost every tick. Ten minutes keeps the sidecar warm at 144 ticks a day.
  - **Deployment topology is pinned by tests** rather than left to drift: the sidecar's existence, its port, its lack of a health check, its lack of secrets, the `ENTRYPOINT`/`CMD` pair, the backend's use of the private address over the public one, ops-on, writes-off, and the sub-900s interval. Files: `Dockerfile.render-mcp`, `render.yaml`, `packages/config/settings.py`, `docs/render-mcp.md`, `docs/configuration-reference.md`, `tests/test_render_mcp.py`.

- **Render MCP integration — the agency can now see the platform it runs on** (2026-07-31). `agent/log_monitor.py`, the front of the self-heal chain, works by attaching a `logging.Handler` to the root logger, so it only ever observes failures that a *running* Python process wrote to a log. Everything the platform does around the process was invisible to it: a build that failed so no container ever started, an OOM kill, a restart loop, memory pinned at the plan ceiling, a deploy stuck in `build_in_progress`, edge 5xx served before any handler ran. Those are the failures that take production down hardest, and the self-heal loop was blind to all of them. The [Render MCP server](https://github.com/render-oss/render-mcp-server) exposes exactly that view; this wires the agency to it.
  - **Coding sessions get Render over stdio.** `.mcp.json` registers `ghcr.io/render-oss/render-mcp-server` for any MCP-aware harness working in this repo, so an agent debugging production reads deploy history and platform logs itself instead of asking a human to paste them.
  - **The running agency gets Render over Streamable HTTP.** `packages/integrations/render_mcp.py` is a typed facade over the upstream tools (`list_services`, `list_deploys`, `list_logs`, `get_metrics`, …) with payload normalisation that tolerates the several shapes upstream returns — a bare array, a named-key envelope, a JSON text block — and degrades to "no reading" instead of raising when a shape changes. The backend is a long-lived async service, not a per-session subprocess host, so it deliberately does not use stdio.
  - **`agent/mcp_client.py` was extended, not duplicated.** One client now serves both the in-process server at `/mcp-internal` and third-party Streamable-HTTP servers: requests advertise `Accept: application/json, text/event-stream`, responses decode from either media type (SSE notification frames skipped, the `result`/`error` frame taken), a server-issued `Mcp-Session-Id` is captured and echoed, and `rpc_path` lets a caller pass a URL that already names the endpoint. The default `"/mcp"` preserves existing behaviour exactly; the 76 existing MCP tests pass unchanged.
  - **A new autonomy daemon turns platform failures into self-heal work.** `services/render_ops.py` (registry id `render-ops-monitor`, registered in `services/background.py` beside `LogMonitor` and `SelfHealingAgent`) polls each watched service and files `deploy_failed`, `deploy_stalled`, `error_logs`, and `memory_pressure` findings through `ImprovementLoop.register_external_issue` — the same intake the log monitor already uses, not a second issue pipeline. Dedup is `sha256(kind + service_id + discriminator)` on a 6-hour cooldown, so one failing commit is one issue. The loop never raises: an unreachable Render is recorded in `last_error`, because a monitor that dies on an unreachable dependency stops monitoring exactly when it matters most.
  - **Reads and writes are kept apart in two layers.** `RenderMCPClient` refuses `trigger_deploy`, `update_environment_variables`, and every `create_*` unless `RENDER_MCP_ALLOW_WRITES` is on — via an explicit deny-list of tool names, so a new upstream *read* tool is never accidentally blocked. Independently, the ops loop is read-only regardless of that flag: nothing that runs on a timer should be able to restart production because a flag was left at its default. `backend/render_router.py` exposes no write route at all, and a test pins its method set to exactly `{"GET"}`.
  - **`GET /api/render/*`** serves health, services, deploys, logs, metrics, loop status, and an on-demand `/ops/scan` that reports without filing. Admin-only — Render deploy history and platform logs describe the whole deployment, including other tenants' service names in a shared workspace, so they are not per-user data. Transport failures return 503 rather than 500 so the dashboard can tell "Render is unreachable" from "this endpoint is broken".
  - **Enabled by default once `RENDER_API_KEY` is set.** `RENDER_OPS_ENABLED` defaults to `true`, and is only honoured when the API key is also present — a credentialless install stays dormant rather than failing a tick every ten minutes. (This bullet originally shipped the flag off; the follow-up entry above turns it on and is the current behaviour.) Files: `.mcp.json`, `packages/integrations/render_mcp.py`, `services/render_ops.py`, `backend/render_router.py`, `agent/mcp_client.py`, `packages/config/settings.py`, `services/background.py`, `backend/server.py`, `loops/registry.yaml`, `render.yaml`, `docs/render-mcp.md`, `tests/test_render_mcp.py`.

- **Review hardening for the LLM router, plus a Render health-check timeout and three memory bounds** (2026-07-31). Follow-up to the routing layer in this same release. The PR review surfaced defects that the original tests did not cover; each is fixed with a regression test that fails against the previous code.
  - **Every `/api/llm/*` route now requires authentication.** `POST /v1/chat/completions` had no guard at all, so with gateway mode enabled any caller reaching the app could spend provider quota and paid budget. `/config` (provider ids and key *variable names*), `/usage` (spend), and the other read routes were public too. All are guarded; Prometheus scrapes `/metrics` with a bearer token.
  - **Key- and model-scoped failures no longer feed the breaker's rate rule.** `record_failure` appended every outcome to the deque `_should_trip` averaged, so a key exhausting its quota with 429s raised the windowed failure rate until the next 5xx opened the breaker for the whole provider — the exact escalation ADR-008 §4 exists to prevent, defeated by the very mechanism meant to enforce it.
  - **httpx transport errors were classified permanent.** `httpx.ConnectError` is not an `OSError` and `httpx.TimeoutException` is not a `TimeoutError`; the hierarchy is `TransportError → RequestError → HTTPError → Exception`. The stdlib-only predicate matched none of them, so every connect timeout and connection reset stopped failover instead of driving it.
  - **Numbered key pools were never detected for providers declared in YAML.** `_env_key_names` ran only on the env-derived path, which skips any provider already in `providers.yaml` — and every shipped provider is. Setting `CEREBRAS_API_KEY_4` added nothing to the rotation and warned about nothing, quietly undermining the headline 429 defence.
  - **A late success cleared an active key cooldown**, handing a rate-limited key straight back to the next request; **`force_open` was cancelled** by an in-flight completion; **half-open probe permits leaked** when a candidate was skipped, so a provider could never be probed again.
  - **The cache key omitted routing constraints.** A request with `allow_paid=False` could be served a paid provider's cached response, and one with `exclude_providers` the excluded provider's — a silent policy breach, since the stored body names the provider the caller rejected. `allow_paid`, `providers`, and `exclude_providers` are now part of both the cache key and the dedupe fingerprint.
  - **The queue documented priority ordering it did not implement.** A FIFO semaphore accepted the `Priority` argument and ignored it, so `CRITICAL` work queued behind every `NORMAL` request that arrived first. Admission is now a real `(priority, arrival)` heap, with tests asserting both that CRITICAL overtakes queued BULK work and that ties stay FIFO.
  - **Multi-turn tool conversations were broken on Anthropic and Gemini** — assistant `tool_calls` and `role: "tool"` results were flattened to plain text, so the second turn of any tool conversation lost the blocks those APIs require. Gemini also dropped image parts while its models advertised `supports_images: true`. `tool_choice: "none"` was mapped to `"auto"` on Anthropic, enabling the tools the caller forbade. Streamed parallel tool calls were truncated to the first in three places; `StreamChunk.tool_calls` is now a list.
  - **`{value:g}` truncated counters to six significant digits**, so `llm_tokens_total` of 1234567 was exposed as 1234570 and every `rate()` over it was wrong; empty labels were dropped, splitting one metric family into two label sets.
  - Also: `automatic_failover`'s local-tier demotion was an integer no-op; unmetered providers sorted last under `token_budget_optimized`; `keep_recent` was unreachable in the sliding window; `chunk_document` could emit oversized chunks; `${VAR:-default}` ignored the default for an empty value; embeddings were paired positionally and cached under the requested rather than the serving model; Redis degraded to local-only permanently after one failed ping; `QueueFull`/`BudgetExceeded`/`PermanentError` escaped the compatibility shim to callers that only catch `BrainFailoverExhausted`.
  - **`/api/health` now bounds its storage ping at 2s.** Render fails its health check at 5s, but motor's server-selection and socket timeouts are 20s, so a slow or cold MongoDB made the endpoint hang past the deadline and a serving deploy was marked unhealthy. A ping that has not answered in two seconds is degraded, and reporting that quickly is more useful than reporting nothing slowly.
  - **Three unbounded structures bounded**, which matter precisely because this platform is meant to run for weeks: metric label cardinality (the gateway accepts a caller-supplied `x-agent` header — one series per request, forever), budget daily/monthly buckets and free-form agent/model dimensions, and `_refresh_gauges()` running a full `budget.snapshot()` deep copy on every completion rather than per scrape. Files: `packages/llm/`, `packages/ai/failover_client.py`, `packages/ai/model_catalog.py`, `packages/config/settings.py`, `backend/server.py`, `frontend/src/v5/components/ProviderConsole.jsx`, `docs/llm-router/`, `tests/test_llm_router_*.py`.

- **LLM routing layer — one gateway for every model call, so no single provider, key, model, or quota can stop the platform** (2026-07-31). New `packages/llm/` package (ADR-008). The platform already survived most provider failures — `failover_client.py` walked a chain, `key_pool.py` rotated keys, `traffic_director.py` ordered by utilisation — but each lived in a different module with a different policy, and fifteen call sites imported LLM plumbing directly, so an improvement in one path never reached the others. `LLMRouter` is the single egress point on the enabled path — the flag defaults to off and unmigrated callers still run through `packages/ai/`, so this is the target architecture rather than the whole runtime today.
  - **Failures are attributed to the narrowest scope that explains them.** A 429 cools *that key* and the request retries the same provider with the next key; a 404 naming a model skips *that model* and tries its siblings; only 5xx/timeout/reset counts against the provider's circuit breaker. Marking a whole provider dead because one model was decommissioned is exactly the failure behind the schedule-multiplication incident (`CLAUDE.md` §7), and there is a regression test for it. A 429 is allowed to open a breaker only once every key for that provider is already cooling.
  - **Circuit breaker with half-open probing.** Trips on 5 consecutive failures *or* a >50% rolling failure rate over 10+ samples — the rate rule catches the provider that fails 60% of the time but never five in a row, which a consecutive-only rule misses entirely. Recovery admits exactly one probe rather than the full firehose, and each further trip doubles the open window to a 15-minute cap, so a provider that is genuinely down stops being probed every minute while one that blipped recovers in a minute.
  - **Bulkhead isolation.** Each provider holds its own concurrency semaphore. Without it, one provider timing out at 60s drains the shared pool and stalls providers that are perfectly healthy — the outage spreads instead of being contained. Local Ollama gets 2 slots (a GPU serialises anyway); vLLM gets 16 (continuous batching genuinely parallelises).
  - **Providers are data, not code.** One `OpenAICompatible` adapter serves OpenAI, Azure, Groq, Cerebras, NVIDIA NIM, OpenRouter, Together, Fireworks, DeepInfra, Mistral, LM Studio, vLLM, LiteLLM, and LocalAI; only Anthropic, Gemini, and Ollama need bespoke adapters, because only they have a different wire format. Adding a vendor is a `providers.yaml` entry. Writing 290 hand-written adapters — the OmniRoute approach — would have tripled the code for zero capability.
  - **Fifteen routing strategies**, configurable globally, per agent, or per request. The default `adaptive` blends live success rate (weight 3.0), 429 frequency (2.0), load (1.5), latency (1.0), and cost (0.5), so a degrading provider sinks within one rolling window without anyone editing YAML and climbs back as soon as it recovers — unlike the static priority list it replaces.
  - **Context is managed losslessly.** Escalate to a larger window → prune tool blobs and duplicate turns → sliding window → summarise the dropped span with a cheap model → retrieve the dropped turns relevant to the question → chunk oversized documents. The first user turn is never dropped because it carries the task definition. Message *text* is never rewritten: OmniRoute's 12-engine lossy compression was studied and rejected, because it corrupts code blocks, tool-call JSON, and structured outputs, and every agent here depends on all three.
  - **Six optional YAML files** under `config/llm/` (`providers`, `models`, `routing`, `keys`, `health`, `cache`). All optional — absent files fall back to defaults derived from the environment variables the platform already reads, so an untouched deployment behaves identically with no config at all. `keys.yaml` holds variable *names*, never key material; values resolve from the environment at call time, so a rotated key takes effect without a reload and nothing is persisted.
  - Also: priority queue with backpressure (`QueueFull` rather than unbounded growth), request deduplication collapsing identical concurrent calls onto one completion, five independently-configurable cache layers, token/cost accounting by agent/workflow/provider/model with threshold alerts, dependency-free Prometheus exposition at `GET /api/llm/metrics`, optional Redis-backed distributed rate limiting and durable queue, and an optional OpenAI-compatible gateway at `/api/llm/v1/*` for other applications.
  - **Backwards compatible by shim, not rewrite.** `failover_chat_completion()` keeps its exact signature, return type, and raised exception; with `LLM_ROUTER_ENABLED` unset (the default) the legacy implementation runs unchanged. The flag is read per call, so rollback needs no restart. A test asserts no model from `packages/ai/registry.py` disappears from the new registry.
  - 195 new tests across 8 files covering config loading, provider adapters, error classification, circuit breaker, key rotation, retry/backoff, all 15 strategies, queue/bulkhead/dedupe, caching, context fitting, budgets, end-to-end failover under 429/5xx/timeout/reset, streaming, sustained load, the gateway API, and the compatibility shim. Files: `packages/llm/` (16 modules), `config/llm/` (6 files), `backend/server.py`, `packages/ai/failover_client.py`, `frontend/src/api.js`, `docs/adr/008-llm-router-multi-provider.md`, `docs/llm-router/` (6 guides).

- **Provider page rebuilt as one health-ranked console** (2026-07-31). The Providers screen stacked five surfaces that each answered "what is running?" differently: a brain card, a local-brain switch, a provider-health switch, a paid kill switch, a grid of database providers, and a second grid of catalogue providers. The same provider could appear in three of them with three different states, the header's "default provider" badge read the database while the brain card read live routing, and the per-surface assignment matrix was nested *inside* the "N configured providers" text label — a grid rendered inside a text node.
  - **One list.** Live routed providers, database-configured providers, and the catalogue of things you could configure are merged on identity (with vendor aliases, so `nvidia-nim` and `nvidia` collapse) into a single table. A provider appears exactly once and its row states which of those it is.
  - **Health is the sort order.** Serving first, then healthy, probing, degraded, tripped, idle, and unconfigured last — with filter chips and live counts. You never hunt for the row that matters.
  - **"Brain" is a role, not a place.** Exactly one row carries the SERVING badge — what the router would pick for the next request — with the reason stated beside it (strategy, success rate, p95). Every switch that had its own card is now one control in one rail. The three legacy cards are preserved behind a disclosure because they still do things the console does not (per-role model assignment, the cross-machine local-brain toggle), rather than being deleted.
  - Each row expands to per-key health (by digest, never by value), models, adapter, bulkhead occupancy, last error, and actions: probe now, take out of / restore to rotation, test, edit, delete. Files: `frontend/src/v5/components/ProviderConsole.jsx` (new), `frontend/src/v5/screens/ProvidersScreen.jsx`, `frontend/src/api.js`.

- **MCP tool annotations support (MCP spec 2025-11-05 §5.6.1)** (2026-07-27). `agent/mcp_client.py` now models and exposes the per-tool behavioural hints that MCP servers may include in `tools/list` responses. Three additions: `ToolAnnotations` dataclass with four typed hint fields (`read_only_hint`, `destructive_hint`, `idempotent_hint`, `open_world_hint`) and an `is_safe_to_explore` convenience property (True only when `read_only_hint=True` AND `destructive_hint=False` — absent or unknown hints are treated conservatively as unsafe); `get_tool_annotations(tools, name)` extracts typed annotations for a named tool from any `list_tools()` result (returns an all-`None` instance when the tool is absent or carries no `annotations` block, so callers always receive "unknown" rather than an exception); `filter_safe_tools(tools)` returns only tools where both hints are explicit — callers can pass the filtered list to an exploration-mode agent that must not produce side-effects. 19 new tests in `tests/test_daily_2026_07_27.py`. Files: `agent/mcp_client.py`, `tests/test_daily_2026_07_27.py`.

### Fixed

- **Health ping timeout raised from 2s to 4.5s** (2026-07-31). The 2s ceiling added in the routing layer caused `hibernate-wake-error` on Render free tier: a cold MongoDB Atlas connection on wake-up can take 3-5s, so the ping reported degraded before Atlas responded, and Render killed the waking instance. 4.5s stays under Render'''s 5s health check deadline while giving Atlas room to connect.

### Fixed

- **Key rotation hardened after review: an exhausted pool no longer falls back to the resting primary key, rotation is opt-in per provider, and the digest is actually keyed** (2026-07-27). Four defects in the rotation shipped hours earlier, three of them found by automated review and reproduced before fixing.
  - **An exhausted pool sent the primary key anyway.** Once every key is cooling `next_key` returns `None`, and `_build_request`'s `api_key or provider.api_key` fallback then used the provider record's primary key — the very key that was supposed to be resting. Reachable with the shipped defaults: a provider whose breaker reopens after 30s while its keys still rest on the 60s key cooldown. The chain now declines to dispatch, which is the same rule the rest of this work established — a cooldown is not something to route around.
  - **Rotation is now opt-in per provider** via `<PROVIDER>_KEY_ROTATION`, not automatic on the presence of `<KEY>_2`. Several providers' acceptable-use policies prohibit registering additional accounts to exceed published limits — Groq documents limits per organisation and forbids orchestrating accounts around them — so automatic discovery would have let the platform commit a terms violation on the operator's behalf from nothing more than an env var existing. The docs no longer use Groq as the worked example and state the constraint before the configuration.
  - **The digest is keyed now, and the docs stop over-claiming.** It was `sha256(key)[:8]`, described as "salted" — it was not. An unsalted prefix is a permanent fingerprint: the same key yields the same label in every deployment forever, so a label from a log can be matched against a candidate key offline and two logs correlated as using the same account. Now BLAKE2 keyed with a per-process random salt, and documented as stable only within a process.
  - **Environment reading moved into `brain_config`**, the only module permitted to read it (CLAUDE.md §3) — and the right home for a secret-discovery path regardless. The pool logger also joins the repo's `qwen-proxy` hierarchy.
  - Second review round: an **absent primary key no longer promotes a sibling**. With `<BASE>` unset but `<BASE>_2` still present the sibling was returned and dispatched, because the first-gap rule started at slot two rather than slot one — so clearing a provider's key to switch it off would silently keep calling it on a leftover variable. Two regression tests, both verified to fail against the pre-fix code.
  - 16 further tests (42 total). The exhausted-pool guard is verified to fail against the pre-fix code, and the digest guard asserts the label is neither a bare SHA-256 nor an unkeyed BLAKE2 of the key.

- **Rate-limit cooldowns were erased on every exhausted call, so four free providers were hammered continuously instead of backing off** (2026-07-27). Production logged `brain_failover: all 4 provider(s) exhausted (nvidia, google, zai, groq) — nvidia 429 rate-limited; google 429 rate-limited; zai 429 rate-limited; groq 429 rate-limited` every ~15 seconds for over 20 minutes, and blocked tasks after 5 failed dispatch attempts. The chain was not failing to *distribute* traffic; it was refusing to *wait*. The same mistake appeared in three separate routes: **when everything is cooling, ignore the cooling and try everything anyway.**
  - **`failover_client._recover_all_unhealthy` called `record_success()` on every provider** when all were unhealthy. That is not a breaker reset, it is a lie: `record_success` sets `health = CLOSED` **and `failure_count = 0`**, and `is_healthy` returns `True` for `CLOSED` *without ever consulting `cooldown_until`* — so it erased both the cooldown and the exponential-backoff counter that produced it. The cycle was: all four 429 -> all marked OPEN -> next request resets all four -> all four hammered again -> 429. Backoff could never grow past its base value because the counter never survived a cycle, which is why the interval stayed at ~15s instead of climbing to 480s.
  - **Recovery no longer fabricates success.** New `FailoverManager.allow_probe()` moves a provider to HALF_OPEN while *preserving* `failure_count` and `cooldown_until`, so a failed probe backs off further rather than restarting the ladder. When every provider is in an ordinary 429 backoff the chain now declines to reset and reports the wait (`soonest retry in 42s`) instead of probing a fleet that has already said "not yet". The original anti-deadlock intent is kept as a bounded safety valve: `stuck_beyond_cooldown()` still allows a probe for a provider wedged far past its own window, so a corrupted `cooldown_until` cannot permanently disable the brain.
  - **The router's last-resort bypass had the same shape.** When every provider was on cooldown it called them all anyway. That is right for a cooldown that may no longer reflect reality — a connection blip, a one-off 5xx — but wrong for a 429, where it spends a request to be told the same thing and pushes the rate-limit window further out. Providers cooling from a 429 are now excluded from the bypass; if that leaves nothing, the request fails with a clear message rather than hammering.
  - **`nim_pool` counted every sub-500 response as a breaker success**, so 429 and 419 — the dominant failure mode for a NIM pool — reset the failure counter and the breaker could never open for the one condition it exists to protect against. Rate limits now record a failure on both the return and the raise path; the response itself is returned unchanged, so callers' own 429 handling is untouched.
  - `openclaw_gateway` was audited and needed no change: it records success only on a genuine 2xx and has no reset-all-breakers path.
  - Self-review follow-up: the anti-wedge valve's threshold was `4 * cooldown_seconds` floored at 900s, but `record_failure` caps the 429 exponent at 4, so the widest *legitimate* backoff is `cooldown_seconds * 2**4` — 1440s for NVIDIA's 90s base, above that 900s floor. The valve would therefore have fired on a perfectly healthy backoff ladder and re-probed it, restoring the hammering on the provider that rate-limits most. The factor is now double the exponent cap, leaving headroom above any real ladder, and the guard asserts the invariant against `_PROVIDER_REGISTRY` rather than the providers that happen to be configured — the first version of that test walked the live registry and passed against the broken threshold, because NVIDIA has no key in CI and so was never checked.
  - 17 new tests in `tests/test_rate_limit_backoff_survives.py` plus one at chain level, covering all four routes. Each is verified to **fail against the pre-fix code** — the router one reproduces the production symptom directly (`bypass hammered rate-limited providers: ['groq', 'cerebras']`), and one asserts that backoff windows strictly grow across four consecutive 429s, which is exactly what the reset destroyed.

- **A failed Render deploy reported itself as a green job, and four shipped directories could not deploy at all** (2026-07-27). `Deploy Backend to Render` fires the Render deploy hook and then polls `/api/health`. Render keeps the previous instance serving until the new image builds, and this image builds a React bundle and installs Chromium — so the poll, which started **20 seconds** after triggering, was answered by the *old* instance every time. On timeout the step ran `exit 0`. The job could therefore only ever be green: a build that failed on Render and one that succeeded produced identical output.
  - **`/api/health` now reports what it is running** — `version` plus `commit`, the latter from `RENDER_GIT_COMMIT` (falling back to `GIT_COMMIT` / `SOURCE_COMMIT`) via a new `version.deployed_commit()`. Both are public facts — the version is in the repo and the SHA is a public commit — so the endpoint stays unauthenticated. Without a build marker the endpoint looked identical before and after a deploy, which is the whole reason the check could not tell them apart.
  - **The verification step waits for the commit that was just pushed** and exits non-zero when it never arrives, pointing at the Render build log. A host that stamps no commit is treated as *unverifiable*, not as a mismatch: the step warns and passes rather than failing every deploy on a platform that does not set the variable.
  - **Four directories the image ships were missing from the deploy trigger.** `Dockerfile.backend` COPYs `packages/`, `loops/`, `voice/` and `.claude/`, but none appeared in the workflow's `paths:` filter — so a change confined to any of them produced no deploy at all. `packages/` is the entire AI layer (router, brain config, providers, traffic director); a fix there would have sat on master looking shipped.
  - **Root modules are now taken wholesale (`*.py`), matching the Dockerfile.** The Dockerfile moved to `COPY *.py ./` precisely because copying modules one-by-one kept dropping new ones; the deploy filter had gone on listing 11 by name, leaving 24 shipped modules — `proxy.py`, `chat_handlers.py`, `telegram_bot.py`, `app_settings.py` and the rest — unable to trigger a deploy on their own.
  - New `tests/test_deploy_trigger_covers_image.py` parses both files and fails when they drift apart again, plus four tests on the health contract. All four drift guards are verified to fail against the pre-fix workflow — the bug class is guarded, not just the instances.

- **Three free providers were classified as paid, and free-only calls refused rather than use them** (2026-07-27). `provider_access_tier` decided a provider's tier by checking a hostname substring list *before* the explicit provider-id lists. `together-free`, `qwen-dashscope` and `google-gemini-free` are all registered in `_FREE_CLOUD_PROVIDER_IDS`, but their hosts each contain a shorter entry from the commercial list — `api.together.xyz` contains `together.xyz`, `dashscope.aliyuncs.com` contains `aliyuncs.com`, `generativelanguage.googleapis.com` contains `googleapis.com` — so all three came back `commercial`. Two of them (`dashscope.aliyuncs.com`, `generativelanguage.googleapis.com`) are also listed verbatim in `_KNOWN_FREE_HOSTS`; the shorter commercial substring simply matched first. Any call made with `allow_commercial_fallback=False` therefore deferred them and raised `CommercialFallbackRequiredError` — asking a human to approve paid spend — while three configured, healthy, free providers sat unused.
  - **Explicit ids now win over hostname inference.** An id in one of the tier sets is a declaration about that provider; a hostname substring is a guess. The guess is now only consulted for ids that appear in no list.
  - **Hostname inference resolves by longest match**, so ordering the two lists can no longer decide the answer: `dashscope.aliyuncs.com` (20 chars) beats `aliyuncs.com` (12) for an unlisted id on that host, while `bailian.aliyuncs.com` still resolves commercial. Adding a broad new entry to either list can no longer silently re-tier an existing provider in the other.
  - The practical effect on failover order is that the free tier is now one contiguous run of eight providers (`deepseek`, `groq`, `sambanova`, `cerebras`, `qwen-dashscope`, `together-free`, `mistral`, `google-gemini-free`) instead of three fragments split by mis-tiered members — which is also what gives the new traffic distribution a pool worth spreading across.
  - Nothing moved the other way: `anthropic`, `openrouter`, `bedrock`, `zhipu` and `minimax` are pinned as commercial by a test, so the fix cannot start spending money that the old classification withheld.

- **Video URLs are now readable: transcript extraction, a `video-context` skill, and a reject that the scheduled sweep actually honours** (2026-07-27). A quick-note issue carries its whole value in its URL, and some of those URLs are videos. `fetch_url.py` reduced a YouTube watch page to navigation chrome and a title — none of what was said — so context generated from a video link was derived from the URL slug and the model's priors about the topic. That is precisely the ungrounded-plan failure `docs/QUICK_NOTE_CONTEXT_RULEBOOK.md` R1 exists to catch, arriving through a path R1 could not see.
  - New `.github/scripts/video_transcript.py` — standard library only, no API key, no account, no new dependency. Parses the video id from every YouTube URL shape (`/watch?v=`, `youtu.be/`, `/shorts/`, `/embed/`, `/live/`) while ignoring the tracking parameters social shares append; brace-matches the `ytInitialPlayerResponse` blob out of the watch page (a lazy regex truncates it, because the blob nests objects and carries escaped braces inside strings); reads `captions.playerCaptionsTracklistRenderer.captionTracks`; and flattens the `json3` timed-text format to prose, falling back to the older `<transcript><text>` XML. Track preference is manually-written English, then auto-generated English, then any language — and when the track is `kind: "asr"` the output header says so, because ASR wording is approximate and must not be quoted as verbatim speech.
  - **It never raises and never fabricates.** Captions disabled, an age-gated video, or a blocked request all return `""`, and `fetch_url.py` falls through to its ordinary page strategies rather than failing the run. Long talks are capped at 20,000 chars so one video cannot consume an entire context window.
  - Wired into `fetch_url.py` as **Strategy 0**, ahead of the page-fetch strategies and gated on the host, so `generate_context.py` receives spoken content instead of chrome. `bulk-issue-context.yml` stages the module into `/tmp` alongside the scripts it already copies — without it, a video quick-note would silently degrade to page chrome after a branch switch.
  - New **`.claude/skills/video-context/SKILL.md`** so agents and Claude Code sessions reach for this instead of guessing a video's contents from its title (a fabricated-specifics failure, CLAUDE.md §14.10 pattern 1). It documents the retrieval path, what to do with a transcript, and the limits that matter — no captions, ASR approximation, age-gating, datacenter-IP blocks, and the 20k truncation.
  - **A `reject` verdict now survives the scheduled sweep.** `issue-context-generator.yml` skipped the immediate implementation dispatch on `reject`, but deliberately leaves the issue **open**, and `process-quick-note.yml` selects open issues on a 4-hourly cron without consulting any verdict — so a rejected source would have been picked up hours later and built anyway, defeating the rejection entirely. The generator now labels rejected issues `quick-note:rejected` and the sweep excludes that label. It is kept distinct from the existing `quick-note:exhausted` because the two mean different things: `exhausted` is three failed attempts, `rejected` is a deliberate decision not to build. Removing the label hands the issue back to the sweep, and the rejection comment says so.
  - 35 new tests in `tests/test_video_transcript.py`, all hermetic — URL shapes, caption-track preference, both timed-text formats, brace matching against nested and string-embedded braces, ASR labelling, truncation, and the "returns empty rather than fabricating" contract. The network paths are deliberately not unit-tested; a mock of them would assert only that the mock was called. Three further tests pin the integration: Strategy 0 ordering in `fetch_url.py`, the `/tmp` staging in the bulk workflow, and the reject-label filter in both workflows.

- **Self-healing agent: infrastructure errors no longer trigger code fixes** (2026-07-27). Issue [#1134](https://github.com/strikersam/autonomous-ai-agency/issues/1134) — the `SelfHealingAgent` classified a `pymongo.errors.ServerSelectionTimeoutError: [Errno 111] Connection refused` as `timeout` and dispatched an improvement task asking the executor to "add retry logic". No code change can make a service that isn't running respond; the dispatched task spent provider tokens and changed nothing. Two fixes: (1) A new `INFRASTRUCTURE_ERROR` category in `FailureCategory` is checked **before** `timeout` and `network_error` in `_classify_failure`.
