It matches error texts from MongoDB (`ServerSelectionTimeoutError`, `MongoClient`, `MongoError`, `[Errno 111]`), Redis, Postgres/psycopg, Docker (`Cannot connect to the Docker daemon`), and generic `Service Unavailable`. (2) In `on_ci_failure`, when the failure is classified as `INFRASTRUCTURE_ERROR` the fix dispatch is skipped entirely and the healing event is set to `awaiting_human` immediately — with a warning log explaining that ops intervention is required. Regular code failures (assertions, syntax errors, import errors) are unaffected. Hint text updated for `infrastructure_error` to state clearly that no code change helps. 19 new tests in `tests/test_daily_2026_07_27.py`. Files: `agent/self_healing.py`, `tests/test_daily_2026_07_27.py`.

- **Quick-note context generation: a crashed entrypoint, and a rulebook so the output stops being filler** (2026-07-27). Issue [#1135](https://github.com/strikersam/autonomous-ai-agency/issues/1135) produced two comments and no plan: a `NameError: name 'ma' is not defined`, then a block of generic boilerplate. Both had the same root cause — nothing checked that the pipeline's output was worth anything.
  - **Two scripts in this pipeline ended in a bare `ma` instead of `main()`.** `.github/scripts/generate_context.py` (every context generation died at the last line of the file) and `.github/scripts/apply_review.py` (invoked by `process-quick-note.yml` to apply review feedback, dead the same way). Neither is a SyntaxError, so `compileall` — the only gate in CI that reads these files — passed both; and the one test covering `generate_context` loads it under a non-`__main__` name, which never evaluates the guarded line, so the suite passed too. Both now call `main()`, and three scripts regained the trailing newline whose absence is how the truncation announced itself.
  - **A third truncation, and the worse kind.** `.github/scripts/nvidia_models.py` ended in `CANDIDATE_MODELS = NVIDIA_CANDIDATE_MO`. Same defect, but in a plain module-level assignment rather than a `__main__` guard, so it raises `NameError` at **import** — any consumer doing `from nvidia_models import CANDIDATE_MODELS` fails immediately. That is why all three of its intended consumers (`apply_review.py`, `implement_agent.py`, `review_agent.py`) still keep their own inline copy of the model list: the shared module they were meant to import could never be imported.
  - **The bug class is now guarded, not just the instances.** `tests/test_context_rulebook.py` parses every script in `.github/scripts/` and fails on three things: a `__main__` guard whose body is a bare name, a module-level reference to a name the module never binds, and a missing trailing newline. The first two are each verified to fail against the real truncated files; `compileall` passes all of them, which is why CI never noticed. Adding the trailing newline is explicitly *not* the fix — it removes the only visible symptom, which is how the `nvidia_models.py` truncation initially slipped past this change.
  - **New `docs/QUICK_NOTE_CONTEXT_RULEBOOK.md` — 12 rules, 11 of them machine-enforced** by the new `.github/scripts/context_rules.py`. Each rule names the defect it prevents, drawn from documents the pipeline actually shipped: TODOs whose first step was *"Review the linked repository"* (R5 — reading the source is the generator's job, and it already happened); *"features such as improved model routing"* (R6 — hedges that read like facts); `key_store.py` listed under Risk Flags by a plan that never touched it (R7 — a flag is earned only when a TODO modifies the module); fabricated-but-plausible file paths (R8 — checked against the filesystem); and prompts that re-taught `pytest -x` and type hints (R9 — every agent already loads CLAUDE.md, so repeating it just pads a thin plan).
  - **`reject` is now a first-class verdict.** Every document records `adopt`, `adapt`, or `reject` with a reason. Most linked artifacts genuinely do not belong in this codebase, and the old pipeline had no way to say so — it always emitted a plan, which is how unrelated sources turned into invented features. `issue-context-generator.yml` reads the verdict and **does not dispatch the implementation workflow on a reject**, so an agent is no longer sent to build what the analysis just ruled out.
  - **The gate runs before anything is published**, feeds violations back for exactly one repair round, and keeps the repaired result only when it scores strictly better (a model can regress). Output that still fails ships with a **Quality Gate** section listing every unmet rule by ID — slop is never published silently, and a reviewer can triage in seconds instead of reading a plausible-looking plan to the end before finding it empty.
  - **Every document now opens with a Source Grounding block** — the URL, whether the fetch actually succeeded and how many characters it returned, the provider and model, and the repair count. Whether a plan was derived from retrieved content or from the URL slug alone was previously invisible, which is exactly what let ungrounded plans read as researched.
  - **`fetch_url.py` now strips site chrome before the 6000-char cap.** On the gist in #1135 it removes 1,434 chars of GitHub navigation furniture ("Sign in", "You must be signed in to star a gist", the embed block rendered twice), which had been consuming the window the article needed: the preamble before the article body drops from 1,773 chars to 405, so the same 6,000-char budget now carries 5,595 chars of real content instead of 4,227. Removal is exact-match against a chrome list plus de-duplication of short repeated lines, so long-form prose can never be truncated. Unicode non-breaking spaces are normalised first — `Sign\xa0in` had been defeating the match.
  - **`scripts/enrich_quick_note_issues.py` no longer writes context.** It posted a fixed template — identical on every issue, never grounded in the linked URL, naming files it had no intention of touching — on a 4-hourly cron; that comment is the second one on #1135. It now detects quick-note issues with no context branch and dispatches `bulk-issue-context.yml`, which owns generation. Detection here, generation there, one implementation of the rulebook (CLAUDE.md §2.1). Dispatch is capped per run (`--max-dispatch`, default 10) so an unattended cron cannot stampede a backlog. It also pointed at the pre-rename repository name, so its GitHub calls were resolving only through a redirect.
  - 66 new tests in `tests/test_context_rulebook.py`, including parity assertions in both directions between the rulebook and the gate — a rule enforced but undocumented, or documented as `[gate]` but unenforced, now fails CI.

- **A stale model catalogue no longer reads as a dead account, and no longer switches the provider off** (2026-07-26). `config/models.yaml` lists three models for Cerebras — `qwen-3-coder-480b`, `llama-3.3-70b`, `llama-3.1-8b`. An operator whose key serves none of them (routine: providers archive models, and free and paid tiers expose different subsets) had every candidate rejected as unknown, at which point `_try_provider` concluded `no accessible model (404 model_not_found)` and threw the provider's kill switch. The key was valid and the account in good standing the entire time; only the catalogue was wrong, and clearing the switch needed a human. The catalogue is a snapshot of what a provider *offered*, never of what a *key* is entitled to, so the two drift apart by design.
  - New `packages/ai/model_discovery.py` — asks the provider itself, via the standard OpenAI-compatible `GET /v1/models` route, which models the configured key may use. A provider that does not answer it is not a problem: the result is "unknown", never "no models", and the static list is used exactly as before. Cached for six hours on success and ten minutes on failure, because it runs on the failure path, which is exactly when a provider is least likely to answer. It never raises and never blocks: an unreachable provider leaves the previous behaviour untouched.
  - `_try_provider` now orders its attempts from that list when one is known — dropping models the key cannot use instead of spending the attempt budget on certain 404s, and trying models the key serves that the catalogue never listed. This is a **cache read only**; a chat request never waits on a second round trip.
  - `_auto_disable` on `404 model_not_found` is now gated on evidence. "No accessible model" is a claim about the account, so it is confirmed against the provider's own list before it is acted on. If that list names models nobody tried, the catalogue was stale and the provider stays enabled. The reprieve is granted **once** — if the key's own advertised models then fail too, the account really is unusable and the switch throws, so this always converges. When discovery is unavailable there is no evidence either way, and the established behaviour is kept.
  - The brain-config route `GET /admin/api/policy/brain` gained `served_models` on each provider, alongside the `candidates` it already reported, so the catalogue's guess and the provider's own answer are visible side by side; when they disagree, the catalogue is the stale one. (`GET /api/brain/providers`, which powers the Providers toggle, is a different endpoint and is unchanged.)
  - Disabling requires **complete** evidence. Only `_MAX_MODELS_PER_PROVIDER` (3) models are sent per round, so a key serving more than that can never have its whole list exercised in one call; attempts are therefore accumulated across rounds, untried models are ordered first, and the switch throws only once every model the key serves has been tried and rejected. Bounding it this way is also what removes the need to read shared cache state after the fact, so concurrent requests for the same provider cannot consume each other's reprieve.
  - 37 new tests (`tests/test_model_discovery.py`, plus two in `tests/test_unit5_ui_provider_surface.py`), and a `conftest` fixture dropping the discovery cache between tests so a listing left by one test cannot change which model the next one tries.

### Added

- **Multi-key rotation per provider — the one lever that adds capacity rather than rationing it** (2026-07-27). With ten of fourteen providers switched off for invalid keys or exhausted credit, four free tiers were carrying all traffic and no amount of distribution could help: spreading, pacing and backoff all divide a fixed budget, none of them raise the ceiling. Free tiers are rate-limited **per key**, not per provider, so a second account on a provider is a second budget.
  - New `packages/ai/key_pool.py` — round-robin selection across every key configured for a provider, with a per-key cooldown. On a 429 only the refused key rests (honouring `Retry-After`, clamped so a hostile value cannot park a key indefinitely) while its siblings keep serving; the provider itself is cooled only once **every** key is resting.
  - Configured as a numbered suffix on the existing variable — `GROQ_API_KEY`, `GROQ_API_KEY_2`, `GROQ_API_KEY_3`. The scan stops at the first gap so a typo'd `_4` cannot silently promote itself into the `_2` slot and leave an operator believing three keys are live when two are, and duplicates are collapsed because the same key twice is one budget, not two — counting it twice would advertise capacity that does not exist while halving the real cooldown.
  - **Inert with one key**, which is every provider's current configuration: `next_key` returns it unconditionally, `all_cooling` is immediately true, and the brain chain takes byte-for-byte the pre-rotation path. Rotation engages only from two keys up.
  - `ProviderInfo` gained `key_env` so the pool can find sibling variables from the registry rather than re-deriving the name from the provider id — several ids would not round-trip, and a wrong guess silently means "no extra keys" instead of failing loudly.
  - **No key material can reach a log or an API response.** Keys are identified in diagnostics by a short salted digest, never by a prefix or suffix of the key itself, because a leading fragment identifies the account outright for several providers. A test asserts the snapshot contains neither the key nor its first or last eight characters.
  - **The gain is across requests, not within one.** When a key is refused, `failover_client` has already added that provider to the request's `tried` set, so the current request moves on to the next provider rather than retrying the same one with the sibling key — spending several keys on one request would be the wrong trade. What changes is the provider's state afterwards: without rotation a single 429 cools it for 30–480s and every request in that window skips it; with rotation it stays `CLOSED` and the next request reaches it on the next key.
  - 26 new tests in `tests/test_key_pool.py`, including the pass-through guarantee for single-key providers, the gap-stop and duplicate-collapse rules, per-provider pool isolation, `Retry-After` clamping, and that Anthropic's native `x-api-key` header rotates too rather than only the OpenAI-compatible `Bearer` path.

- **Traffic distribution across providers, so free-tier keys stop taking turns being the one that 429s** (2026-07-27). The router walked its provider list in strict priority order: provider #1 absorbed every request until it refused, and only a 429 moved traffic to #2. Every recovery mechanism this repo has — exponential backoff, `Retry-After`, the 419 per-model skip, the dead-model memory, the probe lock — is *reactive*; each one makes the failure cheaper, none of them stops the first provider being driven into its per-minute ceiling while two idle free keys sit behind it. The missing half is spreading load *before* anyone refuses. Ported from the routing strategies in [LiteLLM's `Router`](https://github.com/BerriAI/litellm) (`litellm/router_strategy/`), adapted to this codebase's provider model.
  - New `packages/ai/traffic_director.py` with four distribution strategies alongside the existing order, selected by `LLM_ROUTING_STRATEGY`: `weighted-shuffle` (weighted random pick by `<PROVIDER>_WEIGHT`, falling back to remaining RPM headroom, then to an equal share — LiteLLM `simple_shuffle`), `least-busy` (fewest in-flight requests), `usage-based` (lowest fraction of its configured RPM/TPM budget spent in the current minute — LiteLLM `lowest_tpm_rpm_v2`), and `latency-based` (lowest EWMA latency, unsampled providers first — LiteLLM `lowest_latency`). The default is `priority`, which returns the list untouched, so **nothing changes until an operator opts in**.
  - **Reordering can never cross an access tier.** The router passes the tier rank from `provider_sort_key` as a grouping key and distribution happens strictly within each group, so no strategy can promote a paid commercial provider ahead of a free one and start spending money on a shuffle. Without that invariant, `least-busy` would do exactly that the moment the free tier got busy — which is precisely when it is most tempting and least wanted.
  - **A pre-call budget check that routes away instead of sleeping.** `packages/ai/rate_limiter.pace()` already paces requests when `<PROVIDER>_MAX_RPM` is set, but pacing *blocks the caller*; that is the right move only when there is nowhere else to go. `over_budget()` reports whether a provider has already spent its configured `_MAX_RPM` / `_MAX_TPM` / `_MAX_PARALLEL` for the current 60-second window, and the router skips it for the next provider — the same trick as LiteLLM's `async_pre_call_check`, which raises a synthetic rate-limit error so the caller falls through to another deployment rather than spending a real request to be told 429. With a single configured provider the check is disabled, because skipping the only provider would convert a slow request into a failed one; pacing stays the right tool there.
  - **Three new per-provider budgets**, all read through `packages/ai/brain_config.py` (never `os.environ` in business logic): `<PROVIDER>_MAX_TPM` (free tiers publish a token ceiling as well as a request one, and large-context agent calls hit the token one first), `<PROVIDER>_WEIGHT` (share weighting for `weighted-shuffle`), and `<PROVIDER>_MAX_PARALLEL` (the concurrency ceiling NVIDIA NIM enforces with 419 — configuring it lets the router spread load to a sibling instead of collecting the 419 first). Unset means "no limit", as does zero, a negative, `inf`, `nan`, or anything unparseable — a limit of zero would wedge a provider out of rotation permanently.
  - **`<PROVIDER>_MAX_RPM` was unsettable for the providers that need it most.** The lookup upper-cased the provider id, and the router's real ids are slugs — so pacing NVIDIA NIM required an environment variable literally named `NVIDIA-NIM_MAX_RPM`, which shells refuse to export and most dashboards refuse to store. All four budget vars now accept the dash-to-underscore form (`NVIDIA_NIM_MAX_RPM`) and still read the literal name first, so any variable an operator already set keeps working.
  - **Ties are broken randomly, deliberately.** At cold start every provider scores zero on every metric, and a stable sort would hand the entire opening burst to whichever one happened to be first — reintroducing the pile-up by the back door. A provider that returned a 429 inside the current window also carries a fixed usage penalty, so it sorts behind its healthy siblings for a minute after its cooldown expires rather than being the first one probed again.
  - New `GET /api/metrics/traffic-distribution` reports the active strategy and, per provider, in-flight count, requests and tokens used in the current window, the configured budgets, EWMA latency, and how many requests were routed away for being over budget. It is the counterpart to `/api/metrics/rate-limits`: that endpoint reports what providers *told us* about their quota, this one reports what the router actually did with it.
  - Accounting is best-effort by construction and cannot fail a request: the director is fetched through a helper that returns `None` on any import error, every call site swallows exceptions, and `record_end` runs in a `finally` so a successful early return can never leak an in-flight count and pin a provider at its concurrency ceiling forever.
  - Review follow-ups: a fractional `<PROVIDER>_MAX_PARALLEL` no longer truncates to a hard `0` (`int(0.5)` is `0`, and a concurrency cap of zero makes the `in_flight >= cap` check true at zero in-flight requests, excluding the provider permanently — the exact "limit of zero" outcome a malformed value must never produce); the latency reported to the director is the value measured when the response arrives rather than re-measured after JSON parsing, task classification and cost attribution, which had been folding local post-processing into what `latency-based` routing reads as provider round-trip time; and `record_start` is exception-guarded like `record_end`, so the module's "nothing here can fail a request" invariant holds at every call site.
  - 38 new tests in `tests/test_traffic_director.py` — each strategy's ordering, the tier-crossing invariant, random tie-breaking, window expiry, every budget check, the dashed-provider-id env lookup, and four router-integration tests pinning the two behaviours that matter most: an over-budget provider is skipped for its sibling, and the default configuration still produces the exact strict-priority sequence it always did.

  **On the second linked repository:** [`tiiuae/onebitllms`](https://github.com/tiiuae/onebitllms) was reviewed and nothing was taken from it. It is a fine-tuning library for producing 1.58-bit BitNet checkpoints — quantisation-aware training and weight packing. It has no routing, load-balancing, or rate-limit handling of any kind, and this agency calls hosted inference endpoints rather than training or serving its own weights, so there is no seam where it would apply.

- **Public brain-outage diagnosis, and a supervisor that stops destroying the backlog during one** (2026-07-26). A blocked task reported only `No runtime available: All runtimes failed and policy prevents paid escalation … All brain providers exhausted — none configured or all in cooldown`. That message means the failover chain was **empty** — zero providers were even attempted — but nothing on an unauthenticated surface said which provider was off or why, so diagnosing it required an authenticated call to `/api/brain/providers`. Two changes:
  - `services/brain_failover.brain_availability_summary()` — one non-secret answer to "can the brain answer right now?", reporting `total` / `usable` / `disabled` (with the pre-mapped remedy) / `cooling` / `state_durable`. Deriving it in the module that owns provider state keeps its three consumers from disagreeing. It deliberately excludes API keys, key fragments, provider base URLs and raw `last_error` text (a provider error body can echo a token), so it is safe to serve unauthenticated — pinned by two tests asserting no secret reaches the response.
  - New **`brain_providers` check on `GET /api/doctor/public`** (already the sanctioned unauthenticated diagnostic, alongside the existing Ollama and runtime checks, which covered the local engine and the runtimes but never the failover chain). `fail` with the per-provider reason and remedy when nothing is usable, `warn` on reduced capacity, `pass` otherwise; it also warns when provider on/off state is not durable and will reset on the next deploy.
  - **`CEOSupervisor` now checks brain availability before acting.** Every runtime plans through the brain, so waking runtimes does nothing when the chain is empty — the supervisor would re-drive stalled goals into certain failure, and because each re-drive costs an intervention, three sweeps of an outage abandoned the entire backlog for a reason unrelated to any goal in it. Re-drive and abandonment are both suspended while no provider is usable; closing finished goals continues, since that needs no brain. A paused goal is recoverable, an abandoned one is not. `SweepReport` gained `brain_unavailable` and `paused` so a sweep that did nothing explains itself, and `GET /api/ceo/status` now returns the same `brain` block — "the CEO is doing nothing" is almost always this. The check **fails open**: if it errors, the normal path runs, because wrongly pausing every goal is worse than one wasted re-drive.
  - 14 new tests in `tests/test_brain_availability_doctor.py`.

- **CEO micro-management: a tier ladder, an anti-slop gate, a durable ledger, and a 24x7 supervisor** (2026-07-25). The CEO delegated work and then never looked at it again. It split every request the same way (one scout, one dev), recorded whatever came back as done, and had no memory of a delegation once the coroutine ended — so a subtask that returned "all done" having changed nothing was indistinguishable from a subtask that worked, and a delegation interrupted by a redeploy or a sleeping runtime was simply lost. The agency looked busy and silently dropped work. Four new pieces, modelled on the MicroManager orchestration pattern (a delegating orchestrator plus a ladder of implementer tiers) and mapped onto the runtimes this repo already has:
  - `services/ceo_micromanager.py` — a four-rung tier ladder (`intern → junior → midlevel → senior`), each with its own step budget, file ceiling and written remit; brain-driven decomposition into subtasks small enough to finish (at most 2 files each), with the previous scout-then-dev split kept as the deterministic fallback when the brain is unreachable, and the strategy recorded so an outage is visible rather than silently degrading; a seven-section subtask brief (Context / Scope / Focus / Outcome / Completion / Instruction Priority / Mode Restriction) so a delegated agent never has to invent its own scope; and the **anti-slop quality gate** that judges every result before it is accepted.
  - `services/ceo_ledger.py` — durable record of every goal, subtask, attempt and verdict, following the dual-backend contract of `agent/schedule_store.py` (Mongo in production, SQLite in dev/CI, in-memory when neither is reachable, never raises). Writing the plan *before* execution is what makes closure trackable: a process that dies mid-fan-out leaves an open goal with pending subtasks, not nothing.
  - `services/ceo_supervisor.py` — the 24x7 babysitter, wired into `services/background.py` alongside the existing autonomy loops. Each sweep force-wakes sleeping runtimes, then closes goals whose subtasks all passed, re-drives goals that have made no progress past the stall threshold, and abandons goals that spent their intervention budget or age limit. `closed` and `abandoned` are deliberately distinct states so a failure can never read as success. A drive that ends `PARTIAL` or `FAILED` leaves the goal **open** rather than abandoning it — the escalation ladder is bounded to a single drive, so a failure caused by a transient condition (a sleeping runtime, a provider mid-429) would otherwise be recorded as permanent the moment it happened. Only the supervisor, which holds the intervention budget, gives up on a goal.
  - `backend/ceo_router.py` — `GET /api/ceo/status`, `/api/ceo/goals`, `/api/ceo/goals/{id}`, plus admin-only `POST /api/ceo/sweep` and `/api/ceo/goals/{id}/redrive` (admin-gated because both start real work that spends provider tokens; the manual re-drive still honours the intervention cap, so it cannot be used to loop a broken goal).

  **Escalation is bounded twice over** — by the ladder itself (a subtask at `senior` has nowhere to go) and by `CEO_MAX_ATTEMPTS_PER_SUBTASK`, which is clamped to the ladder length when read, so an env typo cannot uncap retries on an unattended loop. Worst case for one subtask is 3 executions, not a retry storm. The supervisor carries its own five bounds (sweep interval, stall threshold, per-goal intervention cap, per-goal age cap, and a cap on re-drives per sweep so one sweep cannot stampede after an outage), all clamped on read.

  **The file-based slop checks are conditional on the runtime being able to report.** Only `internal_agent` and `e2b` return the files they touched; `hermes`, `claude_code` and `goose` return prose. Applying "you changed no files" to a runtime that cannot say would reject every successful subtask it ran, so the gate records that it could not verify and judges on the remaining signals. Similarly the give-up detector matches only the summary's *first sentence* — a genuine refusal opens the message, whereas scanning the whole summary would escalate honest work for describing a bug it correctly declined to fix.

  New env knobs, all optional: `CEO_MAX_SUBTASKS` (6), `CEO_MAX_ATTEMPTS_PER_SUBTASK` (3), `CEO_MIN_OUTPUT_CHARS` (24), `CEO_REQUIRE_TESTS_FOR_CODE` (true), `CEO_LLM_DECOMPOSITION` (true; off under `TESTING`), `CEO_ESCALATION_ENABLED` (true), `CEO_SUPERVISOR_ENABLED` (true; off under `TESTING`), `CEO_SUPERVISOR_INTERVAL_S` (180), `CEO_SUPERVISOR_STALL_S` (1800), `CEO_SUPERVISOR_MAX_INTERVENTIONS` (3), `CEO_SUPERVISOR_MAX_GOAL_AGE_S` (86400), `CEO_SUPERVISOR_MAX_REDRIVES_PER_SWEEP` (2). 84 new tests across `tests/test_ceo_micromanager.py`, `tests/test_ceo_supervision.py` and `tests/test_ceo_router.py`. New `ceo-supervisor` entry in `loops/registry.yaml`.

- **Per-provider on/off switch, with auto-disable for failures only a human can fix** (2026-07-25). A provider whose key is invalid, whose account is out of credit, or whose account cannot access any of its models fails identically forever — leaving it in the rotation cost a wasted attempt on every call and buried the providers that did work. `services/brain_failover.py` gained a persisted enable/disable layer (`disabled_providers()`, `set_provider_enabled()`, `auto_disable_provider()`, kv_store-backed so it survives restarts), and `next_provider()` now skips disabled providers. `packages/ai/failover_client.py` auto-disables on `401`/`403` (invalid key), `402` and billing-shaped 4xx (out of credit), and "no accessible model" (`404 model_not_found` on every model), each requiring an explicit manual re-enable. **Transient failures are deliberately excluded**: `429`, `5xx` and network errors already have circuit breakers and cooldowns, and auto-disabling on a `429` would switch off every free provider the first time a burst tripped a rate limit — strictly worse than the problem it solves. New `GET /api/brain/providers` (health + enabled state + the reason for an auto-disable; asserted to expose no API keys) and `PUT /api/brain/providers/{id}/enabled` (manual re-enable also clears the circuit breaker so it takes effect immediately rather than waiting out a stale cooldown). New `ProviderHealthToggleCard` on the Providers screen shows ONLINE / COOLDOWN / OFF per provider with the reason and a toggle. 12 new tests in `tests/test_provider_enable_disable.py`, including parametrised proof that each unfixable status auto-disables and each transient status does not.


- **Dashboard: rate-limit pacing, self-healing activity, and spend-by-task-type surfaced on the primary v5 screen** (2026-07-24). Three backend capabilities that already run in production had no visibility on the dashboard users actually see (`/v5`, not the legacy `/legacy` route): per-provider rate-limit quota (`GET /api/metrics/rate-limits`, existing), per-model/per-tag cost attribution (`GET /api/metrics/cost-attribution`, existing — previously only surfaced on the legacy `ObservabilityPage.js`), and the log-monitor/self-healing loop's real activity (previously only a boolean "running" flag in `/api/autonomy/status`, no event-level visibility anywhere). New `GET /api/metrics/self-heal` endpoint (auth required) returns `LogMonitor.get_stats()` plus the 20 most recent `SelfHealingAgent` healing events sorted newest-first, with active/resolved/awaiting-human counts; degrades gracefully to zeroed state when the engines aren't bootstrapped (e.g. bare `TestClient`). `frontend/src/v5/screens/DashboardScreen.jsx` gained three widgets — `RateLimiterWidget`, `SelfHealWidget`, `CostBreakdownWidget` — wired through the existing `useSafeData` multi-fetch hook (each widget fails independently; a down endpoint never blanks the screen) and the existing dependency-free `BarChart` component. Checked for overlap before building: loop-fleet readiness already has a dedicated `LoopsScreen.jsx` (readiness score dial + per-loop table) and CEO/agency cycle activity already has `CEOCyclePanel` in `AgentsScreen.jsx` — neither was duplicated. 3 new backend tests in `tests/test_self_heal_metrics.py`. Files: `backend/server.py`, `frontend/src/api.js`, `frontend/src/v5/screens/DashboardScreen.jsx`, `tests/test_self_heal_metrics.py`.

- **Structured output strict mode + refusal handling** (2026-07-24). As of mid-2026, `json_schema` with `strict: true` is the canonical structured-output pattern; `json_object` (legacy) is still supported but de-emphasised. Three additions to `packages/ai/structured_output.py`: `is_strict(response_format)` identifies strict-mode requests; `system_instruction()` now emits a stronger constraint when `strict: true` is set (tells the model to respond ONLY with schema-conformant JSON or emit `{"refusal": "<reason>"}` — the same contract native-strict providers use via the `choices[0].message.refusal` field); `extract_refusal(response_body)` detects both native provider refusals (`message.refusal` set + `content: null`) and the proxy convention (`content` is a `{"refusal": "..."}` singleton JSON object). Refusals are logged and excluded from the response cache so a rephrased request always gets a fresh model attempt. 33 new tests in `tests/test_daily_2026_07_24.py`. Files: `packages/ai/structured_output.py`, `packages/ai/router.py`, `tests/test_daily_2026_07_24.py`.

- **MCP tools/list TTL caching** (2026-07-24). Per the MCP spec 2026-07-28 RC, servers may include `ttlMs` in `tools/list` responses to signal that the tool list is stable for that duration. `agent/mcp_client.py` now caches the result and skips the JSON-RPC round-trip on subsequent `list_tools()` calls until the TTL elapses. When the server omits `ttlMs` a conservative default of 60 s applies (configurable via `MCP_TOOLS_LIST_DEFAULT_TTL_MS`). `invalidate_tools_cache()` forces an immediate refresh — useful after deploying a new tool to an MCP server without restarting the agent. 6 new tests in `tests/test_daily_2026_07_24.py`. Files: `agent/mcp_client.py`, `tests/test_daily_2026_07_24.py`.

- **Model cost table: GPT-5.6 family, Claude Sonnet 5, and o3** (2026-07-24). `packages/ai/cost_tracker.py` now includes the July 2026 model releases: `gpt-5.6-sol` ($5/$30 per M tokens — complex reasoning/coding, o3 successor), `gpt-5.6-terra` ($1.5/$7.5 — balanced/lower cost), `gpt-5.6-luna` ($0.5/$2.0 — fast/high-volume); `claude-sonnet-5` / `claude-sonnet-5-20260501` / `claude-fable-5` ($3/$15 — GA July 2026, 1M context, adaptive thinking); `o3` ($10/$40 — deprecating late August 2026, migrate to `gpt-5.6-sol`). The `GET /api/metrics/cost-attribution` endpoint surfaces these automatically. No behaviour change; cost attribution only fires on successful calls. Files: `packages/ai/cost_tracker.py`, `tests/test_daily_2026_07_24.py`.

### Fixed

- **A failing brain chain reported "none configured" while the doctor showed providers usable** (2026-07-26). An operator hit `/api/doctor/public` during an outage and got `brain_providers: warn — 4/14 brain providers usable`, while every dispatched task died with `All brain providers exhausted — none configured or all in cooldown`. Both were accurate: the doctor described the registry, and the terminal error was built **only from the recorded failures**, ignoring what had actually been attempted. A chain that ran four healthy providers and got `410 Gone` for every model produced a message identical to a deployment with no API keys — sending the operator to look for missing configuration while the real cause was decommissioned model ids. Three fixes:
  - **`410 Gone` now records an error.** The handler `continue`d to the next model without touching `last_error`, so a provider whose models had *all* been decommissioned returned an empty failure string, contributed nothing to `failures`, and vanished from the report entirely.
  - **The terminal message can no longer deny that the chain ran.** `BrainFailoverExhausted` now uses `tried`: when providers were attempted but none reported a reason, it names them and points at the model ids ("a decommissioned model returns 410 for every request without a usable error") instead of claiming nothing was configured. The genuinely-empty case keeps its original wording, so the three outcomes — nothing configured, attempted-but-silent, and attempted-with-errors — are now three distinguishable messages rather than two.
  - **The paid reserve no longer starves the free tier for an unreachable provider.** `_untried_paid()` counted any provider with `tier == "paid"`, including ones switched off by the kill switch or sitting in cooldown. `next_provider()` filters those out, so with a disabled paid provider (exactly the reported state — `anthropic` among ten switched-off providers) the reserve held back the free tier's last `BRAIN_PAID_RESERVE_ATTEMPTS` attempts for a provider that could never be selected, cutting the usable budget from 6 to 4. It now counts only providers that are actually selectable, restoring the documented intent that the reserve stays inert unless paid capacity is reachable.
  - 9 new tests in `tests/test_failover_silent_exhaustion.py`, including one asserting the three outcomes produce three different strings.
- **The dispatcher gave up without making a single HTTP request, and the log said nothing useful about why** (2026-07-26). `brain_failover: all 0 provider(s) exhausted (none) — no provider attempted` was the single most common error in production, ~30 occurrences in a seven-minute window, and it is a **regression from the kill switch added in #1123**. A provider switched off by the kill switch is not in cooldown, so its circuit breaker is CLOSED and it reads as `is_healthy == True`. `_recover_all_unhealthy()` bailed out on `any(p.is_healthy)` evaluated over **all** providers, so a single switched-off provider was enough to mask the all-unhealthy condition and skip the inline breaker reset. Meanwhile `next_provider()` correctly excluded the disabled ones and returned `None` because every *enabled* provider was in 429 cooldown. Net result: total deadlock — zero attempts, an immediate `BrainFailoverExhausted`, and every task ending `blocked after 5 failed dispatch attempts`. Reproduced against the production shape (two providers auto-disabled by 401/402, two in 429 cooldown): before the fix, 0 HTTP attempts and the exact production log line; after it, the chain resets the enabled breakers and succeeds on the first attempt. `_recover_all_unhealthy` now considers only enabled providers, and a test asserts recovery never revives a switched-off one — reviving it would silently undo the operator's decision, which is the entire point of a manual-re-enable switch.
  - **The zero-attempt message now names which of the three causes it is.** "No provider attempted" has exactly three explanations that need completely different responses — nothing configured (set a key), everything switched off by the kill switch (fix the named cause and re-enable), everything in cooldown (wait) — and the old message distinguished none of them. It now reports `N configured | N usable | N switched OFF: zhipu (Update the key, then switch it back on.); deepseek (Top up the account.) | N in cooldown: groq, nvidia`, so the log line alone says what to do, and flags `state not durable` when the switches will reset on the next deploy. The counts are **formatted from** `brain_failover.brain_availability_summary()` (added in #1129) rather than recomputed — that helper is already the shared answer for the public doctor endpoint, the CEO supervisor and the Providers screen, and a second implementation here would be the one thing worse than no diagnostic: two that can disagree. The same pass removed a duplicated `disabled_providers()` try/except from `_untried_paid`, so the disabled set is now read through one helper instead of two copies. Diagnostics are exception-proof: a broken registry degrades to `registry unavailable` rather than turning a failed call into a crash.
  - 7 new tests in `tests/test_failover_client_shared.py`.


- **CEO micro-management: review-round corrections** (2026-07-25). Fixes found by review on the micro-management PR, each a real defect rather than a style note:
  - **The tier step budget never reached the runtime.** `InternalAgentAdapter` caps `AgentRunner` via `spec.context["max_steps"]`, but `_execute_once` never populated `context`, so every sub-task ran at the global `AGENT_MAX_STEPS` default and the per-tier ceiling — a headline bound of the feature — was decorative. Now passed through, and pinned by a test asserting the value on the dispatched spec.
  - **Escalation discarded the caller's step ceiling.** The initial plan clamped with `min(max_steps or profile.max_steps, profile.max_steps)`, but the retry path assigned `profile.max_steps` outright, so a caller who asked for a tighter budget got the full tier envelope from the second attempt on. `SpecialistTask.caller_max_steps` now carries it and the retry re-clamps identically.
  - **The detached re-drive task had no strong reference.** `asyncio` holds only a weak reference to a running task and `add_done_callback` does not keep it alive, so a supervisor re-drive could be garbage collected mid-flight. Tasks are now held in `CEOSupervisor._tasks` until completion.
  - **SQLite `open_goals()` paged by the wrong column.** It ordered by `updated_at` (stamped on *every* write) and re-sorted the truncated page by `last_progress_at` in Python — which only reorders rows the `LIMIT` already chose, so with more open goals than the limit the most-stalled goal could be truncated away and never re-driven. `last_progress_at` is now a real indexed column, ordered on before the limit, and the redundant post-sort is gone. Existing databases migrate in place via `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN`.
  - **An unparseable `MONGO_SELECTION_TIMEOUT_MS` crashed module import.** The bare import-time `int()` raised `ValueError` while importing `services.ceo_ledger`, taking down the whole CEO subsystem instead of degrading. Now read lazily and clamped, like every other env knob in the subsystem.
  - **Two soft quality penalties compounded into a hard rejection.** A terse summary (-0.3) plus a missing test (-0.3) hit 0.4, under the old 0.5 accept threshold — escalating a correct-but-brief change through the entire tier ladder, even though each signal is specified and tested as "a warning, not a hard reject". Acceptance is now categorical: only the hard signals (execution failed, no summary, an opening refusal, claimed-success-with-no-file-changes) reject, and the score is kept purely as an observability signal.
  - **`?state=` filtered after the limit.** `GET /api/ceo/goals?state=abandoned&limit=25` could return nothing while abandoned goals sat just outside the newest-25 window. The filter now over-fetches and slices after filtering.
  - **A supervisor re-drive lost its execution context.** It replayed only the request, so a goal originally run against a specific worktree fell back to the process working directory — the wrong repository for a company-scoped goal. `workspace_root` and `user_id` are now persisted on the goal and replayed. **Credentials are deliberately not stored**: a durable ledger is the wrong place for a token, so a re-drive runs with the server's own repo access, and a goal that only succeeded via a caller's elevated token now fails its push step rather than silently acting with different permissions.
  - Blocking `CEOLedger` calls (a deliberately synchronous store) moved off the event loop with `run_in_threadpool`; `_require_admin` delegates to `backend.company_api._is_admin` rather than duplicating the role rule; the re-drive route uses a new public `CEOSupervisor.request_redrive()` that keeps the budget check and the in-flight claim together, so no caller can bypass either; `services/ceo_quality.py` split out of `services/ceo_micromanager.py` at the plan-versus-judge seam so both are inside the 800-line limit (re-exported, so no import site changed).
  - **The module split introduced a circular import.** `services/ceo_quality.py` imports from `services/ceo_micromanager.py`, and the latter re-exported the judging half back at the bottom of the file — so importing the quality module *first* raised `ImportError` on a partially initialised module. Invisible to the test suite, which happened to import the micromanager first. The re-export is gone; callers import from the module that owns the name, and a subprocess regression test asserts `import services.ceo_quality` works standalone.
  - **Ledger writes ran on the FastAPI event loop.** `_open_ledger_goal` and `_close_ledger_goal` are called from the async `delegate()` path and perform synchronous SQLite/Mongo I/O; in Mongo mode a slow update or a server-selection stall would freeze the whole API loop before any agent started. Both now go through a `_offload` helper (`run_in_threadpool`, with a direct call when there is no web stack).
  - **Two sweeping processes could both re-drive the same goal.** `RUN_BACKGROUND_IN_WEB` defaults to true, so a deployment that also runs the dedicated worker has two supervisors on one shared ledger. The claim was only the process-local `_driving` set after a plain upsert, so both could classify the same stalled goal, both spawn a re-drive, and race the intervention counter — duplicating edits and doubling token spend on precisely the goals already in trouble. `CEOLedger.claim_intervention()` is now a compare-and-set (conditional `update_one` on Mongo, `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction on SQLite); the loser reports the goal as in-flight and starts nothing.
  - **The ledger no longer writes to a shared on-disk database under `TESTING`.** `CEODispatcher.delegate()` now records every goal, so any test touching that path wrote to one `.data/agency.db` shared across the whole session — and with the developer's real database. `_sqlite_path()` returns `:memory:` under `TESTING` unless `AGENCY_SQLITE_DB_PATH` is set explicitly, so tests that genuinely exercise durability still do and everything else is isolated.
  - 10 new tests (94 total for this feature).

- **Every medium- and high-complexity task failed before a single specialist ran** (2026-07-25). `WorkflowOrchestrator._handle_execute` calls `ceo.delegate(goal=…, request=…, max_steps=…)`, but `CEODispatcher.delegate` accepted neither `goal` nor `max_steps` and defined no `**kwargs`, so the call raised `TypeError` — and the orchestrator re-raises unexpected exceptions rather than falling back to `AgentRunner`. The result: the entire EXECUTE phase died for exactly the tasks the CEO exists to handle, which is why complex work "struggled to complete". The existing test for that path (`test_handle_execute_routes_medium_complexity_to_ceo`) patched the dispatcher with an `AsyncMock`, which accepts any keyword argument, so CI never saw it. `delegate()` now accepts both parameters and honours `max_steps` as a ceiling on the per-tier step budget. The regression test in `tests/test_ceo_supervision.py` binds the **real** signature instead of a mock, so a future divergence fails at the call contract rather than in production.

- **CEO-executed work always reported an empty changed-file list** (2026-07-25). `CEODispatcher._run_via_runtime_manager` hardcoded `"changed_files": []` with the comment that runtime results do not carry per-file diffs — but `internal_agent` populates `metadata["changed_files"]` and `artifacts`, and `e2b` populates `artifacts`. The orchestrator's `ExecutionResult.changed_files` was therefore always empty for CEO-executed work regardless of what actually changed on disk, and downstream consumers (cross-verification's risky-module check among them) saw nothing to inspect. `_harvest_changed_files()` now reads `metadata["changed_files"]` first, then string- or dict-shaped `artifacts`, and reports separately whether the runtime was *able* to answer.
- **The configured remote skill repos were never fetched, and Doctor said they would be** (2026-07-25). Surfaced by a Doctor screenshot warning *"44 local skills loaded; 7 remote skill repos configured but none fetched yet"*, with the explanation *"They will appear after the first refresh"*. There was no first refresh. `SkillRegistry.refresh_remote()` had exactly four callers — `GET /api/skills/discover`, `POST /api/skills/refresh`, `proxy.py`, and `services/onboarding.py` (company onboarding only) — and **none of them run at startup**, so a fresh deploy left every configured GitHub registry unfetched until a human happened to open the skills page. The warning could therefore never clear on its own, and the explanation actively misled the operator into waiting for something that was not coming. This is the same defect shape as the dead `RUN_HERMES_IN_PROCESS` flag fixed in the same release: working code with no caller. `services/background.py` now schedules one refresh at boot, **fire-and-forget** — it is one HTTP round trip per registry and boot must not wait on GitHub — gated by `SKILL_REGISTRY_REFRESH_ON_BOOT` (default on), cancelled on shutdown so an in-flight fetch cannot keep the event loop alive, and fully non-fatal: `refresh_remote` is TTL-guarded and gathers with `return_exceptions=True`, so a rate limit or a GitHub outage costs the remote skills for that cycle and never the boot. Local `.claude/skills` are unaffected either way. 8 new tests in `tests/test_skill_registry_boot_refresh.py`, including one that asserts `start_background_services` **actually invokes** the helper — a helper nothing calls is the same bug wearing a different name, which is precisely how this and the Hermes flag went unnoticed.


- **Hermes could never run on a single-service deployment, and would not have been worth running if it had** (2026-07-25). Two problems, and the second is the one that mattered.
  - **`RUN_HERMES_IN_PROCESS` was dead configuration.** The flag and `settings.is_hermes_in_process` (`packages/config/settings.py:203`) have existed since it was introduced, and this file documents "Hermes in-process :8100" as production behaviour — but **nothing consumed the flag**. Grepping for the property returned its own definition and no callers. The only way to run Hermes was `Dockerfile.hermes` as the separate `agency-hermes` Render service, so on a one-service deployment `runtimes/adapters/hermes.py` probed its default `http://localhost:8100`, found nothing listening, and reported the runtime permanently offline — meaning `RUNTIME_CODE_GENERATION=hermes` silently fell back to `internal_agent` forever, with nothing in the logs, because falling back is by design. `services/background.py` now starts `services.hermes_server:app` inside the backend process, before `runtime_manager.start()` so the first health probe does not race the bind. It binds **127.0.0.1 only, never 0.0.0.0**: `POST /tasks` executes arbitrary agent work and its bearer check is optional (`HERMES_API_KEY` may be unset), so a public bind would be a remote-execution hole. Startup failure degrades to the existing fallback rather than breaking boot, and shutdown uses uvicorn's graceful path so port 8100 is released — cancelling the task outright leaves the socket bound until the process dies, turning the next boot's bind into "address already in use".
  - **Hermes was `internal_agent` with extra latency.** `services/hermes_server.py` executes every task through `InternalAgentAdapter`, on the same brain, the same providers and the same failover chain — so simply switching it on would have changed nothing except adding an HTTP hop, and routing `code_generation` to it would have bought exactly zero. It now resolves the coding-specialist model when the caller expresses no preference, via the existing `resolve_coding_model_preference()` (reused, not reimplemented). That resolver already documents itself as serving "the agency's coding loop **+ Hermes**", so this implements intent that was written down but never wired. It returns `None` unless the coding brain is enabled and the active provider can serve it, so a deployment that has not opted in is unchanged, and a resolver failure never fails the task — a model preference is an optimisation, not a precondition.
  - Verified by execution, not inspection: the server starts, `GET /health` returns `{"runtime":"hermes","ours":true}`, and the real `HermesAdapter.health_check()` reports `available=True` against it. 9 new tests in `tests/test_hermes_in_process.py` bind a real socket on an OS-assigned free port — the failure being prevented is "nothing is listening", which a mock cannot observe — covering the health probe, the adapter's verdict, idempotent double-start (web lifespan and `worker_main` can both call it in one process), the off switch, degradation when the bind fails, port release on shutdown, and the three model-preference paths.


- **The attempt budget starved the paid tier, so paid capacity bought for exactly this situation could never be used** (2026-07-25). Regression introduced by the fan-out cap in the same release. `BRAIN_MAX_PROVIDER_ATTEMPTS` (default 6) caps total attempts across the whole chain, and `brain_failover.next_provider` orders providers free → local → paid. An operator with ten free keys configured therefore spent all six attempts inside the free tier and the chain raised `BrainFailoverExhausted` **having never contacted the paid provider at all**. Measured, not inferred: replaying the real registry shape (10 free providers + Aerolink admitted as paid, every free provider returning `429` — their observed production state) the chain stopped at the sixth free provider, and `aerolink` appeared in neither the request log nor the `tried` set. The failure mode is self-defeating: paid capacity exists precisely to cover the case where the free tiers are rate-limited, which is the only case that can exhaust the budget. `packages/ai/failover_client.py` now reserves `BRAIN_PAID_RESERVE_ATTEMPTS` (default 2) attempts of the existing budget that only paid-tier providers may spend — taken **out of** the cap, never added to it, so the fan-out fix is not weakened. The reserve is inert unless a paid provider is both admitted and untried, so a free-only deployment keeps its whole budget and sees no change; it is skipped rather than stranded when every paid provider is in cooldown; and `allow_paid` (enforced in `_build_registry`, which simply omits paid providers) remains the sole spend gate — this changes *reachability*, never *authority*. Only the attempt count is reserved, never wall-clock, since holding back seconds would let the deadline expire with the reserve unspent. Set `BRAIN_PAID_RESERVE_ATTEMPTS=0` to restore the flat cap. The value is read by a dedicated bounded parser rather than the module's `_env_int`, which floors at `1` and so could not express "off" (`0` silently became `1`, making the documented escape hatch a no-op) and did not cap against the total (a reserve at or above `BRAIN_MAX_PROVIDER_ATTEMPTS` left the free tier a single attempt, quietly turning a cost-control knob into a paid-first switch). The range is `0` through `BRAIN_MAX_PROVIDER_ATTEMPTS - 1`, clamped rather than rejected so a fat-fingered value degrades predictably instead of failing a deploy at import. 8 new tests in `tests/test_failover_client_shared.py`: the paired proof that the reserve is what makes the difference (paid reached at reserve 2, not reached at reserve 0), a guard that total attempts still never exceed the cap, a parametrised bounds check that reads through the **environment** rather than monkeypatching the constant — the earlier version bypassed the parsing and so could not have caught the `0` bug — and a sweep asserting no reserve value can exclude the free tier before a single attempt.

- **The operator could not read why a provider had been switched off** (2026-07-25). The Providers screen showed the raw stored reason in a column away from the switch, so deciding whether to re-enable meant reading `auto: 400 out of credit/quota` in one place and finding the toggle in another. `services/brain_failover.describe_disabled_reason()` now renders a stored reason into a status code, a plain-language summary, and the remedy — mapped in the module that owns the disabled state rather than in the UI, so the wording cannot drift from the strings `failover_client._auto_disable` actually writes. `GET /api/brain/providers` returns `disabled_code`, `disabled_summary` and `disabled_action` alongside the untouched `disabled_reason`, and `ProviderHealthToggleCard` renders the code and summary **in the same table cell as the toggle**, with the raw reason on hover. An unrecognised reason is passed through as its own summary rather than dropped — a reason the operator cannot read still beats none. `auto_disabled` is now derived from the same renderer instead of a separate `startswith("auto:")` check at the call site. 9 new tests in `tests/test_provider_state_durability.py` — one per real reason shape, asserted against the literal strings `_auto_disable` writes, plus a seam guard that scans those call sites and fails if any reason has no mapped remedy — 4 in `tests/test_brain_providers_api.py`, and 4 in `frontend/src/__tests__/providerHealthToggleCard.test.jsx` including one that fails if the reason drifts out of the toggle's cell.

- **Operator provider state was wiped by every deploy, so switched-off providers came back and the paid-provider toggle reverted** (2026-07-25). Both settings that gate which providers the failover layer may use — the per-provider kill switch and `allow_paid` — were read from a local SQLite `kv_store` file. Render's disk is ephemeral (the repo's own `render.yaml` warns about this), so each deploy silently re-enabled every provider an operator had switched off, and reverted `allow_paid` to false even though the UI toggle had written it to Mongo, quietly dropping the paid tier out of `_build_registry`. `services/brain_failover.py` now reads and writes both settings through Mongo when Mongo is the configured backend, with the SQLite file kept as a warm local mirror and the dev/CI backend. The kill switch dual-writes (Mongo for durability, SQLite so a Mongo outage still leaves the running process with the operator's intent); `allow_paid` reads the same `providers` document `_set_provider_policy` already writes, falling back to the mirrored kv key and then to `false` — a storage fault can never enable paid spend. Both readers sit on the **sync** hot path inside the event loop, so this uses pymongo rather than motor and is bounded by three guards: the existing short read caches (5 s kill switch / 10 s paid policy), a `MONGO_SELECTION_TIMEOUT_MS` server-selection timeout (default 2 s), and a 60 s backoff opened by any Mongo error, during which SQLite serves alone — so an unreachable Mongo costs one bounded stall per minute, not one per dispatch attempt. The Mongo path is off unless `STORAGE_BACKEND=mongo` **and** `MONGO_URL` is set explicitly (the storage layer's `mongodb://localhost:27017` default is a placeholder; treating it as configured would add a connection timeout to dispatch on every dev machine without a local Mongo) and is disabled under `TESTING` so the suite can never mutate a shared operational store. `GET /api/brain/providers` gained `state_durable`, and `ProviderHealthToggleCard` shows a warning when it is false — otherwise an operator on an ephemeral disk watches their switches reset with no way to learn why. **Behaviour change to note:** on a Mongo deployment whose stored policy has `allow_paid: true`, paid providers now stay in the chain across deploys instead of dropping out — that is the toggle working as intended, but it has a cost consequence, so verify the stored value before deploying. 24 new tests in `tests/test_provider_state_durability.py` (the gate's five conditions, Mongo-wins-over-stale-mirror, restart survival, both-stores-failing degrades without raising, and a real-Mongo round trip that runs in CI and skips where no server is reachable) plus 9 in `tests/test_brain_providers_api.py` closing the missing endpoint coverage for the switch (persistence, unknown-provider rejection, auth, and an assertion that no API key reaches the browser) and 8 in `frontend/src/__tests__/providerHealthToggleCard.test.jsx`.

- **The failover chain self-inflicted the 429s it was routing around** (2026-07-25). Measured against the real provider registry, a single failing task could issue **up to ~840 HTTP requests**: 14 providers x 3 models = 42 per completion, x up to 4 `_chat_json` parse retries = 168 per planning step, x `_DISPATCH_RETRY_LIMIT` (5) task re-queues = 840 — while the agency runs many tasks and 34 loops concurrently. No free tier survives that, so every provider returned `429`, every task ended `BLOCKED`, and the logs read as "all 14 providers are broken" when the caller was generating the load. This is why an agency with valid keys across a dozen providers still never completed a task. `packages/ai/failover_client.py` now enforces a shared budget for one logical completion — `BRAIN_MAX_PROVIDER_ATTEMPTS` (default 6) caps total HTTP attempts across **all** providers and models rather than per provider, and `BRAIN_FAILOVER_BUDGET_SEC` (default 180s) bounds wall-clock, so worst-case cost is linear in the budget instead of the product above. Both are env-tunable because breadth-versus-quota is an operator decision: raise them with paid capacity, lower them to protect a tight free tier. Failover itself is unchanged — a healthy provider still answers in one attempt, and recovery across several failing providers still works within budget; only the unbounded tail is removed. 4 new tests, including one asserting the cap holds at 6 calls where the same chain previously made 30.

- **Billing refusals burned three model attempts each, and the exhaustion log under-reported which providers were tried** (2026-07-25). From a production chain where all 14 providers failed. Two defects in the failover dispatcher:
  - Providers disagree on the status code for "your account is out of money": DeepSeek returns `402 Insufficient Balance`, Anthropic returns `400` with `"Your credit balance is too low"`. Neither was recognised as provider-level, so each burned the remaining two model slots repeating a refusal that could never succeed. Both now fail over to the next provider after one attempt, matching how `401`/`403` are already handled. A genuine bad-request `400` (e.g. an unsupported parameter) still advances to the next model on the same provider — the distinction is made on the response body via `_is_billing_refusal()`, not the status code alone.
  - The terminal log reported `tried=['cerebras', 'deepseek', ...]` — 9 providers — beside 14 distinct failures, with `ollama` listed twice. Cause: `tried` is the *exclusion* set and `_recover_all_unhealthy()` clears it so providers can be retried after a circuit-breaker reset, which makes it wrong for reporting. The message now uses a separate append-only record and dedupes the failure list, so the count and the names agree.
  - 4 new tests in `tests/test_failover_client_shared.py`.

- **CI's `Test (Python 3.13)` job was red on `master` from an ordering-dependent motor event-loop failure** (2026-07-25). `tests/conftest.py` calls `reset_store()` before each `TestClient` enters its lifespan specifically to stop this class of flake — its docstring names `test_auth_me_regression` as the test it was written for. A motor `AsyncIOMotorClient` binds to whichever event loop was current when it was constructed, and the function-scoped `client` fixture gives every test a fresh TestClient, portal and event loop; any client cached from an earlier test is therefore bound to a closed loop and raises `RuntimeError: Event loop is closed` on next use. But `reset_store()` cleared only the storage-layer client in `packages/storage/mongo.py`. `services/company_graph_store.py` builds its **own** `AsyncIOMotorClient` on a module-level `_graph_store` singleton, which survived every reset and stayed bound to the first test's loop for the whole session. That is exactly the observed signature: the test passes in isolation (13/13) and fails only after several hundred others have run, and CI uses `pytest -x`, so the whole job stopped there. `reset_store()` now drops the `_graph_store` singleton too. New `tests/test_reset_store_clears_motor_singletons.py` guards the invariant structurally, including an AST scan that fails the build if `AsyncIOMotorClient` is ever constructed in a module `reset_store()` does not know about — which is how this bug arose in the first place.

- **`packages/ai/rate_limiter.py` proactive pacing was silently dead on `master` due to a sequential-PR merge collision** (2026-07-23). PR #1108 (header-driven `RateLimitTracker`/`pre_flight_check()`) and PR #1111 (operator-configured `TokenBucket`/`pace()`) both added a full implementation of `packages/ai/rate_limiter.py` at the same path with completely different content; when #1111 merged on top of #1108, its version fully replaced the file, silently deleting `TokenBucket`, `pace()`, `_configured_rpm()`, and `reset()`. `packages/ai/router.py` still called `from packages.ai.rate_limiter import pace as _pace_provider` on every provider attempt — wrapped in a `try/except Exception: pass` (by design, so pacing bugs can never block a request), which meant the resulting `ImportError` was swallowed silently on every single request with zero log signal. Net effect: any operator who set `<PROVIDER_ID>_MAX_RPM` to enable proactive rate-limit pacing got no pacing at all, with nothing indicating the feature wasn't running. Reconciled `packages/ai/rate_limiter.py` to carry both layers side by side (header-driven tracker + operator-configured token bucket, using the GCRA-style slot-reservation `TokenBucket` implementation that fixes a separate concurrency bug where concurrent callers on a drained bucket used to all wake and proceed together instead of being staggered one interval apart); restored the corresponding token-bucket test coverage into `tests/test_rate_limiter.py` alongside the header-tracker tests. 34 tests total, all passing. Files: `packages/ai/rate_limiter.py`, `tests/test_rate_limiter.py`.

- **Fallback that recovered was still reported as an error, and Hermes could never wake up** (2026-07-25). Two related reliability problems, both surfaced from a live session.
  - **Severity semantics.** A provider failing while the chain still had untried providers was logged at `ERROR`, so a *successful* failover left errors in the log. That is not cosmetic: `agent/log_monitor.py` attaches an `ERROR`-level handler that auto-files GitHub issues, and its operational-skip list covers `429`/`502`/`503`/`504` but **not** `401` — so every recovered auth failure could open a ticket for a provider the system had already routed around. Intermediate failures in `packages/ai/failover_client.py` are now `WARNING`, a recovered call logs a single `INFO` naming what it recovered from, and exactly one `ERROR` is emitted — only when the whole chain is exhausted and the caller genuinely cannot proceed. Same rule applied to `runtimes/health.py` (a failed health probe is a warning; the circuit breaker retries and the router falls back) and `services/company_agency.py` (runtime-assignment failure logged `ERROR` on the line before it successfully fell back to `internal_agent`).
  - **The terminal error now names every provider that failed, not just the last.** Reporting only the last error made a whole-chain outage look like one broken key: `401 Unauthorized for open.bigmodel.cn` pointed at Zhipu while four other providers were failing for their own reasons. `BrainFailoverExhausted` now carries every attempt with its own cause (`zhipu 401 unauthorized/forbidden; minimax 429 rate-limited; ollama network error: …`), so the log line alone is enough to act on.
  - **Hermes cold starts.** `runtimes/adapters/hermes.py` is a SIDECAR adapter — it speaks HTTP to an already-running server and never starts one — and `agency-hermes` is its own Render free-tier service, which sleeps after ~15 min idle and takes 30-60s to wake. The health probe used a hardcoded 5-second timeout, which could never observe a cold-starting service, so Hermes read as permanently DOWN and every task that should have routed to it (`AGENT_TASK_TYPE_ROUTING` sends `code_generation` there) silently fell back to `internal_agent`. On the free plan that was the steady state, not an edge case: only agent traffic would wake the service, and that traffic never arrived because the adapter reported it dead. The probe timeout is now `HERMES_HEALTH_TIMEOUT_SEC` (default 25s — deliberately inside `RuntimeHealthService`'s own 30s `asyncio.wait_for` budget, or the outer timeout would fire first and the value would be meaningless), and the existing keepalive workflow now warms the sidecar alongside the backend. The ping is non-fatal by design: an operator who never deployed `agency-hermes` must not get a red keepalive job every 3 minutes, since Hermes being down only costs a fallback. 11 new tests in `tests/test_hermes_warm_and_severity.py`, plus 3 in `tests/test_failover_client_shared.py` asserting a recovered failover logs zero errors and an exhausted chain logs exactly one.

- **The CEO (and every other `call_llm` caller) could not reach the brain-failover provider chain** (2026-07-24). The multi-provider dispatch loop lived inline inside `AgentRunner._chat_text`, so only the agent loop could fail over across the env-configured chain (nvidia, groq, cerebras, zai, zhipu, deepseek, together, dashscope, moonshot, mistral, aerolink, openrouter, minimax, google, anthropic, ollama). Everything routed through `backend/server.py`'s `call_llm` — the agency CEO's strategic assessment above all — fell back only across the DB-configured `providers` records, a strictly narrower set. When those were rate-limited the CEO caught `ProviderFallbackError` and degraded straight to its rule-based `_ceo_assess_rules()` path while a dozen healthy providers sat untried, so the agency stopped making LLM-driven decisions without ever reporting a provider problem. The loop is now extracted verbatim into `packages/ai/failover_client.py` (`failover_chat_completion()` + `BrainFailoverExhausted`), which `AgentRunner._chat_text` delegates to and which `call_llm` falls through to when the DB provider records are exhausted — one implementation, two callers, per the no-duplicate-logic rule. Escalation policy is unchanged: paid providers are admitted to the chain only by `brain_failover._build_registry`, which still gates them behind `ALLOW_PAID_BRAIN` or the Providers UI toggle, so this widens reach without widening spend authority. 14 new tests in `tests/test_failover_client_shared.py`, including guards that neither caller may re-inline the loop and that the dispatcher stays within the 50-line function limit. Review hardening: a 2xx response with an unusable body (empty `choices`, non-JSON, null content) is now treated as a failed attempt and fails over, instead of raising `IndexError`/`JSONDecodeError` out of the dispatcher and defeating its guarantee to either return a complete result or raise `BrainFailoverExhausted`; the 503 paths log via `log.exception()` and chain from the failover error.

- **Agent tasks blocked by a guaranteed-400 Anthropic call, a 404'ing Gemini base URL, e2b/Anthropic SDKs missing from the production image, a doctor that couldn't see popup-OAuth GitHub tokens, and an unreadable dead-host probe error** (2026-07-24). Six independent production faults — five reported from a live session, one found alongside them — each traced to a specific line rather than inferred:
  - `agent/loop.py` + `packages/ai/router.py`: the brain-failover dispatch loop built **every** provider request as OpenAI-compatible — `POST {base}/v1/chat/completions` with `Authorization: Bearer`. `services/brain_failover` offers `anthropic` as an ordinary failover candidate whenever a paid brain is allowed (env `ALLOW_PAID_BRAIN` or the Providers UI toggle), and Anthropic's native API serves neither that route nor that auth scheme, so selecting it produced a deterministic `400 Bad Request for url 'https://api.anthropic.com/v1/chat/completions'` on every model — burning the whole dispatch budget and surfacing as `Task ... blocked after 5 failed dispatch attempts`. Anthropic-shaped providers now POST `/v1/messages` with `x-api-key` + `anthropic-version`, reusing the router's existing `_anthropic_payload` / `_anthropic_to_openai_response` translators so there is one implementation of the Anthropic wire format. New shared predicate `packages.ai.router.is_anthropic_base_url()` replaces the ad-hoc `endswith("anthropic.com")` host check in `agent/loop.py` (which also matched look-alike hosts such as `notanthropic.com`); OpenAI-compatible Claude gateways (Aerolink, OpenRouter) are deliberately not matched and keep the `/chat/completions` path.
  - `services/brain_failover.py`: the `google` provider's `default_base_url` was the bare host `https://generativelanguage.googleapis.com`, which resolves to `/v1/chat/completions` — a route Gemini does not serve. Corrected to `https://generativelanguage.googleapis.com/v1beta/openai`, matching `config/models.yaml` and `packages/ai/brain_config.PROVIDER_DEFAULT_BASE_URL`, which both already used the `/v1beta/openai` form. A new parity test asserts the failover registry's base URLs cannot drift from the catalog again.
  - `backend/requirements.txt`: `Dockerfile.backend` (the image Render builds) installs **only** this file, never the root `requirements.txt` that CI installs — so `anthropic`, `e2b-code-interpreter`, `PyYAML` and `psutil`, listed only in the root file, were absent from every production image. This is why the Providers screen reported `e2b OFFLINE — e2b-code-interpreter SDK not installed` regardless of `E2B_API_KEY`, and why `agent/loop.py`'s native Anthropic path silently fell through to the OpenAI-compatible call above (its `import anthropic` raised into a swallowing `except`). The same drift had already caused three earlier outages (scanner deps, langfuse); `tests/test_backend_requirements_cover_runtime_imports.py` now fails the build when a package imported by shipped code is missing from the production install set.
  - `backend/server.py`: a GitHub token reaches the backend by two routes writing to two different places — `users.github_repo_token` (the `PUT /api/github/token` endpoint and the `/api/auth/github/repo` redirect) and `github_settings.token` (the popup OAuth callback) — and only the PUT wrote to both. Both doctor endpoints read only the user document, so every account that connected via the popup was reported as `missing_github_token` even though repo listing, cloning and PR creation all worked (those call sites already had a `github_settings` fallback). Fixed at the root: the popup OAuth callback now mirrors to the user document exactly as the PUT does, and a new `_resolve_user_github_token()` helper checks both locations, degrading to `None` rather than a 500 if the lookup fails. Every remaining direct reader of `users.github_repo_token` (agent dispatch, onboarding status, workflow execution) now goes through that resolver, so the token is read from one place and — per the secrets rule — is deliberately NOT copied into a second collection.
  - `services/brain_liveness.py`: a provider whose hostname no longer resolves surfaced as the bare OS error `Probe error: [Errno -2] Name or service not known`, which the Brain card truncates to `✗ Probe error: [Errno -2] Name` — naming neither the failing host nor the knob that fixes it, and reading like a key or model problem when it is neither. Confirmed for Aerolink: `capi.aerolink.lat` returns NXDOMAIN while the registrable domain `aerolink.lat` still resolves, so the shipped endpoint is dead rather than misconfigured. Name-resolution failures (including `socket.gaierror` wrapped inside `httpx.ConnectError`) are now reported with the host that failed and the `<PROVIDER>_BASE_URL` env var that overrides it. The shipped Aerolink URL is left unchanged — its correct replacement is not known, and `AEROLINK_BASE_URL` already overrides it.
  - `backend/server.py`: the Google provider still advertised `gemma-4` in the user-facing model picker (`PREDEFINED_MODELS`) and assigned it to all three agent roles (`AGENT_ROLE_MODELS`), even though the same file already documents — at the `GEMINI_MODEL` definition, from the 2026-07-22 fix — that Gemma is a separate open-weight family Google does not serve through the generative-language endpoint `GOOGLE_BASE_URL` targets, so it 404s unconditionally. That earlier fix corrected only the *seeded provider's* `default_model`; the catalog the UI reads and the role assignment were left pointing at the dead model, so anyone selecting Google in the picker got a provider that could never answer. The picker now offers `gemini-2.5-flash`, `gemini-2.5-pro` and `gemini-2.0-flash`, and all three roles resolve to the configured `GEMINI_MODEL` default. Because `GEMINI_MODEL` is operator-overridable while the catalog is static, the configured model is registered into the catalog at import time — otherwise an override outside the listed set would be assigned to all three roles while being unselectable in the UI. A new test asserts no role can be assigned a model the picker does not list. Also corrected the shadowed `google` entry in `services/brain_liveness.py`'s fallback map, which still held the bare host and would have gone live if `google` were ever dropped from the catalog.
  - 48 new tests across `tests/test_anthropic_failover_endpoint.py`, `tests/test_backend_requirements_cover_runtime_imports.py`, `tests/test_brain_liveness_dns_reason.py`, `tests/test_doctor_github_token_lookup.py`, and `tests/test_google_provider_models.py`.

- **Knowledge Base Graph tab 404'd permanently on a stale `localStorage` company ID** (2026-07-21). `CompanyGraphPanel` (`frontend/src/v5/screens/KnowledgeScreen.jsx`) trusted the persisted `COMPANY_ID_KEY` outright and called `GET /api/company/{id}/graph` with it unconditionally, so a company deleted or a DB reset since that ID was last saved produced a permanent "Couldn't load the company graph: Company <id> not found" with no recovery — reported directly from a user session immediately after the tab shipped in #1105. `CompanyScreen.jsx` already solved this exact class of bug for its own company selector (PR #962: validate the stored ID against the live `listCompanies()` result on mount, and self-heal on a 404 by clearing the stale ID and falling back to the first available company) — `CompanyGraphPanel` just hadn't been given the same treatment. Ported the identical fix: validate `storedId` against the fetched company list before trusting it, and on a 404 from `getCompanyGraph`, clear `COMPANY_ID_KEY`, re-list companies, and auto-select the first *other* company found (not blindly `list[0]`, which a CodeRabbit review on the PR caught could itself be the just-404'd ID) instead of leaving the tab stuck. Also fixed (Codex review): if the re-list itself fails while recovering from a 404 (network/500/auth expiry), the panel now calls `setError(...)` instead of silently clearing the graph with no explanation. Also fixed (Codex review): the mount-time company-list validation effect and the graph-fetch effect ran concurrently whenever a stale ID was stored, so a slow 404-recovery for the old ID could overwrite a selection the validation effect had already corrected -- added a per-invocation `cancelled` flag (standard React stale-closure guard) so a superseded 404 handler no longer mutates state. 5 new regression tests in `frontend/src/__tests__/knowledgeGraphTab.test.js`. Files: `frontend/src/v5/screens/KnowledgeScreen.jsx`, `frontend/src/__tests__/knowledgeGraphTab.test.js`.

- **Live provider-fallback reliability: wrong Gemini seed model, wasted retries on 413/401/403, and an unbounded baseline-pytest timeout crashing the Quick Note automation** (2026-07-22). Investigated recurring production errors surfaced directly from the live activity log rather than guessed at:
  - `backend/server.py`: the `google-gemini` provider's seeded `default_model` was hardcoded to `"gemma-4"` — not a real Gemini API model (Gemma is a separate open-weight family Google does not serve via this endpoint), so every call through this provider 404'd unconditionally, recurring every cycle. Fixed to use the same `GEMINI_MODEL` env var (default `gemini-2.5-flash`) already used by `packages/ai/router.py`'s own Gemini provider construction. `seed_default_providers()` already syncs `default_model` for existing DB rows when it drifts from the in-code value on every boot, so this self-heals already-broken deployments without a separate migration.
  - `agent/loop.py`: the brain-failover HTTP dispatch loop retried a different *model* on the same provider when a request failed with `413` (Payload Too Large) or `401`/`403` (Unauthorized/Forbidden) — but a payload-size or auth failure fails identically regardless of which model on that provider is asked, so this burned every retry attempt on guaranteed-repeat failures before ever trying a different provider. Confirmed in production: a task blocked after 5 dispatch attempts, all identical Groq 413s or Mistral 401s.
